And &eñ. t&A'ii said to hermit : "She Is tnar per- ticulai:" 'However, whe sent into the drawing-room some bit 'cakes of her own making, and when she removed the things found them untouched. "My!" she tMd. I hope 'nits ain't ill. No, miss would have been better off if she had been ill; something

worse had happened to her—a look back into the past. Somehow the Mavnards were always talking of her—Frank to ran down, the elders to palliate. It was Lily who first noticed that on these occasions Colonel Albert always listened, but never joined in. It was to Mrs. Maynard that he said one day, when, after a discussion about their neighbour, the chil- dren had gone out: "I suppose she is very lonely." "I can't helo thinking. Colonel, that she has had some disappointment. What age should you take her for?" "Oh. she isn't more than thirty-five," said he, decidedly—"l mean—one has to allow—she dresses not like most ladies of her age. And thirty-five," he went on musingly, "that is really nothing—some women are quite young--" "If they are fairly happy," said Mrs. Maynard.

"She isn't happy?" aia he quickly. "I think only lonely women get into what the children call those frumpy ways, and lonely people are never very happy. I don't like Frank speaking of her as he does, because she is really very good'. I visit a little myself, and all the poor people are fond of her." "I should imagine that." "I didn't know you had noticed her enough to judge." "Well—l—you see the children talk of her so much, and of course it's rather hard for a woman." "What is? oh, living alone." - "Exactly," said Colonel Albert. "I didn't quite take your meaning," said Mrs. Maynard, and then the subject dropped. The Colonel, when he went up to his room that night, stood at his window looking out for a long time, not on to the Maynards' garden, but towards Miss Chichester's. which wasn't as large nor as pretty. No doubt after his Indian exile one old-fashioned English garden was as charming as another. Miss Chichester's fidgets seemed to have subsided when one evening Colonel Albert, the children being out, volunteered to carry a message for Mrs. Maynmd to one of the cottages. Mrs. Williams received him respectfully. "I won't come in," he said, "I see you have a visitor." For in the gathering dusk be could see a lady sitting by the fire. "Oh, never mind, sir," said Mrs. Williams, "it's only Miss Chichester—she often conies and sits with me." The Colonel had already crossed the thresholl„ and he said, mechanically: "011, indeed, very kind," and bowed to the lady sitting on the hearth, in her straight-cut dark gown and bonnet close to her head, and her hands, gown, her thread gloves, folded in her lap. Mrs. Williams was so busy dust- ing a chair for the Colonel that she never noticed the warm colour that came up into Miss Chichester's face as she bent her head in response to the Colonel's salutation.

"The evenings de close in now, sir," said the good woman; "it's dark in here, ain't it?" which gave Colonel Albert time to recover himself, and to give his message, and make some ordinary remarks, and listen without impatience to Mrs. Williams's volubility. But all the time he was thinking sadly and bitterly, "How she is changed! What a fool I have been!" till Miss Chichester, who had said scarcely anything, rose. •

"I think I must be goinlz, Mrs. Williams," she raid; "it is getting very dark." The Colonel started up. "You must let me see you home, "he said, and agAin the flush came into her face, fainter this time.

"Yes, miss." said Mrs. Williams, "you don't like being out in the dusk alone, and, of course, it ain't fit for laaies." Miss Chichester shook hands with the woman, and went out, without a word to Colonel Albert or even a look at him, as he stood at the door waiting. But he followed her out into the lane, and Mrs. Williams, as she looked after them, said to herself: ",There. I wouldn't have thought she'd let him go along of her. Many's the time she's said to me that she don't think it proper for ladies to be walking about with gentlemen at dark. But there, she ain't that young." Outside, in the soft deepening dusk, Colonel Albert walked by. the old maid's side, and said not a word, as if ho had been born dumb. Then, sud- denly. he drew her hand on to his arm, and, perhaps because he felt it tremble, there was that sudden rush of emotion in his heart that forced his lips to open. "Maggie," be said, tenderly.

Then they both stood still, and she looked up at him—young again, transformed, as if time had reversed itself' and been as kind to her as the summer breeze that had played with the hair on her forehead and twisted it into ripples. It was fifteen pears ago—there were the arching trees, and the summer scents, and the warm breeze; and he was holding her hands and telling her he love.' her, and she was blushing, and trembling, and lifting sweet, soft eyes. She wasn't staid and prim and eccentric; he hadn't shut out all love and hardened himself, and suffered thirst and heat and cold and wounds, and thonght of shadowy lanes and cool dusk. Yes, that had been the dream—the creeping years and the bitter changes; this was the reality. He was smoothing the brown hair, just streaked with silver here and there—it hada% been in that old time, fifteen long years ago—and her tears were gathering and falling; she hadn't shed tears much for many a year. Somehow they had seemed dried up, and what need has a lonely old maid to cry? She might be crying all day for the matter of that, if only for the pain always in her heart. They found words at last; they came from him first—a sad "Maggie, how you have suffered! It was all nip; fault"—and from her a quick: "On, no—mine, too—l was proud—unforgiving." And neither asked the other: "Do you love me still?" Did they not know it ? "We are not so old—either of us," Colonel Albert said; "there are years before us yet, though I have lost fifteen years.' "You have lost them!"

"Could you come to me and say, Forgive ' P That was my place. But the years drifted on. and I tried to forget—to think you had never cared— that you had forgotten. Somehow I never could forget—the old memories came surging up when I thought they had been effaced, So, Maggie, if you can forgive my wrong, my silence—if you can trust me enough to give me the chance to retrieve what I have done—to make the years before you happier than those that have gone--" -

"Do you know what you are saying?" she asked. "Can we close up the gap of years like that? we are friends again—but look at me. I am thirty-five by age—how old by narrowing myself down to my own grief, and growing dull and eccentric? You, a man, have had other interests—l, a woman, atot forced to work, have had leisure to be hedged round with little habits and fads that made up my life. I might be happy—but you!" "You will grow young again when you are happy •—you will always be young to me," he said, earnestly. "Do you know how little-minded and selfish a man can get who lives to old age without any ties? We misunderstood each other miserably —were foolish and proud; don't let us ruin all our lives as we have ruined our youth." Then he passed his arm round the slim waist of this old maid—who did not seem shocked by the impropriety. She looked almost pretty with her down-dropped eyes and colour coming and going A smile broke over her face at last—"l think you'll be sorry," she said. The children came in before the Colonel did that evening; but lie appeared shortly afterwards, and entered as usual into the family chaff and fun. He told it all very simply when he and his old friend and Mrs. Maynard were alone. He said he and Maggie had had a quarrel—lie did not go further i into t than that—and lie had gone out to India. It had been his fault; lie would lay no blame on her, though of course they knew it takes two to make a quarrel. Stil:, they could see that if each had been wrong it must be the man who makes the mode, and Colonel Albert hadn't done that. . The Maynards called on Miss Chichester the next day, and they got very friendly with her, finding that beneath all the excrescences of old maidisth she was gentle and unselfish. Lily and the Colonel between them coaxed her into fashionable gowns and curled hair. Nobody . knew, however, how charming she could look till they saw her in her madding-gown, and-her eyes shining so softly under the rings of hair on her forehead. Lily was the only bridesmaid, and was almost as happy as this bride. . come and see us, Frank?" said Maggie, just before they were to start,,for the Highlands; "I owe you reparation, you know. You shall run wild if you like. Lily and Frank went together to the lovely placo Colonel Albert had bought. Ho didn't leave the Service, of course, but xlienever he could was a-. • this country house. 7• It's delightful," Lily reporteq to her mother, when she got hack; "you wouldn't know her for the same person ; she's a bit precise some things, but she seems to have got all thos,! angles rubbed off. I don't believe she likes a chair. cover awry, but she let Frank romp about as 11.4 liked." "And he ?" said M ijor May narl. • "Oh, lie langli: at her. He's nicer than ever—% fairly hugged him w got tte.re. I always like peoph! who hare had a lot of trouble, they are F.ll gwacit. avid 5... ilist bow Maggie is."

Some Successful Women Writers in Society.— Amongst the latest to join the ranks of literary peer- esses is the Marchioness of Tullibardine who is engaged upon a history of that famous regiment, the Black Watch. The Duchess of Sutherland who has already made some well-known additions to Mudie's list of current fiction, is shortly bringing out a new novel. Both she and the Duchess of Leeds have a real literary taste which appeals to the reading public on its merits. So also has Lady Wawrick, who however has not yet at- tempted fiction, nor has Lady Juene, though it is said that a novel will shortly appear from her pen. No list of successful women writers in Society would be com- plete which does not include Mrs. Cornwallis West, Lady Colin Campbell, and Mrs. Alfred Lytleton. who is writing a novel, and also a new play in collaboration with Lady Betty Balfour. Among the writers of a less ambitious kind might be mentioned the Countess of Jersey, Lady Cromartie, and Lady Pembroke, one of the sisters of the Duchess of Leeds. Why not try a No-tip League ?—The great success which has attended the opening of a " no-tip " restaurant in London, has induced some sanguine people to believe in the possibility of abolishing the "tipping " system, as it exists in hotels, restaurants, on the railways, and among servants generally. It vrould be an ideal reform if it could be effected, for of late years tips to servants have grown tol such an extent as to become a serious item when visiting friends or venturing to stay away from home. Only a very few strong-minded people—women mostly—dare to stand out against it, and it is to be feared that tipping has become so much the recognised thing that it will never be put down to any appreciable extent. Vet it is an entirely underhand proceeding, the product of a desire on the part of those with the longer purse, to get the better of those less well-to-do. One way to fight against such a system would be the formation of a " No-tip League." but it would have to be very influentially supported before people would care to venture into hotels or res- taurants, wearing a badge which, to the waiters, maids, boots, and others, would be very much like a red rag to a bull.

Canine Parties.—Pet animals were never more fathion- . able than now, and there are no extremes to which some ; Society women will not go in pampering their bejewelled favourite. Tn America—the land of fads and fanatics—we read of fashionable dog parties taking place. The dogs are invited by costly cards bearing their names, and those of their owners. When they arrive on the appointed day, they. are conducted to the banquet and their masters and mistresses etand behind , and wait upon their pets. Whether the dogs enjoy this ceremonious style of banquet or not, their owners seem to think it delightfully funny, and it seems likely that canine parties will become all the rage in the select &clay circles of New York. A Prevalent Frand.—The letter of the Secretary of the Drapers' Chamber of Trade, draws attention to a class of fraud which has long been prevalent,and has no doubt brought a satisfactory harvest to the people who practice it. 'I he mode us operandi is to distribute hand- bills,which it is stated will be called for,containing a cata- logue af goods at ridiculously low prices. Shortly after- wards a traveller calls, and receives orders for some of these bargains, stating that he will not take the money, but payment can be made when the goods are delivered. The unsuspecting housewife, thinking to receive some i goods at a sixth or so of their value, s then persuaded to purchase other material which • the man produces. This she pays for, only to find that inferior goods have been palmed off upon her at exhcrbitant prices, and that the "bargains "never arrive. A Dangerous Fashion.—Several cases of blood- poisoning have occurred lately through the wearing of bright red stockings, which, together with red belts, underskirts,and headgear, are having a great vogue just now. The wearers of highly coloured hosiery always run a considerable risk, as should the colour permeate the skin, through a scratch, or cut, blood-poisoning is the probable result. Therefore it would appear more sensible to leave this mode severely alone, and content ourselves with the black hosiery that is warranted fast, dye, and simply sewn with scarlet. Stoles and Muffs.—The newest stoles will afford ample warmth, coming as they do to a point behind which extends almost to the waist, forming a cape on the shoulders and completely covering the chest, from whence they narrow off and fall well below the knees, finishing in a cluster of " In greyand lock squirrel, also caracul—which is the least costly of all the skins that can rank as "real fur"—these new "stoles" are within the poslibility of the moderately stocked purse, and are to be preferred to the more or less successful imitations of the costly furs. Muffs are i very large, the "pillow" shape, which s long, deep, and flat, being first in favour. Severity in Tailor-made Costurnes.—Leadi ng Viennese and Parisian tailors are building their bodices after the style of the habit coat. These they, are heavily braid- ing, and finishing with sharp points in front. Heavily- braided and much-gored skirts, clearing the ground, accompany these new bodices, and no other toilette could possibly be more becoming to a woman possessed _ of a good figure.

Fortnightly Meeting.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

The autumn modes are no longer in an in definite stage but are now firmly established, and for the next four or five months we will see an evolution and elaboration of the fashions which new prevail. These are merely a modification 14 all that best pleased the leaders of the world of dress during the summer months. There is still everywhere a decided leaning towards picturesqueness of attire both for indoor, and street wear, and while coats and skirts are rather more simple and cut more strictly on the lines of the tailor-made," proper evening and afternoon gowns are distinguished by an increase of elaboration and a profuseness of trimming. In regard to the selection of a dress or coat and skirt costume for street wear I would cer- tainly advise that those whose means are not sufficient to allow of a dress of this kind made to order should instead select as smart. as

possible ready-made garments definitely deciding the amount they can afford to lay out before starting on a shopping expedition. There are some who prefer to buy the re- quisite amount of cloth, lining, buttons, etc., themselves and have the dress made up at home or by a dressmaker. This, however, is rarely successful, for there is a cut and a finish which is necessary for the success of an out- door costume which is somehow only achieved by a skilled cutter and tailor whose business it has always been to turn out a certain descrip- tion of work. _ _ _

The cosy coat sketched would not look at all smart if it were riot fashioned by an ex- perienced hand. It is a delightful wrap for a cold afternoon or evening as the box cloth of which it is made is heavy and warm and is rendered all the more so by a lining of tar! an- patterned satin. The collar, revers, and deep. cuffs are of fur, and the coat is of the fashion- able three-quarter double-breasted sac shape. Made in fawn, with sable brown collar, etc., it looks very smart, while a putty tone with black fur trimmings and a gaily hued lining looks modish. Face cloth could be used if a less weighty coat is preferred. Box cloth, however, wears splendidly though it is rather more expensive. One can often use up an old half worn fur cape or coat for making the collar and revers on a new coat or cloak, leav- ing out any very worn or rubbed portions. In this way a garment can be made to look twice as valuable and an old and unfashionable fur made to do duty in another form.

This year there are many imitation fur coats which are exceedingly pret They are made

out of plush treated in various ways chid the imitation of the real skin is in many instances simply marvellous, while the price is equally moderate. These are very useful for wear with different skirts.

The lovely picture hat shown in my second sketch is made of a large soft flappy black felt of a satiny surface curved and wired into shape and trimmed with 'handsome curling ostrich feathers, while black chiffon strings are tied in a smart little bow at the left side. A black velvet bow caught with a buckle in the centre covers the bandeau which gives the hat an upward tilt at the front. The crown. which is broad and flat, is finished byl a narrow black velvet band, but it is in the shaping of the hat that the secret of its success lien. A hat like this would do a wholtwinter's wear for best and is sufficiently dre.s;.for restaur- ant or theatre wear, although for the latter purpose a small hat or toque is more sensible and comfortable both for one's self and those who sit behind. ,Managers of most theatres prefer, of course, .that hats should be left in the cloak rooms, but it is not always necessary to do so, neither is it always advisable as in the crush of leaving one's clothes and hata get crushed. Besides it is always a difficulty almost insurmountable to get within reason- able distance of a looking-glass and one has to trust to chance in the arrangement of a hat or toque at,,the right angle. Still, the picture hat is often to be seen. in such circumstances and looks very charming when expressed all in black and worn with a white or pale coloured gown. Another style of hat is illustrated in my last sketch. The style is much simpler and there- fore more suited for morning wear. It is made of a furry surfaced felt or beaver in a soft shade of fawn and is trimmed by a twist of petunia coloured velvet , and a couple of magpie tinted birds at one side. The effect is very stylish. A black hat of the same style trimmed in a similar fashion and in the same colours would go very tastefully with a black cloth costume. A great deal of ribbon is being used on this season's hats, and there are not perhaps so many whole birds as formerly, but choux of (ribbon or large close bunches of flowers take their .place in many instances. For fiats, 'however, such as I have sketched,l there is nothing so smart looking as a bird or! some wings. Satin and velvet ribbons are' arranged in quillings and ruchings round the low, flat, widec rowns of some of the most fashionable hats, while others again have smartly arranged bows of fanciful patterned ribbons through which one or two quills are thrust.

(Comiright. A REGULAR OLD MAID.

By MARIE MAITLAND.

"Odious old fidget! I don't wonder she never got married."

"Why, Frank, how spiteful!" said Lily, looking up from her drawing. "What has poor Miss Chichester done now?"

"Poor, indeed!" said Frank, at the unchivalrous age of fourteen; "she's a regular old fidget! She's al ways worrying about something. First, I chase her cat; then my rabbits get over the hedge and nibble her rotten old cabbages; then she complained because Tom and I, played cricket on the laurel path, and the balls might—only might—break her greenhouse windows—an old nuisance :ike that ought to live in a desert."

"She isn't old," said Lily, indignantly. "She is—she looks forty." "That isn't old, and she doesn't look forty. Boys are such duffers. She wears her hair plainly, and she looks pale and worn, and you can't see the difference. The hair is awfully pretty—all ripply, if she would only not wear it so primly; and I think her eyes are sweet." "Oh, what bosh! she's a beastly old worry."

"For shame, Frank!" "What's the matter?" said Mrs. Maynard, open- ing the dining-room door. "Lily, you look—well, angry—and Frank—" "Frank will go on about poor Miss Chichester, mother."

"Now, mother," cried the boy, running up to the universal authority, "isn't she forty?" "Forty? Dear me, no—you've got to take five years off her for her hair and dress," said the mother, highly amused. "You don't mean to say you've been discussing a lady's age? I thought you wanted to be like Colonel Albert ?"

"Oh, well," said Frank, disconcerted. For he had always made a model of his father's brother officer, whom he had heard of so much, but never seen, and in his own boyish mind Colonel Albert was a hero and an idol, equal to the doughty knights and magnanimous pirates of his tale-books. And he knew that the last thing such a chivalrous personage as Colonel Albert (so the children had always called him) would do would be to run a tilt against a woman, however uninteresting. His mother had compassion on the boy's discom- fiture, and said: "I came to tell you about that very Colonel of ours. He's coming to stay with us. His regiment arrived from India last week, and your father telegraphed to him to come. Fifteen years away! It's a long time. Frank, you must go to the station with your father to meet the Colonel." The Colonel, who had become famous in the May- nard household, arrived in time for dinner. He was tall and bronzed, and a soldier all over, and got such a warm welcome that he seemed at a loss what to say. He made great friends with the children, Frank being full of admiration for the

man who had a real sword, and had killed people with it.

"You'll come and see my rabbits to-morrow?" said the boy, when he was going to bed. ‘I shall be delighted," said Colonel Albert, and ha was dragged off next morning after breakfast and shewn the patches of garden belonging to the 3hildren,where nothing would grow, and the rabbits that everybody attended to save Frank: "You've got a nice place here, Maynard," said Colonel Albert, who was known in the service as Colonel Esselmont. "Jolly big compound—l men garden." "Time you settled down in some such place," said the Major, wlio had sauntered out after his friend. "Are you not tired of India?" "No " the Colonel looked away over the trees while he answered—"and what should I do in a place like this?" "Find a wife," said Maynard, laughing.

"What, at my age?" "My dear fellow, what nonsense! You've only just turned forty, and upon my word you've worn better than I'd have expected in that climate. You haven't as many grey hairs as I have," looking critically at the other's dark hair, which certainly 3hewed very little change of colour for a man who had led a hard life in a trying climate. "But then you've had an easy life—plenty of money, no 'brats Like these to plague your life out."

Colonel Albert here laid his hand almost uncon- 3ciously on Tom's fair head, that young gentleman Lifting big, round, wondering eyes to the soldier's face. Children feel so quickly—perhaps Master Tom had a vague idea of something beyond a caress in that touch. .

"No," said the Colonel, "I've had only myself to think of—if that keeps a man young. Well, Frank, are those all the rabbits? "

"There's the black one," said Tom; "it's trying to get into Fidget's garden—l'll get it." "Fidget!" said Colonel Albert, whereat Frank poured out his woes, and the Colonel laughed. Lily exclaimed: "Hush, there she is!

She was walking in her garden—this ogre of Frank's—and yet she. wasn't much like an ogre. She had probably never been exactly pretty, but she had rather fine grey eyes and wavy hair, which she spoilt by folding away in prim braids. She was not tall, but she had a slight figure, clothed in a gown not quite in the fashion, and falling too stiffly. A huge brown hat, of the snrt called "basin," was Dn her head; and whether she were forty or, as Mrs. Maynard averred, five years younger, she had acquired the indefinable old maid air. They all looked towards her; Frank muttered "Old cat!" and the Colonel turned away—of course he had only glanced at her; but he went up the garden as if he had forgotten all about the black rabbit he was to have seen. Lily was going after him. when Miss Chichester's voice over the hedge arrested her.

"Miss Maynard!" "Good morning, Miss Chichester," said Lily. "I think your rabbit is in my garden," said Miss Chichester, severely; "if your little brother will come round properly to the gate he shall have him." "Oh, thank you," said Lily, smothering a laugh. tell Tom," and she ran off, coming sharp upon Colonel Albert, who hadn't got as far on his way as he should have done.

"Did you hear her?" said she; "isn't Frank half right ? " And then she noticed that he was looking ;tern, with knitted brows, and she said quickly: -Oh, I bvg your pardon," thinking she had vexed ni m.

"What for, my child?" he said, smiling. "Well, go and send Tom round to the gate; I daresay she is a little precise," with a good deal of hesitation. When Lily had left him he sat down on a bench ri_‘ar, and remained very still, as people do who hold themselves together with an effort. Presently he became aware of little feet pattering along the path, and up came Tom with his hands full of flowers. She gave them to me," he said, and Colonel Albert tifted the little fellow on his knee. "Who gave chein to you?" he said, in that tender way he had with children.

Tom couldn't say "Chichester"; he nodded his nead towards her garden, and said 'She." Then the Colonel took the lovely bunch, all fresh and dewy, in his hand—holding the child close meanwhile. "I mustn't beg for one, must I?" be said, "since they were given to you." - -

"011, yes," said Tom, who was pleased at the tribute to his. flowers; "I'll 'find you one," and being a generous child he picked two of the loveliest roses in the bunch. The wondering look came again into, his eyes when Colonel Albert kissed him.

Miss Chichester's maid soon informed her mis- tress that there was a grand soldier staying next door; she had heard he was someone very important in India. Probably she thought he had conquered an army in his own person. _

"I think I saw him in their garden," said Ali3S Chichester, "a tall man, not very young." "Yes, miss, out he's a handsome gentleman," said the domestic. "Shall I bring in tea,_ miss ?"

Miss Chichester glanced at the clock on the mantelpiece, a clock that never committed the impropriety of being wrong. "It wants five minutes to five," said she; "at five you can bring the tea. [like punctuality. Susan.,,

WARRINGTON CHAMBER OF COMMERCE. The Stag Bridge Lay-By.

The principal item under discussion at a meeting of the Council of the Warrington Chamber of Commerce which was held on Monday, was the utilizing of the lay-by with the Manchester Ship Canal, at Walton, for the purposes of local traffic. The opinion was generally expressed that by utilizing the lay-by it might be advantageous for Warrington manufacturers to have their goods for abroad shipped at Walton.—lt was decided to write to the Ship Canal Company for further information, and that if the facilities offered by the company were of such a character as to commend them- selves to the Council, a special general meeting of the Chamber should be called in order to lay the matter before them.

PROPOSED LECTURE. The SECRETARY (Alderman Bennett) reported that he hoped to arrange for an early lecture by Mr. Thomas H. Barker, the secretary of the Liverpool Chamber, on the Trans-Siberian railway. THE ASSOCIATED CHAMBERS. Alderman BENNEW reported that he attended the autumnal meetings of the Associated Chambers in Man- chester, accompanied by the Vice-President (Mr. J. Fulton Smith) Mr. John Fairclough and Mr. T. E. Gibson. Resolutions were carried approving of the recent Anglo-French agreement ; supporting an arbitra- tion treaty with the United States ; in favour of the development of our present system of technical and commercial education; of the nationalization of canals; of the adoption of the recommendations of the joint committee of the Lords and Commons on municipal trading; of the further reform of the patent laws; of providing untaxed alcohol for manufacturing purposes; for the repeal of the sugar tax, of the abolition of light dues; of the concession of one vote to every limited company • of improving the position, status and influ- ence of 'Chambers of Commerce and of many other im- portant commercial topics. The delegates were sump- tuously entertained by the Manchester Chamber, and among the excursions organised for their enjoyment was a visit to the works of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, in which, through the kindness of the firm, they were accompanied by a large number of the mem- bers of the NVirrington Chamber. The meeting was well attended and was tinder the chairmanship of Mr. C. E. Parker.

A LIVERPOOL POLISHER'S PERIL ----- Zam-Buk Cures Poisoned and Ulcerated Arms.

Very few of those housewives who admire the beauti- ful work of the French polisher think of the physical suffering which has sometimes to be endured so as to give such a finish to the furniture. The chemicals used have frequently a serious effect upon the man's hands and arms, and in a recent Liverpool case, blood poisoning and ulceration was the result. The victim was Mr. Joseph H. Jones, of 2' Raglan-terrace. Alma-street, Everton, Liverpool, and he has given an account of his sufferings, and of his cure by using Zam-Buk, to a Liverpool reporter: "About seven or eight years ago," said Mr. Jones. "my fingers got covered with blisters in consequence of a strong polish Iliad been using. These blisters became very irritable,and in a short time developed into ulcers, which caused me to seek medical aid. For several years I was taking medicine, but I never seemed to properly recover. Shortly after Christmas the affection spread to my arms and legs, and I was quite unable to work. I had to lay up. My fingers were swollen, stiff, and were all out of shape, whilst my arms had to be bandaged twice a day, and I was helpless as a child. My wife had to wash, dress, and feed me, and I settled down as a confirmed invalid. "About this time my wife had been using Zam-Buk successfully for her chapped hands, and she prevailed on me to give it a trial. I did so, with a result that can only be described as magical. With absolute truth, may say that from the first my hands and arms com- menced to improve, and at the end of a fortnight I was completely cured. The swellings had gone,the stiffness vanished and the sore places were all healed up. I at tri- bute this entirely to the soothing, antiseptic and cura- tive powers of Zam-Buk,and when I think of the nights of sleeplessness I have suffered, of the agony I endured, my cure seems incredible." Zam-Buk is invaluable for poisoned wounds, festering sores, ulcers, eczema, piles, psoriasis, boils, pimples, sores, rash, bad legs, cuts, bruises, burns, scalds, cb.afings, itch, cramp, stiff joints, raw chins, black-heads, scalp ; irritation, sore heads, aching feet, weak ankles, sprains, stiffness, swollen knees, inflamed patches, and sunburn. EVENTS OF THE WEEK.

DUKE OF CONNAIIGIFT IN A MOTOR SMASH.

The Duke of Connaught has had a narrow •escape from death in a. motor car accident which happened whilst His Royal Highness was travelling from Edinburgh to Gosford House, in East Lothian. It appears His Royal Highness had been making an inspec- tion of the garrisons in Scotland in his capacity of Inspector-General of the Forces, and he was on his way to join the Duchess and the Princesses of Connaught at Gosford House, East Lothian, the seat of the Earl of Wemyss, when the accident happened. He was attended by Major Murray, his aide-de-camp, and accompanied by Captain McLaren, aide-de-camp to General Sir Charles Tucker, Commander of the Forces in Scot-

'TEE DUKE OF CONNAUGHT.

land. Including the chauffeur (Mr. Hendry, manager of the Scottish Automobile Club), there were four occupants of the car, which was followed by another containing members of His Royal Highness's suite. The road is a broad one with a fine , surface, and ad- mirably adapted for motoring. All went well with the party till they had reached a point between Piershill Cavalry Barracks and the suburban seaside resort of Portobello. Here a large country cart suddenly loomed up in the darkness. The Duke's motor had just swung past one of the tram cars which run between Edinburgh and Portobello, and the chauffeur had no time to pull up. The motor was going at a moderate speed at the time. The cart had no lights and furthermore was loaded with wood, which projected over the One side of the car dashed into the cart and was torn away. It Ivas the side on which the Duke of Connaught was sitting, and His Royal Highness was thrown into the road, where he was picked up bleeding and insensible. Fortunately, the second car soon made its .appearance and the Duke was taken back to Edinburgh with all possible speed. During the short ride his Royal Highness rallied somewhat, and was able to walk from the car across the broad pavement into the hotel. - Here he was attended to by Professor Annandale and Sir John Chiene, and is now progressing satisfactorily.

===== 0002601_19041022_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WAR IN THE :FAR EAST.

After the usual period of waiting and recuperation, to which the onlookers of the West have become quite accustomed, another battle has been fought in the Far East, and it has been the most important of the war. At the beginning of the .week the news began to filter through that General Kuro- patkin had suddenly made a complete change in his plans and had assumed the offensive, acting presumably under orders from St. Petersburg with a view to relieving Port Arthur. Later on this unexpected advance to the attack was confirmed, and the Treatest battle of the war took place round "Yentai, resulting in one gigantic Russian ,defeat An along the line. The Tsar's order had been to fight to the last man, which snakes the moral effect of the defeat all the -worse. Indeed, it was a crushing blow to Russia's prestige and her military strength. Kuropatkin's plan appears to have been to turn the Japanese right and overwhdlin Kuroki's army while he held Oyama, No.;lzu, .and Oku in check, but the result has been ,the reverse, as the Russian left has been lutflanked by -Kuroki. It was a masterly

MARSHAL OYAMA. GENERAL OKLI GENERAL KUROKI

stroke of strategy on Oyama's part, and places the whole of she Russian army in a very unpleasant and somewhat ludicrous predicament. The Japanese left and central armies in their turn worked their way across the Shaho River and pressed the enemy towards the Hunho River while they were trying to retreat to Mu&den by the Fushan road. The losses have undoubtedly been enormous, though not so much so; it would appear, on the Japanese side. The Russians are reported to have had 80,000 men killed and wounded, and it is certain that, taking both sides together, over too,ooo men have been put out of action: In addition, the Russians have lost a hundred guns in their retreat. This engagement promises to rank as the biggest battle of modern times, and it is hoped in diplomatic circles that it may have more far-reaching effects than appear on the surface. Mean- while, the ,fight round Port Arthur goes On with great keenness on both sides, and constant bombardment is reported, but the fall of the fortress appears no nearer.

===== 0002601_19041022_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
DEATH OF THE KING; OF SAXONY.

The death of the King of Saxony followed hard upon his appointment of his son to the regency, as it was only last week that he handed over the reins of government owing to ill health. On Saturday morning he died of heart failure at Pilluitz Castle, near Dresden, where he was born seventy-three years ago. It would be idle to deny that he was popular amongst his own people, as he was far too autocratic: and their estimate of him is summed 'up in their nickname of the "Economical King." Re- cently, when a new Court Theatre was required at Dresden to preserve the traditions of three hundred yea's, the late King was unwilling to bear the expense of it and, as a compromise, arranged to borrow the money from Herr Lingner, the millionaire dentifrice manufacturer, at nominal interest. The Government, however, intervened and can- celled the undignified loan. The late King George's chief claim to fame rests on his exploits as a cavalry leader in the war between Austria and Prussia and the Franco-

THE LATE KING. HIS SUCCESSOR. German war. The Crown Prince, now King Frederick Augustus 111., is on the other hand, universally popular; and great sympathy has been expressed for him in his late matrimonial troubles. Two years ago, it will be remembered, the Crown Princess Louise eloped with M. Giron, causing a public scan- dal throughout Europe. At the kite King's command the Crown•'Prince divorced her in Dresden; but it is commonly reported that he is still in love with her and is anxious to re-marry her, blaming her surroundings at the tiaxon Court rather than herself. That, how- ever, is now hardly probable. The new King is thirty-nine years of age and was married thirteen years ago:

===== 0002601_19041022_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
SIR JOHN COCKBURN.

Death always keeps politics simmering, and it is seldom that the country is not in- terested in some bye-election or other. At present there is more than one on the political tapis, but the one which is arousing the most interest is the West Monmouth vacancy. This is so for two reasons -,- one the importance attaching to the late representa- tive, Sir William Harcourt, and the other the interest sur- rounding the candidate with whom the Tariff Reformers are attacking this stronghold of Liberalism. Al- though new to politics in this country, Sir ,John Cockburn is an old hand .at the game, and has in his time been a Premier, though still comparatively a young man. In himself he is a striking personality .and a strong candidate of rather unor- thodox views so far as actual party ques- tions are concerned. His principal hobby is the question of Old Age -Pensions, which he has seen established around him in Australia and New Zealand, and his pet aversion in politics is the Deceased Wife's Sisters' Bill, which raises the question of the legitimacy of so many Colonials in the Mother Country, though acknowledged by the law of their own land. Like most Colonials, he is unfettered by the usual conventions of politics, and his debut under our British system is therefore being watched with con- siderable interest by both rivals and sup- porters. Born at CroSbie, near Duns in 'Scotland, fifty-four years ago Sir Johp Cock- burn was educated in London and took the degree of Doctor of Medicine at the London University, gaining the, gold medal of his year. But a roving instinct sent him abroad, and he soon entered the sphere of politics in South Australia. In the course of eighteen years he held all the principal offices in that colony, rising in due course to the Premiership. Then he returned home to represent South Australia in England as Agent for the Colony. Since he resigned the post three years ago his desire has been to enter politics in this country, and this week he takes the first step in the West Mon- mouth contest.

TEE CONSERVATIVE CANDI- DATE FOR WEST MONMOUTH.

RUMOURED RETIREMENT OF LORD MILNER.

Considerable excitement was occasioned in London last week by the announcement that Lord Milner was about to retire, and eventually the Colonial Office was drawn into an official denial. However, neither the rumour nor the denial threw much light upon the situation, and the publication of the re- tirement as an actual fact may be ascribed to journalistic keenness. It has for a long time been an open secret that Lord Miler will not stay in S longer than is

LORD MILER.

absolutely necessary. He is in himself not a particularly strong man physically, and the strain of the last five or six years would have been enough to break the health of the most robust man in the world. On the other hand, Lord Milne' will not quit his post until the time is ripe for him to leave and he feels that his work has been brought to such a success- ful point that he can hand it over with a dear conscience to his successor. He is too thorough and conscientious a man to wish to shirk anything even at the expense of his own health: and the public may be certain that nothing short of a breakdown will bring him home again before he has. arrived at the proper point of pacification in South Africa. It IS generally stated that he will remain until he has established self-government. but • it is more likely that the institu- tion of a new constitution will fall to the lot of his successor. Probably the return of Lord Milner will not be long delayed now: but that is all that can be said at present. THE NORTHERN UNION.

NORTHERN LEAGUE.

First Division.

OLDHA.NI v. WARRINGTON.

• The first meeting of the season between Oldham and Warrington took place at Watersheddings on Saturday. Warrington were short of Hallam and Harris, for whom Knight and Short played, while Oldham had to substi- tute Tyson for S. Lees at c‘ntre.The weather was bright and tracing, and under the circumstances it was rather surprising to find not more than 5,000 spectators assembled to welcome the .usually popular Warringtonians. Per- haps the severe defeat of the Oldham team at Salford the week before had discouraged their somewhat fickle supporters. Warrington were accompanied by about 200 enthusiasts per special train from the Central. Mr. J. Smith (Widnes) was the referee and the teams lined out as follows :

Warrington—Knight, full back ; Fish, Isherwood., Dickenson, and Short, threequarters ; Davies and Hocken- , hall, half-hacks; Preston, Boardman, Thomas, Belton, Shugars, Cook, Jolley, and Naylor, forwards. ; Oldham—R. L. Thomas, full back; Spottiswoode, Civil, Tyson and Cash, threequarters ; A. Lees and Lawton, halves; Vowles, Frater, Ferguson, Owens, Wilkinson. Kelly, Ellis, and D. Thomas, forwards. Oldham won the toss and Dickenson set the ball in motion against a strong breeze which blew from end to i end. The opening stages were uneventful and play settled at centre after good punting by R. L. Thomas. Here A. Lees made a mark and Ferguson took a shot at goal from the half-way line. Aided by the breeze the ball sailed high over the bar, and Oldham were two points up. Play livened up and Lawton broke cleverly away from the scrum only to be grassed by Thomas near the quarter. Warrington were soon under the ban of the referee and Ferguson took an easy penalty kick but was wide of the mark. Smart punting by D. Davies changed the venue, and then Oldham came back strongly Short only just securing the ball near the line after a race with Tyson. Dickenson broke up the passing of , the attackers and found touch neatly near centre. War- rington continued to have the worst of matters for a time but easily kept Oldham at bay. The forwards played strongly and gradually gained ground. Hocken- hall and Fish combined in a speedy dribble and R. L. Thomas was exceedingly lucky to stop the ball with his legs when the line appeared at Warrington's mercy. The visitors now shewed their quality in some fine combination, and Fish made a great effort, only Thomas's fine tackling saving a try a yard from the line. With high pressure still being main- tained Warrington were penalised—a fate that befel them repeatedly at crucial moments. Knight mulled the rival full-back's kick, and Oldham got to centre again. Lawton was conspicuous and gave Spottiswoode a good pass, the latter doubling inside and falling a victim to the ever-reliable Isherwood. Warrington were holding an adyantage forward, and frequently drove the home team into their own quarters. Oldham became very dangerous again through Lawton,Cash and Fergus- son who carried play right to the line. Civil next received and dashed for the line, only to be hurt by Isherwood in a vigorous tackle. He was dazed for some moments, but before Warrington could clear he received another pass and ran in splendidly near the posts,Fergu- son failing at goal. Half-time came a moment later with Oldham leading by 5 points to nil. On resuming, Civil was absent for ten minutes Ferguson going back, and Thomas coming into the centre. Warrington soon showed aggressive tactics,and beautiful passing ended in Fish being thrown in touch. Civil resumed with play at midfield, and Oldham at once got the upper hand. Spottiswoode evaded Fish and then raced past Knight to score at the corner, thus putting Warrington 8 points in arrear. A moment later he again got away, but this time Knight was safe and the right-winger landed in touch somewhat heavily. This was Oldham's last attack,for Warrington displayed fine form and carried play right to the line, Fish failing by a yard to get over. A tremendous assault on the home line followed, Hockenhall dodging in under the posts only to lose the ball on the verge of the line—very hard luck. In all departments Warrington were now showing superiority and Board- man got over in a rush, the try being disallowed amid groans from the Warrington supporters. Still the visitors pegged away though Boardman had retired with a damaged hand. Dickenson received from Hockenhall and threw wide to Fish, who romped over brilliantly, but failed at goal. There were still five minutes, and Hockenhall electrified the crowd with a beautiful effort which carried him over near the goal. Two points only required for a draw! Fish placed the ball carefully but .iaiissed a simple shot, and Oldham had won by 8 points to 6. They were a lucky side, and Warrington received general sympathy. Five minutes more would prob- ably have turned the scale.

BRADFORD v. HALIFAX.

At Park-avenue, Bradford, before 12,000 spectators. The start was delayed 15 minutes. The game was barely ten minutes old when Dunbavin scored an unimproved try. From a mark Little kicked a goal, and play con- tinued even to half-time. On resuming, Eagers scored a fine try, Mosby failing. Little kicked a goal from a penalty. The game became rough, and in the closing stages Bradford pressed, but could not score. Result : Bradford, 2 tries (6 points); Halifax, 2 goals (4 paints).

WIGAN v. SALFORD.

At Central Park, Wigan, before 14,000 spectators. Play opened in a sensational manner, Allan scoring from a long pass by Lomas. The goal kick failed. Wigan then attacked, but passed badly. Hector and Lomas made unsuccessful attempts et goal. Lytham eventually scored, Mason failing. Lomas kick ed a penalty goal. Parry added a try, the kick failing. R esult—Salford, 8 points: Wigan, 3 points. HULL KINGSTON ROVERS v. BATLEY.

LANCASHIRE COMBINATION.

WARRINGTON "A" v. OLDHAAI "A."

There was a good attendance at Wilderspool on Saturday to witness this match. As Knight had journeyed to Oldham to take Hallam's place in the first team, Berry appeared for the first time as full-back. Warrington had the best of play in the opening stages and Barnes experienced hard luck in being pulled up twice when a score seemed imminent. Oldham made a rush into the Warrington half, but Berry by a.good run relieved the pressure. The visitors, however, played in determined style and after fine passing Sellars scored at the corner. A good attempt was made at gaol but the ball went just outside. Soon afterwards a mistake by Berry allowed Sellars to again get over somewhat easily. The goal kick was abortive. Oldham were penalized under their own goal-post, and Barnes kicked an easy goal for Warrington. Half-time:—Oldham "A" 6 points, Warrington "A' 2 points. The second half was keenly contested, and Warring- ton assuming the aggressive, Watkins dribbled over and scored near the post from a cross kick by Berry. Barnes kicked a goal. Davies of Warrington, was sent off the field for some informality. Final : Warrington "A" 7 points, Oldham "A" 6 points. SALFORD (A) V. WIGAN (A).

At Salford. Wigan st arted, and at once Sharrocks kicked a penalty goal. The game fluctuated consider- ably, and ultimately Norris equalised. On call of the interval Sharrocks kicked a second goal. In the second half the game was roughly contested, several men being injured, Simm of Wigan, and Harker, of Salford,leaving the field. Ralphs and Harris scored tries for the visitors both of which were agumented by Sharrocks. Final: —Wigan (A), 4 goal 2 tries (14 points) : Salford (A), 1 goal (2 points). ROCHDALE HORNETS v. BROU GHTON RANGERS.

At Rochdale. The Rangers pressed throughout the first half. Bentley obtained two splendid tries and Shaw and Hampson also scored. Hampson dropped a capital goal and Worthington converted two tries. Half-time :--Rangers, 16 points ; Hornets, nil. The Rangers again pressed in the second period, but the only try registered was another hy Bentley. Final : Broughton Rangers,2 goals 5 tries,Rochdale Hornets nil. LEIGH (A) v. CEIADDERTON.

At Leigh, before a good attendance. In the first half each side attacked in turn, Neville and Lees having hard lines for. their respective sides. The interval arrived without any score. Resuming, Leigh pressed, anl Neville scored, Dunbavin improving. Lees then kicked a penalty goal. Afterwards Chadderton fell to pieces, and O'Brien, Smith, and Neville added tries, Dunbavin improving two. Final :—Leigh (A), 18 points; Chadderton, 2 points.

LIVER CHILL!

And What it Led to.

ANOTHER BILE BEAN TRIUMPH

A chill on the liver is a common ailment at this season, in our terribly trying climate. It may be brought on by getting one's feet wet, by going out insufficiently clad., by living in a damp house, or even by sleeping in a damp bed. However caused, no ailment could be more dangerous. Liver chill is to several most serious ailments what the fuse is to the dynamite charge —a starting point for very serious consequences. Miss Fanny Elizabeth Adlam, of New-street, Winter- bourne, Stoke, sustained a chill on the liver, and it brought on several serious complications. She says : "I lost all my colour, I became weak and listless and could take no interest in anything. I consulted a doctor, who having examined me, said I was suffering from a chill on the liver. He prescribed for me, bat his medicines did not do me a bit of good. "I then went to Salisbury Infirmary, where I was treated as an out-patient, but though I attended for nearly three months, I got no better and almost gave up hope of ever being well again. The pains I suffered in my head were dreadful, and to eat was torture because of indigestion. If I even stooped down I felt sick, and my head felt as if it would split open. For months I was like this, and at last had to take to my bed. The neighbours all said I was 'going home,' and I certainly looked like it. _ _

"I tried almost everything I heard 'of but all to no Purpose, till one day a friend of mine sent me a box of Charles Forde's Bile Beans. I began to take them, and soon perceived an improvement in my condition. My colour returned, and I began to enjoy my food—some- thing I hadnot done for two years. My breathing got better, and, in fact, I felt stronger altogether. I con- tinued with the Bile Beans, and they have completely cured me. lam 'fully convinced that I should not Ma alive now but for Bile Beans, and I shall never cease to recommend them to all with whom I come in contact. They saved my life." Bile Beans are acertain cure for indigestion, debility, biliousness, constipation, piles, nervousness, antemia, female ailments, weakness colds, chills, neuralgia, rheumatism, pains in the back, sleeplessness, loss of appetite, liver complaint, headache, flatulence, pimples, and skin eruptions. Of all chemists, or post free from the Bile Bean Co., Red Cross-street, London, E.C., for Is. lid. or 2s. 9d. per box (2s. 9d. box contains three times is. 1?„d. size). Bile Beans are sold only in boxes, never loose.

HOCKEY.

SOUTH MANCHESTER v. STOCKTON HEATH.

This match was played 3.t South Manchester. Stockton Heath made two changes in the team which was de- feated by Huyton, H. Kirkconnel going centre forward, and Eric Fairclotth playing centre half. The game was very fast, and Stockton Heath were soon attacking, Richardson finishing a good movement by scoring from close quarters. For a long time give-and-take play was the order. At length Bond beat all opposition only to miss the ball when taking his final shot. For the rest of this half things were very level, but Stockton managed to obtain a second goal, Bond scoring from a difficult position. Half-time: Stockton Heath, two: South Manchester, nil. From the restart, the Stockton halves were kept busy, and aftertwo unsuccessful shots at goal, Le Roy scored with a shot that just dropped underneath the cross bar. Following a good hit by L. Fairclough, the Stockton forwards got going again, and W. Richardson and H. Kirkconnel both scored before the end. Final: Stockton Heath, four; South Man- chester, one.

fHE ONE HOPE.

BY RAILTON WEST,

Luthor of "The Marquis's Double," "Muriel's Marriage," "Cleverly Tracked," "A Daring Scheme," "Worth Her Weight in Gold," &c.

A man and a girl were slowly pacing up and down a shady avenue, too intent on what appeared to be a discussion of importance to heed the rain- drops pattering through the thick foliage overhead. "After six years of waiting," the girl was saying, "to give him up just when he has come over hoping to be married in a few weeks' time. It seems so horribly mean, Denis; I can't do it. If only he were still in Africa it would not be so bad, but, as it is--" "Our two lives must be sacrificed," the young fellow put in quickly. "I wish you did not care so much and I could bear all the pain," she said with a sigh. "Oh, he must have comet" her tone changing to one of consternation as she caught sight, through a gap in the trees, of a dog-cart being driven rapidly away from the front door. Denis Calvert caught her two hands in his.

I "Then make my excuses to your mother, dear, I and I will make my escape by the side gate. I can't face Atherton yet. Amabel, promise me if there is the least chance of your being released that you will not let any false sentiment stand in the , way of our happiness." "How can you ask ? " she answered, reproach- fully. "You know well enough how hard it is for me to go through with this. He wrung her hands in silence, sprang over the low hedge separating the end of the avenue from the field beyond, and was gone before she realised what the parting meant. A moment later she was summoned to meet Lucas Atherton, the man who had just arrived from Natal to make her his wife. Sixteen when she first 'met him, seventeen the day he had persuaded her mother to allow the engage- ment between them. His prospects were good when he had bade her farewell some weeks after, but things had not turned out as he expected. Year after year had passed and Amabel Wyngtte was Amabel Wyngate still, with but little probability of soon changing her name. Prudent relatives shook their beads and said Mrs. Wyngate was mad to have allowed the engagement, but Amabel, an only child, had never been thwarted by her sunny- tempered, easy-going, widowed mother. "She will have all the money at my death, and I can afford to make her an allowance until then, so why should not the child marry a poor man if she loves him," was all the reply that could ever be extracted from Mrs. Wyngate by the friends who ventured to remonstrate.

"If she loves him." That was the problem Amabel had felt unable to solve to her satisfaction when, eighteen months ago, her lover had paid a flying visit to England. Two days together, then a wire came summoning him back in hot haste, his partner being dangerously ill. But short as had been the stay it was long enough to have aroused a feeling of uneasiness in the girl's mind. Could the good-natured but rough-mannered man be really the Lucas Atherton her girlish fancy had invested with qualities which he, plain-spoken, honed, straightforward fellow would never have dreamt of applying to himself. Had he been longer with her he would quickly have discovered the change in her feelings and released her from her promise. As it was he left, never doubting but that, like himself, her great desire was to put a speedy end to the weary time of waiting. And now the end had come, and they met, he all eager- ness, all impatience to call her his own; she with the knowledge she no longer loved him. "My little girl, if you knew what this means to me," he whispered, when the first greetings were over, not noticing in the joy of holding her once more in his arms how she shrank from his caresses. "Why, my pet, your dress is quite wet, and your hair too," stroking it with reverent touch. "What have you been doing to yourself?" • If I told him "Saying a last good-bye to his friend Denis, the man I love," what would he say? she thought, and turned off the question with a laughing rejoinder of having been under the trees which were so thick she had not noticed the rain.

"Sure proof you need someone to look after you, little girl." The term of endearment she had thought so sweet at seventeen jarred upon her now. "Well, as I said in my last letter, that can be as soon as ever you like. lam not due for another nine weeks, so the plan I thought of was this— working backwards, three weeks for the voyage, three weeks honeymooning, three weeks for getting clothes and things. •How does that division strike you, eh ? " She shivered involuntarily. "I could not possibly be ready in three weeks, but if we made the voyage the honeymoon you would still be there to date, Luc."

"How good it is to hear the old name from your dear lips," he exclaimed, rapturously. "Of course, my feeling is the sooner we are married the better. But you must arrange what suits you best, darling. BY the way, vhat is your mother going to do? She will miss you awfully. I declare I feel a perfect brute taking You away; but we must per- suade her to come out soon on a long visit, for the voyage is really nothing. I would say come back with us, but after being separated all these years to be quite by our two selves will be such happi- ness. Amy, I believe you have caught a chill running about in the rain, you careless little puss, you are trembling all over. "Nonsense," she retorted, lightly. "It has turned colder to-day, and you know I always did feel the cold. But about mother. I am afraid she will miss me, though she is very good about it, and says now that my cousin, Frances Batchelor, has agreed to take my place she shall be perfectly happy." "Ah, yes, Frances Batchelor. Yea have often mentioned her in your letters. You and she are great chums, are you not? I'd say ask her, too, for as long as she liked to stay, but though I've worked my hardest I'm far from a rich man yet. I wish to goodness, for your sake, that I was better off."

"Don't worry about that. I really don't mind in the least," she answered, thankful to be able to say with perfect truth something to please him. He drew her to him, calling her his brave, loyal little girl; praising her for her faith in him, her constancy, till she could have cried from the strain of hiding what it cost her to listen to the words of love.

"By the way," he said, at length. "Do you ever see anything of my old chum, Denis Calvert? You have not mentioned him in any of your letters lately." She nerved herself to answer unconcernedly, and to her relief he found soon after that he must leave to catch the train which would take him on to his father's house some miles away.

"The poor old man will have been expecting me ages ago, he said. "But 1 felt I must see you before anyone else. Let me have one good look at you, Amy, to make sure it is not a dream this time. Often when I have been resting, tired out with a long day's work, I have shut my eyes and fancied you were before me. I seemed to see you so clearly; your grey eyes, pretty oval face, with its soft colour, and lovely sunny-tinted hair. But all the dreaming does not come up to the reality, sweetheart. The dad always has cut up rough about me, you know, and never cared for me as he did for my brother Matt. Poor chap, I used to think what a pity it was that he, the clever one, was killed in that accident, and I, with not half his brains, escaped. But since you love me I am thankful I did escape. It has been hard to bear the governor's harsh words and taunts at my non- success, yet with you to work for nothing has been a hardship. Your love has been .my one hope through all these six years, darling, my one hope. And with those words ringing in her ears Amabel was left alone.

He would be back the next day, he told her, but for the present she was free, free to try and reason down the fierce revolt that had seized upon her. She must get away from the house. Any moment her mother might come in, anxious to hear what Luc proposed doing—if the wedding-day was fixed, and which day she would have to sail. Snatching up a cloak, for it was still raining heavily, she fled to the further end of the garden and took refuge in a:tutnble-down summer-house never used by anyone but herself or her cousin Frances, and it was here some time after that Frances found her, sobbing her heart out, cold, wet and miserable. "Let me be, France. I dare not see mother yet. She would guess something is the matter, and be hurt if I could not explain." "Aunt is in the drawing-room with old Miss Calvert," returned her. cousin. "The old dame always stays a good hour while her fat horses have a rest in the stables, so you are safe for that time at least. Besides. von can't stay out here in the

, - and aant quite sure you ought not to let things gt 'al an instant longer than you can help, so tht looner we talk matters over the better. lam more experienced than you, and may find a way out of ;hedifflculty."

Too wretched to resist Amabel allowed herself tc 3e carried off.

i "I suppose this all came about while I was in Switzerland this winter ? " Frances began when she had administered to her cousin's creature comforts, snd been told in despairing tones that it certainly . would' be a relief to discuss the case in point. I "Before that. I found out I did not care for Luc as I used to do that time he was over for mm :•,ouple of days, but it was not until I met Denis that--"

i "You found out you loved someone else," put in Miss Bachelor, drily. "Why did not you write to Luc and tell him so ? "

"Because until last week, the day we were both nearly drowned on the river, I had no idea Denis Isared for me. He would not let me suspect, for nothing would induce him to be disloyal to Luc, but when that boat upset and he feared he could not Salle me, the words slipped out almost without his knowing. I could not say I did not love him. He risked his life for mine, France. But for him I should suit be here now."

"Yes, I know," replied the other, with a shudder. "He has behaved nobly in every way, for it's harder for a man to suffer in silence, week after week, than do a gallant deed on the spur of the moment. You must put him out of the ques- tion, tlitugh, just at present, and think of what is to be done about Luc."

"There is only one thing to be done : I must marry him." "And spoil three lives ? " "What else can I do? He says that my love has been the one hope that has carried him through these six years of hardships. His great regret ii that he has not been atle even yet to provide a home worthy of me, and that I shall have to what he calls rough it a bit.' Denis is rich, so if I break off the engagement, now what can Luc think but that I have thrown him over for the sake of the position Denis offers me? He will say directly: You never found out you did not care for me till a man well off came across your path.' I had not met Denis when Luc was over here last, you see. If it were not for the money it would be easier, but as it is, what will everyone think of me ? " Miss Batchelor suppressed the reply that rose to her lips: "The world will say you have acted wisely.' Amabel had led a peaceful, sheltered life, and was not well acquainted with the world and its ways. Frances, early thrown on her own resources, was old for her twenty-seven years. "You will say," Amabel went on; "that I ought to have written whether Denis had spoken or not, but Luc was so fond of me and I thought I might make him" happy even if I. were not happy myself." "A fallacy, my dear. A man soon finds out if a woman has ceased to care, and then—Heaven help them both! You are undoubtedly in a most uncomfortable position between your two lovers. Still, my advice is—tell Luc the plain truth. He will be terribly cut up, poor fellow, but better the bitter awakening now than later on when you are bound together for life." There was silence for a minute, then Amabel said: "What about mother? She does not 'suspect anything is. wrong, I am sure."

"Aunt will be torn asunder between sorrow for Luc and joy at keeping you near her • but when the first surprise is over, joy will win the day." "I can't feel yet that it would be the right thing to do," sighed the girl, wearily. "It seems so mean."

Frances talked to her long and earnestly until she made her promise that at any rate Luc should be told the main facts of the case. Then, if he still held her to her word---. "But he won't, or I am very much mistaken in my man," Miss Batchelor concluded. "There goes Miss Calvert's carriage. Run and tell aunt everything, so that when the poor fellow comes to-morrow she will have had time to think over how best to brr.e.. the news."

"Don't cry so, Amy, my darling. It's not your fault, not one bit. I ought never to have let you engage yourself to me when you were too young to know what love really meant." Luc Atherton had been told, and, as Mrs. Wyngate confided to her niece, had taken the news beautifully, and seemed only anxious to make it easy for Amabel. Indeed, so calm did he seem that she began to wonder whether, after all, he did care for the child as much as they had all thought. Frances, understanding the nature of the inant better than did her placid-tempered aunt, guessed how hard was the struggle against self that was going on in the adjacent room. It was long before Amabel could be comforted, long before she would let him leave her. Now that the moment of final parting had come she realised clearly the true worth that lurked beneath the rough exterior of the man she could not love. Well for her that she did not see him when, alone at last, he dropped the mask held so rigidly between her and his suffering. "Denis is a good sort. He will make her happy, dear little girl he muttered, brokenly, when spent with the long struggle he began to turn his thoughts to the future. "As for me. If my country ever needs me I am ready to serve her.' And when that hour came, as it did a year later, Luc Atherton was one of the first to join the ranks. He was no lover of the Boers, knowing facts too well from his long sojourn amongst them. There was no one to work for now, so if they looted the home he had taken such pride to get together, what. did it matter? Ile would give up all, fight for the right, and at least sell his life dearly. The highly-prized cross would have been his had he lived to receive it. for lie saved his commanding officer's life, and helped many a wounded comrade from the thick of the fight; but the hardest bought victory had been long before when he sacrificed the one hope of his life for love of the girl dearer to him than life itself. WYCLIFFE TEMPERANCE SOCIETY. How to Accomplish Temperance Reform. REV. JOHN YONGE'S SUGGESTIONS. IP,

..........; The opening meeting for the session of the Wycliffe Temperance Society was characterized by an interesting address by the president of the society, the Rev. John Yonge on the means to be adopted to bring about temperance reform. There was, unfortunately, only a small attendance at the meeting. .S Mr. B. FARRAR presided and at the outset remarked that he thought there should be a temperance society connected with every church in the town. The need for ' temperance societies and bands of hope was as great at the present time as ever it was. In fact the need was if anything the greater as though temperance principles were undoubtedly becoming more general the advance of those principles was not nearly so great as was the case 30 or 40 years ago. Mr. Farrar went on to say that drink brought a curse into thousands of English homes and it was their duty as ,temperance men and women to combat the evil by being ever ready to back up their principles and to bear testimony to the good arising from temperance. (Hear, hear.) The Rev. JOHN YONGE regretted the smallness of the attendance, but thought this was due largely to the many counter-attractions in the town. Amongst the adult members of Wycliffe Church there had not, he was sorry to say, been that earnest interest in temperance which the cause deserved, and that combined with the exceptional reasons which existed against there being a large gathering, he thought, was responsible for the smallness of the attendance, a smallness which he hoped would not be repeated in the future meetings of the Society. Some 25 years ago Sir John Bright strongly advocated temperance, and said appeals should be made to all members of Christian Churches in the hope of creating a strong public opinion amongst thoughtful and religious persons in favour of temperance, and to afford to the nation a great example of abstinence from the use of articles which were so seldom useful and so often pernicious. Since those remarks were uttered there had been a great increase in the interest shown by Churches in the temperance movement, and in the practice of total abstinence. Amongst Con- gregational ministers had this been especially noticed and to the same extent must the interest shown by Congregational people be increased or else it was the case of a large amount of love's labour being' lost. Since Mr. Bright so strongly advocated total abstinence his advice with regard to the Churches had been so widely followed that temperance sermons were regularly preached, and Temperance Sunday found a permanent , place in our calendar. Personally he did rot feel that ITemperance Sunday was as useful as might be supposed for the obvious reason that the sermons that day were addressed to persons who did not practice temperance and from his personal experience he found that those persons whom they most desired to influence took the opportunity on Temperance Sunday of staying at home, or else of attending somewhere where they did not think they could be shouted at. Advertising the preaching of temperance sermons was not likely to draw to any church a congregation com- posed, or even partly composed of those persons who needed reforming in that direction, and he was sure that it would be better for ministers to take their congregations by surprise and to preach a temperance sermon when such a sermon was least expected. There were four good reasons why Christian Chiarches should have temperance assoviations in their midst. First for

the inculcation of the practice of total abstinence ; secondly, for the confirmation of the habit : thirdly, for the encouragement and promotion of legislation both local and national in furtherance of temperance; and fourthly for the salvation of persons from the evils of drink. The inculcation should begin with the children and that could only be accomplished by means of hard work and at the risk of considerabl3 personal incon- venience and the sacrifice of time. The motive of total abstinence could be taught to the children in the schools by the delivering of scientific lectures by com- petent teachers. That had been attempted and far more was being done year by year. But there still remained. the fact that many Government Inspectors were not friendly to it and that the Board of Education was exceedingly unsympathetic and did not give theproposal its approbation. Apart, however, from the education in schools on the evils of intemperance, more in that direction could be done at their temperance meetings by the giving of scientific lectures on the subject. In that direction they could do a great deal of good. At any rate he thought it was worth trying, as he must confess that as years went by he doubted more and more whether they could find people who could,amid the counter attractions attend the ordinary temperance entertainments. More- over he did not think the temperance entertainment of the present day was strong enough to do any permanent good, though that was, of course, their object. With regard to the second point they wanted more anti-liquor legislation. One of the very valuable services done by the Wycliffe Society, in the past was to keep them in touch with any projects which would have the effect of furthering the progress of the sale of intoxicating liquors. They acted as a sort of vigilance committee and kept their eyes and ears open for all applications for new licences or for the transfer of an old license to a new neighbourhood.Any Christian church which feared to declare itself an enemy of the drink traffic was unworthy of its name, and they at Wycliffe never feared that it should be known that they were always on the side of public welfare and were not to be terrorised by the power of the trade. They did not desire to be offensive but they were determined to be firm and fearless in their opposition to the drink traffic. Happily, and it was to them a matter for congratula- tion,the whole trend of legislation in Warrington during recent years had been in favour of restricting rather than advancing the drink traffic. When they considered that in Warrington they had such a large number of brewers who supported their charitable in- stitutions it was, he thought, a matter for congratula- tion that they had not more licensed houses than was the case, as they often found in towns where brewere were large ratepayers the town had to pay for having in its midst gentlemen, some of whom he must admit were generous benefactors and philanthropists. Had not the Government stepped in with the Brewers Relief and Endowment Bill, known as the Licensing Act of 1903, they would have been able to do more in the future than they had done in the past. It was their duty to see that the next Government so undermined the act that the interest of the people would be safeguarded and placed before the interests of the omnipotent brewers. That act had afforded us the miserable spectacle of the bishop supporting the brewers and of clergyman supporting the brewer because they were afraid to endanger the subscriptions which they received as the result of the drink traffic. In Warring- ton they would probably have noticed that clergymen on just coming to the town were somewhat outspoken on the subject, but had later received certain hints which had caused them to considerably moderate their tone. They must see that what was objectionable in the recent Act -was obliterated, and that in its place new legislation was introduced which would advance the interests of temperance. That could only be done by personal self-sacrifice, by individual effort, and by earnest, kindly and neighbourly influences, and yet it could be done if they as temperance people would only take the duty upon themselves, and never miss an oppor- tunity to do something to further the cause which they had so much at heart.

During the proceedings songs were ably rendered by Miss L. Travis and Mr. B.Hibbert. Miss Hibbert acted as accompanist.

CIRCULATION OF BANK NOTES.

lhe general public don't know much about the circu- lation of bank notes. What is chiefly impressed upon most of us concerning bank notes is, that in the course of their circulation they all too seldom find their way into our pocket books; and that on the rare occasions when this happens their circulation is not long delayed, and they are soon upon their travels again. In America (where they are used much more than in our country), worn-out bank notes are made into car wheels, and in that form enter ..ipon another, though different, career of circulation.

The tendency of everything is to wear out—bank notes, coins, clothes, our livers, kidneys, and what not Truly we can think of no exception. The ultimate fate of aft things is wreck,. _

"For fully twenty years I was what may be called a physical wreck." These ominous words are the beginn- ing of a letter written by Mrs. Elizabeth Shirt, on 10th of August, 1900, to the proprietors of Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup. "in all that time I was never free from indigestion and bronchitis," continues Mrs. Shirt. 'Even in the best summer weather my breathing was bad, and as soon as the cold and wet of winter set in I was laid up altogether. "in December, 1897, the influenza gripped tight hold of me, and for some days my condition was critical. At last after a stiff fight I recovered from the influenza ; but it left me weak and exhausted, with the bronchitis worse than before. Sometimes I had to gasp like a fish out of water, and was so wheezy that my breathing could be heard all over the house. Then a hard dry cough fastened upon me, and shook my body through and through. So violent was it that I used to get black in the face, while beads of perspiration rolled off me. My sleep was so broken, and I ate so little, that a mere walk across the room tired me more than a walk of miles had formerly done. "A doctor attended me for a long while'and did his best; but when at last I was sure his medicine was do- ing me no good, I ceased to use it and began to take Mother Seigers Syrup. I was better from the first bottle, and went on taking the Syrup and improving until I was completely cured." Mrs. Shirt still lives where she lived in 1900—at 12, Hibbert Street, New Town, New Mills, near Stockport. Writing again on June 14th, 1904, Mrs. Shirt, says : "My health continues excellent. The medicine that saved my life has also proved very beneficial in every case where I have recommended it." It has sent sailing . again on Life's ocean many a human craft wrongly sup- posed to have been a hopeless wreck.

The Examiner, Saturday, October 22, 1904.
