COUGHS,

make the most thoughtful ratepayer despair of ever coming to an absolute and satisfying conclu- sion on the trading question. But we counsel our townsmen to mentally weigh, scrutinise, and com- pare the reports, and then, we venture to think, they will be able to settle once and for all in which direction their judgment must be cast. For the present, so late was it when copies of both reports were to hand, we decline to attempt any compre- hensive or detailed examination either of the specific charges made by the Non-Trading Com- missioners, or the defensive and extenuatory statements of the Corporation representatives.

It is interesting, and not unprofitable, although our judgment for the time being is suspended, to put side-by-side some of the conclusions which each trio of Commissioners publishes in the respective reports. We will call the Non-Trading version by Messrs. S. Norbury Williams, John ;Pemberton, and Adam Horrocks, No. 1, and that of the Council nominees,Messrs. Wilkinson,James Evans, and A: Peacock, No. 2. "We in the past, with such have perused and con- serious results to the good sidered the Report of the name of Warrington." three Council Commis- sioners, who preceded us, which we consider is a fair and honest report, and with the finding of which we fully concur." . . . "We have pleasure in re- cording our testimony to the fact that the Officials of the Corporation have rendered all the assistance they could in the conduct of the Inquiry and we are satisfied that the business of the Corporation has been throughout conducted in an able, efficient, and honourable manner."

The above quotations provide a little comedy in contradiction, such as " Truth " would revel in. They are also the very definite findings of men whom, we are to presume, speak from honest con- viction. But it is impossible, in the nature of things, that both verdicts 'can be rigiit. Which is the on to be generally accepted ? That is a grave question which the ratepayers alone can and mus:, decide. There appears to us, however, to be this distinction between the two reports. That of the Non-Traders i. very materially and solidly substantiated ipy evidence in many respects of the clearest char • acter, while the final opinions of the Council Com- missioners bear the appearance of being somewhat hastily formed and somewhat recklessly expressed. For instance, iu No. 2 quotation, these gentlemen say "we would point out that not a single definite charge of any kind has been made against an individual member or official of the Corporation throughout the Inquiry." Their three prede- cessors, also appointed by the Council, in their report themselves named five mem- ' bers or ex-members of the Council whose conduct they classed as "irregular "—and yet Messrs. Wilkinson, Evans, and Peacock say of this previous report that they fully concur with its finding. How are these statements to be reconciled? We do not profess to know, but we think they detracti considerably from the weight and judicial character of the report in question.

The news of the revival of trade is very good hearing. It is a fact that there is an improvement in the business of the Stock Exchange, and this is an index to the whole book of commercial suc- cess. Reference to individual trades seems to bear out the promise. The universal providers in town are doing even better than the proximity of winter would lead one to expect. The beginning of a new season naturally produces quickened activity, but the improvement goes beyond that. There is,apparently,m the significant phrase of the markets,a loosening of money all round. The Stock Exchange portents are advertising better things for, at any rate,the concluding months of the year, and that, with a better agricultural year an.' a better has conspired to render prospects industrial gener tone, has more cheering. A Regent street costumier has lately been receiving again, is an unfailing moving If this is overdue indication that accounts,m 0 andr more mtohnaety, t so, smaller shopkeepers are no less sure to feel the benefit. The outlook in the weaving trade is brighter, and on the whole tradesmen may look for improved returns this Christmas and New Year—as compared with those of last winter. Wholesale and retail merchants are reporting things brisker than for some years past. We have , had the bitter complaint of the lean years. The note to-day is more sanguine, and if the hopeful expressions one hears in various quarters are real- ised, a fillip to trade will be experienced in nearly every direction.

THE EXAMINER.

OCTOBER 22nd, 1904.

"THE hopes in Sc. Peters- THE BALTIC burg that General Kußo- FLEET. PATKIN would be able to

resume the offensive after all, and even break through the Japanese cantre, have been speedily dashed. They had no foundation except the mishaps which befel a Japanese force of less than 5,000 men, which was isolated from its supports,and compelled to abandon a few guns. It is significant that even when enveloped by a whole Russian division this column was able to cut its way out. "Our adversary is of an exceptional kind," says the " Novoe Vremya" candidly. "No European nation has had to stand before such soldiers. On the other hand, the Japanese have no reason to under-rate their adversaries. They have totally frustrated KITROPATKI N's plans, but they have not overcome the spirit of his troops. After ten days of the most determined fighting ever seen, the Japanese line is fifteen miles nearer Mukden ; but the Russians shew no sign of abandoning the city. It is probable that there will be a pause now, such as followed the battle of Liao-yang. Both sides are weary, and the tremendous waste of ammunition has to be repaired. It is difficult to understand how there can be a single Russian or Japanese cartridge left How the armies are provisioned is also a puzzle Evidently the country cannot support them; and if it be true that one pressing reason for KUROPATKIN'S unsuccessful advance was the lack of food, he must be suffering a good deal more on this score than the Japanese. They have perfect facilities for rapid transit; he has only the single line of railway from Europe. Reinforcements come to him that way,but how are they to be fed? It is possible that he may yet be starved out of Manchuria rather than driven out.

It looks as if the Baltic Fleet had positively weighed anchor for its voyage to the Far East ; and the whole world watches this strange adven- ture with wonder, not unmixed with sympathy As far as we can make out, the Fleet is to be dependent for its coaling chiefly upon German steamers, which are to perform this office without any breach of what the Kaiser supposes to be neutrality. What view the Japanese take of this singular arrangement we do not know; but we can imagine a situation in which the process of coaling at sea will be rudely interrupted by emissaries of de wakeful Togo. The appearance of these unfriendly strangers might give the German colliers time to clear off the scene, or it might not. If a misdirected shell or a wandering tor- pedo should send a German steamer to the bottom, what would the discerning KAISER say? Would he upbraid Japan in the War Lord's most emphatic style of eloquence, or would he admit that German merchantmen carrying munitions of war to a belligerent must run the risk of destruc- tion ? In view of the War Lord's uncertain tem- perament, all this is pure speculation. But it is quite plain that he has the strongest desire to see the Baltic Fleet reach the Far East,and destroy the Japanese naval power. The chances of this enter- prise are not brilliant. The voyage, even if it be comparatively smooth and uneventful, will take nearly four months; and it is improbable that before the end of that time Kuropatkin will either have relieved Port Arthur or recaptured it from the Japanese. There is a suggestion that when Port Arthur is occupied by Japan we shall give up Wei-hai-wei, and that the KAISER will seize it as he seized Kian-chau. Wei-hai-wei is of no particular use to us; but we are not likely to give Germany the opportunity of utilisiug it to threaten Japan.

To-day will be .a day of sensation in Warringtog for the long-awaited and eagerly-anticipated final reports by the Commissioners of the Non-Tradinn Association and the recently appointed additional Commissioners of the Corporation on the great Inquiry which has extended over twelve months, are made public. Nothing in municipal affairs can well exceed the gravity of the allegations with which they deal, and we earnestly hope that the ratepayers of Warrington will bring calm, dispassionate, and deliberate judgment to bear on the subject matter of th ese two reports. The task of :ultimately arriving at a sound verdict is rendered especially difficult by the fact that both in substance and detail the reports are diametrically opposed to each other. This is sufficient in itself almost, to

Polyglot Society. Dr. Ormandy gave a very interesting paper on Wednesday on "The Unity of Matter in the World and in the Universe." After introducing the subject in German, the lecturer proceeded in English for the benefit of the non-German .section, and explained in a very lucid manner the various means of proving the uniformity of matter, and the great part which the spectroscope has p . played det9rmunng the identity of gases. A few simple experiments :were useful in driving home the subject, and in the discussion which followed further light was given by Dr. Markel and other members. The next paper by Dr. Ormandy is already being looked forward to with much interest. ASK fGr CUNNINGHAM'S HIGH.CLASb IqnsTERAL WATERS.—Advt.

Crowded Out. Owing to the enormous 'pressure on our alp ce duo to the publication of the Inquiry reports, we have been compelled to hold over several items of news, including the first annual dinner in connection with the Warrington Bowling League.

Accidents. On Thursday afternoon a youth named Harold Perkins, residing in Bostock-street, Warrington, received severe burns about the face and hands whilst following his employment at Messrs. Monks, Hall, and Co.'s ironworks. He was putting some paraffin on a furnace, when it blazed up and inflicted the burns.-- A little boy named Thomas Kelly, aged seven years, residing in Edward-street, was run over in Froghall- lane on Thursday and was severely injured. ASK for CUNNINGHAM'S HIGH-CLA.SS MINIMA WATERS.—Advt. Wycliffe Y.P.S. _ _

The weekly meeting of Wycliffe Young People's Society was held on Monday evening, the Rev. John Yonge presiding. An able and interesting paper on "Employment for women" was submitted by Miss , Emily Brown who alluded at some length to the , domestic servant problem, and suggested that freer ' conditions of work would be an encouragement to more girls to enter service. Dealing with the employ- ment of females as clerks, &c., she expressed the view that when women proved themselves capable of doing men's work they should be paid at the same rate.—An interesting debate followed and Miss Brown was thanked for her paper. Social Service League and Labour Repre- sentation. The opening debate in conn ection with the newly. formed Social Service League t ook place last evening the Rev. H. Dunnico presiding.: The subject under dis- cussion was whether or not the League should see affiliation with the Labour Representation Com- mittee, and after arguments on both sides it was re- solved by a good majority that "Seeing that the Labour Representation Committee is not a trade union society and that the committee is in such an tin settled state we do not seek affiliation." Presentation to a Local Journalist. Mr. Joseph Hawthorn, who has just severed a connection extending from boyhood with the staff o this journal to take up an appointment on the "Liver pool Mercury," was on Saturday evening the recipien of a solid silver cigarette case, suitably inscribed from his Warrington colleaaues. Mr. H. Isherwoo made the presentation in well chosen terms and pai a deserving tribute to the ability which Mr. Hawthor has brought to bear in the discharge of his dutie and to his amicable relationship with his fellow pressmen. Other members of the company endorse Mr. Isheiwood's remarks, and Mr. Hawthorn mad a suitably reply. GREAT SALE OF BOOTS AND SHOES now 011 UNDER THE CLOCK.—Advt. St. John's Literary and Social Society. The usual fortnightly meeting of this Society was held in St. John's schoolroom on Tuesday evening, the President, Mr. P. 11. Brown, occupying the chair. The Rev. J. L. Milne, M.A., 8.D., gave an address entitled "A half-hour with the Dictionary." Mr. Milne said the remark "as dry as a dictionary " was one very commonly heard, but he had not think it was a correct one, and those making it must not have consulted a good dictionary, or if they had they must have done so carelessly. A good dictionary was the most inter- esting and instructive book they could have on their shelves. Mr. Milne went on to show at length how by studying:the dictionary words with their derivations and original meanings much valuable and interesting information is obtained of past times, and of social life &c. He dealt with the many languages from which our words have been derived, indicating how we continue to add new words to our dictionary. Some discussion afterwards took place on the address and the lecturer was accorded a hearty vote of thanks. The next meeting of the Society will be held on Tuesday, November Ist., when there will be mis- cellaneous debates. Passive Resisters' Sale. Another stage in the Warrington Passive Resisters movement was reached on Monday afternoon when the third sale of goods seized in default of the payment of the Education rate, was conducted by Mr. Robert Lowe. Hitherto it has been the practice to hold the sales in the Market Place, but this year the goods were sold together with the goods seized for the ' non-payment of rates in general. The names of the resisters, together with the amount of the rate required were :—Mr. Wm. Clatworthy, 21, Lovely-lane, 7s. 4d.; the Rev. W. j. Roberts, 30, Parker-street, 6s. id. ; Mr. Norman Balmford, 15, Museum-street, 10s. 6d. ; Miss Emma J. Fieldhouse, Bridge-street, us. 6d. ; Mr. Edward Howard, 192, Padgate-lane, ss. 6d.; the Rev. J. L. Milne, 33, Orford-road, 78. 6d.; Mr. William Gallop, 69, Buttermarket-street, 10s. ; Mr. William Mayer, 33, Froghall-lane, ss. 6d.; Mr. Thomas Dale, 76, Priestley-street, is. 4d. There was a large attend- ance of bidders and for several of the lots there was some spirited competition. Six of the lots were bought in, while two, a silver watch and a gold ring were to outside purchasers. A pair of silver candlesticks, the property of Mr. William Clatworthy went to Mr. Curngan, of Altrincham, but were re-purchased by Mr. Clatworthy's agent who returned to Mr. Carrigan the amount of his purchase money together with 58. for his bargain. Town Council Business. The final meeting of the Town Council for the pre- sent municipal year will be held at the Town Hall on Tuesday evening when the report of the Commis- sioners who have conducted the trading inquiry will be presenied.—At a meeting of the Electricity and Tramways Committee a letter was submitted from Councillor Stott asking if fitters' work on the repair- ing of cars was being done by labourers who received sixpence per hour—The manager submitted correspondence on the matter and the com- mittee approved his action.—On the suggestion of Councillor Peacock it was decided to run workmen's cars on the Longford route after 5 p.m.— At a meeting of the General Purposes Committee correspondence with respect to the widening of Warrington Bridge, which appeared in our last issue, was read—The committee after carefully considering Messrs. Fairclough's offer and Mr. J. J. Webster's report thereon, regret that Messrs. Fairdough's pro- posal is not feasible.—Councillor Stott will move that the provision of an open-air swimming bath for the Orford•Ward recreation ground be considered and that the Council adopt a contract penalty clause to the effect that as a penalty for the first offence, the Council shall not consider any tender submitted by the offending contractor for a period of one year from I the date of the contract having been broken; for a second offence the penalty shall be two years, and for a third offence, no tender for any work shall in future be considered by the Council from that Contractor. Alderman Sutton will move a vote of thanks to the Mayor.

H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TSNIC is the KING OF TONICS.-63, SANKEY STREET, Warrington.—Advt. Police Sports.—A Record Financial Result. A meeting of the Police Athletic Club was held at the Police Buildings on Friday, 14th instant, Sergeant Smith, the senior Sergeant, occupying the chair. The hon. secretary, Sergeant Evans, presented his report on the recent Police Sports, and the hon. treasurer, Inspector Taylor, produced the balance sheet in con- nection with the same. The sp9rts(as already reported in our columns) were an unqualified success, and the hon. treasurer's statement, read at the meeting, showed Ithat financially they established a record, the nett sum realised being no less than £73 4s. as against £3l 16s. 6d. last year, though it must be remembered that in 1903 the sports meeting had to be postponed owing to unfavourable weather. Of the total sum realised on the present occasion, £36 .12s. has been handed over to the funds of the WarrTgton Infirmary, and £lB 6s. to the District Nursing . Association, each institution receiving more than twice as much as it received last year. At the meeting a hearty and unanimous vote of thanks was. passed to the Chief Constable, the committee, officials and ticket sellers, who all worked together most energetically to bring about such a satisfactory result. The Chairman also voiced the feeling of all present when he said that the police owed a debt of gratitude to the public of War- rington who continued to so loyally support their efforts in connection with the annul sports fixture. not a little of the success of which, as he aptly observed, being due to the fact that the function took place on the tradesmen's half-holiday, when the temporarily leisured townspeople took a lively interest in the Police Athletic Gathering. All the acting officials were re-elected, and the meeting closed with a vote of thanks to Sergeant Smith for his conduct in the chair.

At Cheshire Quarter Sessions on Monday, Den'S Byrne of Lymm, for stabbing David Ashley twice after being struck with a poker during a public-house row, was sentenced to three months' imprisonment. Sankey Harriers and A.C. The above club had a good muster last Saturday. Starting from the Black Horse the pack proceeded across Burtonwood and Bold, returning through Penketh, with a break from Sankey Chapel House The turnout was captained by Joseph Riley. Daresbury Vicar's Resignation. General regret is felt in the Daresbury parish at the inpending departure of the Rev. F. Clifton-Smith who during his three years' residence in the district as the Vicar of All Saints Church, has made himself very popular. A few days ago, Mrs. Park Yates, of lace Hall, near Chester, offered Mr. Smith the living of St. James's, lace, and he has accepted it, intending to commence duties there in about three months. Concert in Aid of a Cripple. A concert was held in the schoolroom, Croft, on Saturday, the object of which was to raise funds to send James Allen, a cripple boy resident in the village, to Blantyre to be treated by Mr. Rae. The concert was largely attended, Mr. Cawthorne presid- ing. An excellent programme was submitted and as the result of the venture a sum sufficient for the pur- pose in view was realized. The Wesleyan Church for Stockton Heath. We learn that it has been decided to erect a new Wesleyan Church on the site of the present church ie Walton-road, Stockton Heath. THE INQUIRY REPORT OF COUNS. J. WILKINSON, JAMES EVANS AND A. PEACOCK. i;ompiete Divergence from the Non- Traders' Report and Upholding of the Corporation.

Late last evening we received a copy of the report of Councillors Wilkinson, James Evans and A. Peacock, who were present at the last 13 sittings of the Commission on behalf of the Corporation. We are unable to give it in full,but the following account contains the most essential parts. After quoting• from the Municipal Corporations Act, the report continues : Town. Hall, Warrington, September, 1904.

DEAR MR. MAYOR, In accordance with the terms of our appointment, we have in conjunction with the representatives of the Non-Trading Association duly enquired into all the matters referred to us. We have carefully perused and considered the whole of the shorthand notes taken daring the sittings held by our predecessors from the 22nd September, 1903 to the 21st July, 1904,as set out in the printed minutes with which we have been furnished. Since our appointment there have been 13 sittings of the Commissioners and the evidence tendered has been fully reported, making the total number of sittings held 69. We consider that the whole of the shorthand motes are of sufficient importance to v,arrant the ex- pense of re-printing, and the ratepayers being supplied with copies at a nominal charge. The list of shareholders referred to shows that tha number and value of shares held by members of the Council past and present (with one exception) are infinitesimal compared with the capital of such Com- panies. The list also shows five Officials of the Council hold or have held shares in five Companies trading with the Council. The total capital of these Companies is ,e 1,130,000 and the total value of shares .heldby Orhcials £2,100, which is less than one-fifth of one per cent. It may be interesting to know that the total capital of the Companies as shown by the return prepared 'from the Registers at Somerset House was £11,708,001, and the value of shares held by members of the Council and Officials in such Companies £134,019, which is equal to one and one-seventh per cent. A perusal of the shorthand notes taken both prior to our appointment and since, proves emphatically that whilst several gentlemen have been severely and in our opinion unjustly attacked, the main object of those who promoted the recent agitation has been directed -principally against one Company. The following statement prepared by Mr. C. W. Davenport shows the value of materials supplied by the Warrington Slate Co. Ltd. to him as Contractor for works carried out for the Corporation during the seven _years covered by the Inquiry : Materials supplied Contract by the Price. Warrington Slate Co Ltd. £ s.. d. Electric Light Station New Police Buildings New Technical Schools 13,649 1,778 0 0 Delph-lane Pumping Station 9,271 1,829 0 0 High Warren Reservoir 6,748 52 0 0 Sanatorium, Sankey

76,202 11,983 10 0 The following particulars were supplied by Mr. J. B Johnson the Contractor for plastering, &c., in connec .tion with tha undermentioned buildings : Materials supplied by • the Warrington Slate Co. Ltd. £ s. d. Electric Light Station 75 10 5 Police Buildings Hill Warren Reservoir 670 14 4 Sanatorium

The above figures were supplied by Mr. J. B. Johnson on the terms and conditions set out in the following letter. (A copy of this letter is included in the report), The amount paid to the Warrington Slate Co. Ltd. by the Contractor for the Delph-lane Waterworks I •Extensions was an. By the addition of an estimated I -sum of say £2OO paid to the Company by various Con- tractors on the above works we find that the indirect-1 trading done by the Warrington Slate Co. Ltd. with the Corporation on the above contracts was £13.677 •13s. 11d. The total amount paid to the Contractors to date on the above Works according to the statement prepared by the Borough Treasurer is £93,103 13s. 10d. No further evidence has been laid before the Com- missioners relating to the amount of indirect trading by any of the Limited Liability Companies in which members or officials of the Council were interested with respect to other contracts of the Corporation. A suggestion WAS made that as Mayor's Auditor, Ex- Councillor A. J. Jolley and Mr. Alderman Bennett had illegally received payment of the same fees for audit- ing the corporate accounts as were paid to the Elective Auditors. WA find this suggestion without foundation and untrue, the Town Clerk advising that the payment of such fees is perfectly legal. We naturally thought that after the statements and allegations which were made by the agitators previous to the Inquiry, and by Mr. Councillor Dodds in par- ticular, that the gentlemen appointed by the Non-Trad- ing Association would have been prepared with some definite data to work upon. This, to our surprise! was , not the case, not only did they refuse to give any infor- mation, to the Commissioners as to the names of persons willing to come forward to substantiate the statements made by them and others from time to time, but they took no steps so far as we can see to secure evidence in support of such allegations. We would point out that not a single definite charge of any kind has been made against an individual member or official of the Corporation throughout the Inquiry. Adve- rtisements were inserted on several occasions in the local newspapers, inviting evidence and asking for statements in writing to be sent to the Commissioners, but without -any response. An attempt has been made to show, that Ex-Coun- cillor William Roberts has beeu guilty of' immoral' trading with the Corporation becauie he was the paid Secretary of a Limited Company trading with the Cor- poration to the extent of say £lOO a year; yet we find the firm of which Mr. Councillor Dodds is the paid Secretary doing trade with the Corporation to the ex- tent of say £5,000 a year. We are unable to see how one case di ff ers from the other. If it is wrong in one case it is wrong in the other, but we are advised there is nothing illegal, and in our opinion nothing improper in either case. We now propose to deal exclusively with matters aris- ing since our appointment. The greater part of the time of the last 13 sittings was spent in questioning officials and searching Specific- ations, Bills of Quantities and Tenders in order to find out if any and what indirect trading had been done by members of the Council and Officials of the Corporation. No evidence was brought forward to prove that there bad been any illegal or 'immoral" trading by members of the Council or officials. We decline to believe that a single ratepayer with the welfare of the town at heart would agree for one moment that our local merchants should not quote, in open competition, for articles manufactured in the town, because a particular Company happened to have a Shareholder or paid Secretary who was a member of the Council. If such a contention could be upheld it would be the means of depriving our local workingmen of employment: whilst the legality of such a transaction is beyond all doubt. The following is an example of what would be immoral if the opinion of some individuals was accepted: —A obtains a contract from the Corporation and sub- lets a portion of the work to B, who in turn purchases some of his material in the ordinary course of business from C, a Limited Liability Company which has a Shareholder a member of the Council. We leave to the 'common sense of the Council and the Ratepayers the answer to this remarkable piece of sophistry. A suggestion was made and an attempt to prove it by hearsay evidence that the tenders for the construction of the Tramway permanent way in 1901 were improperly opened by the then Mayor. No evidence was brought forward to substantiate the allegations but on the con- trary it was proved that the tenders were dealt with in a straightforward manner. The following are the facts as proved by evidence in connection with this matter : At a meeting of the Tramways Committee held on the 13th March, 1901, at which eight out of a possible tfn members were present, the Town Clerk reminded the Committee that the tenders would be in before the next ordinary meeting. The Committee appointed the Chairman (Mr. Henry Roberts) and the Deputy-Chair- man (Mr. F. W. Monks) as a Sub-Committee to open and initial the tenders, and instructed the Town Clerk to forward them afterwards to Messrs. Preece and Cardew for report. The tenders had to be delivered before 12 o'clock on the 27th March, 1901, in accordance with terms of the advertisement. Arrangements were made for the Sub-Committee to meet at the Town Hall on this day about 5 o,clock. The Chairman attended, but the Deputy-Chairman telephoned to the Town Clerk stating that he could not attend, and requesting the Chairman to proceed in his absence. The tenders were opened and initialed by the Chairman in thepresence of the Town Clerk and his Chief Clerk (Mr. Spinks) who immediately parcelled up the tenders and despatched -them by rail to Messrs. Preece and Cardew. (A schedule of tenders for Tramway Work is here put in by Messrs. Preece and Cardew). We would point out that when Mr. Councillor Dodds appeared before the Commissioners on the 6th June last he stated that he could give evidence with respect to the conversation with Mr. Ewart relating to the question of tramway rails which would be corroborated

Iby a member of the Council. The evidence referred to ' was never corroborated; on the contrary it was abso- lutely denied, Jl9 &town by the following letter : Vernon Street, Warrington, ' June 23, 1904.

Dear Sir. I have read the print of evidence given by Mr. J Dodds before the Municipal Trading Committee. The statements made by Mr. Dodds in reference to my con- versation with him on the journey to Liverpool are not consistent with facts. I can only describe it as a garbled "version, in many respectslfalse, and on the whole unreliable. Such a statement,being the recollec- tion of a conversation which took place over two years ago, should never have been made. Yours truly, J. A. EWART.

The most offensive insinuation in this matter,namely, that the then Mayor used information derived by him from the tenders which he opened and initialled, for the purpose of obtaining business in connection with the supply of rails for the tramways, is shown to be absolutely baseless by the fact that the tender did not contain the name of the Company from whom Mr. Ewart intended to obtain his rails.

The question of the supply of fuel to the Electricity 'Works has been fully inquired into. (A statement is here put in by Mr. Matthias, the Electrical Engineer, giving details of coal contracts.) The report goes on: It will be observed from the above figures that the greater part of this fuel was supplied by Messrs. ThrntchleySt td. of Notting- ham, and Messrs. J. Pendlebury C0.,d0., of Warrington, and that the bulk of it was purchased without comply- ing with the Standing Order, which requires that when- ever an expenditure of £2O and upwards is involved tenders shall be invited by advertisement in the local newspapers. Evidence was given as to the difficulty in using ordinary fuel with the particular boilers in use at the works; and that during the greater part of the period covered by the above return the Engineer (Mr. (4rimsdale) was experimenting with the object of ascertaining the most suitable fuel. The following is theconclnding naragraph of the report of Mr. Grims- dale to the Cammittee, and dated 19th November, 1902 :

From the above figures there can be no doubt that the Nottingham coal has given the best results, both as to cost and cleanliness. With water-tube boilers it is advisable' even at a slightly increased cost per kilowat hour to use clean coal, as :with a dirty coal and one that clinkers badly the firing doors are opened for long periods when clinkering, admitting cold air to the tubes, causing them to eool on one side, resulting in distortion and fre- quent re-tubing We are of opinion that the action of the Committee throughout was in the best interests of the Corporation; and that there is no evidence of anything of an improper character in connection with these transactions In all the trading transactions which have occurred between Limited Companies and the Corporation (in which Companies' and members of.the Corporation have been interested) there is no evidence whatever of any- thing which could be considered of an illegal or immoral character. In no single instance has any evi- dence been adduced to prove that any member or official of the Corporation who was a shareholder in any of the Companies referred to, has in any way whatever attempted to make use of his position in an improper manner or has abused the confidence placed in him by the Burgesses. We have perused and considered the report of the three Council Commissioners who preceded us which we consider is a fair and honest report and with the finding of which we fully concur. It is to be regretted that as a direct result of the unfounded personal charges of corruption Warrington has been deprived of the services of gentlemen who for many years have done good work for the town and who for reasons whichare more or less obvious have been the special subject of attack during the recent agitation We have pleasure in recording our testimony to the pact that the Olicials of the Corporation have rendered all the assistance they could in the conduct of the Inquiry and we are satisfied that the business of the Corporation has been throughout conducted in an able, efficient and honourable manner.

We are, dear Mr. Mayor, yours faithfully, (Signed) JOHN WILKINSON, ARTEIUR PEACOCK, JAMES EVANS.

"THE GONDOLIERS."

SUCCESSFUL PERFORMANCES BY MEMBERS OF THE MUSICAL SOCIETY.

Whea the curtain was ruag dowa ou the first pro- duction of "The Gondoliers" by members of the Warrington Musical Society in Parr Hall on Tuesday, it was to the accompaniment of the appreciative acclaims of a crowded audience. Success had crowned the efforts of the entertainers, and the ear-haunting melodies and spicy humour of one of the most popular of the Gilbert-Sullivan collaborations had given manifest pleasure and amusement. A stranger would probably have been surprised to learn that it is but twelve months since the opera company made its first public appearance. Its formation mlrked an entirely new departure in tke history of the Musical Society and of local amateur musical productions. For years the musical society has itself with commendable zeal concentrated its efforts on the interpretation of classical works, but owing to lack of general supp t a financial deficit gave rise to anxiety. Some of the bolder spirits conceived the idea of catering for the popular taste by producing an opera in the hone 9f placing the society on a sounder footing. The public response was equal to the rosiest expectations, and the complete success which attended the performances of The Yeomen of the Chard " is still fresh in the minds of Warringtonians. It was only in accord with the generally expressed wish, that a second operatic venture was embarked upon, and the choice of "The Gondoliers" Proved most poplar, albeit it was an ambitious one. The',, work bristles with so many difficulties for both chorus and principals that many amateurs might hesitate to attempt to surmount them, but to the credit of the opera company be it said that they entered whole-heartedly into the rehearsals, and Tuesday found them quite prepated for the raising of the curtain. From beginning to end the performances have gone with a delightful swing, and recalls have been the order of each evening. The enthusiasm evinced testified eloquently to the favour which amateur opera has already found in Warrtngton, and must in itself have been fall compensation to those who have played so prominent a part in realizing the project. With a charming setting of Italian and Spanish scenery The Gondoliers" is sparkling and tuneful, and an adequate production calls for sustained vivacity as its first essential. It is truly Gilbertian in concep- tion. Imagine a kingdom under the joint sway of two monarchs who, governing on the lines that "all shall equal be," are full of gratitude to their retainers for allowing them double rations, and think it only proper that they should make themselves useful about the Pal.ace by running errands for Ministers of State! Equally unconventional is the distinguished Duke who has suffered himself to be turned into a limited com- pany and "floated at a premium." The opera story binges upon the marriage in infancy of the real King of Barataria, who, through a revolutionary crisis, has been brought up in obscurity unaware both of his claim to the throne and of the fact that he has been united in wedlock when a babe. Of course, there is a pretty entanglement, but the fertile brain and facile pen of the librettist find a happy solution and the finale is realised under conditions of unalloyed bliss. The opera has hardly the variety of incident and certainly not the scope for dramatic powers that "The Yeomen of the Guard" affords, but to compare the two is somewhat unsatisfactory as the works are strikingly different in character. " The Gondoliers " is purely comic and as such abounds in eccentri- cities and sprightly whimsicalities which find ready , acceptance with the audience. The words, which have the true Gilbertian stamp, are wedded to bright and happily-conceived music, but as may be readily I imagined it is by no means easy for concert soloists to accustom themselves to rendering the catchy and in many cases rapidly-moving numbers which have a telling effect. The principals addressed themselves with confidence to the task, and the result was most highly creditable. In order that the reader may see at a glance upon whom the major part of the work devolved, we give below the dramatis personae The Duke of Plaza-Toro (a Grandee of Spain), Mr. C. P. Gough. Luiz (his Attendant), Mr. J. E. Hannan. Don Alhambra Del Bolero (the Grand Inquisitor), Mr. J. W. Bibby. Marco Palmieri, Mr. J. Rabbitt ; Giuseppe Palmieri, Mr. Fred Fieldhouse ; Antonio, Mr. W. Moores; Francesco, Mr. J. Parkinson; Giorgio, Mr. 3. T. Knowles; Annibale, Mr. J. H. Fisher, (Venetian Gondoliers). The Duchess of Plazo-Toro, Miss Emily D. Jeans. Casilda (her Daughter), Miss Arline Harrison. Gianetta, Mrs. Arthur Booth; Tessa, Miss Mabel Henshall ; Fiametta, Miss A. Daunt; Vittoria, Miss M. W. Collin ; Giulia, Miss Willcocks, (Contadine). Inez, (the King's Foster Mother), Miss Willcocks. It will be noticed that most of the leading characters found a place in the cast of last year, but 'Miss Arline Harrison and Mr. J. E. Hannan made their debuts as principals. Before the first opera, there was naturally much speculation as to how performers who were figur- ing for the first time in such important roles would acquit themselves. The laurels then gained inspired the public with every confidence that justice would be done

o "The Gondoliers." Nor were expectations belied, for there was a unanimity of opinion that the parts were )f siagularly uniform excellence and could hardly have be 3n better placed. When the uprising curtain had !isaloSed a brilliant scene on the Piazetta, Venice, the Lis 3111 Aly had not long to wait for a foretaste of the fascia tams; things th it were to rivet their attention throughout the evening. The buoyant gondoliers hiving ,ninglei with the gay company of contadine, the sang and chorus For the merriest fellows are we" was soon in progress with rare zest. Mr. W. Moores was a rollicking Antonio and his bright singing of this little I solo part combined with clever dancing and a lively chorus called forth an encore. But it was the advent of Marco and Giuseppe—the "two for whom in duty every maid in Venice sighs "—that roused enthusiasm to a higher pitch and the attentions lavished on these favourites by their fair admirers on the stage must have been embarrassing. Mr. J. Rabbitt is to be congratu- lated upon a triumph as Marco. He realised to the full the possibilities of a part brimful of gaiety and gallantry. As vocalist and actor he excelled himself, and his powerful and rich voice, used to the best advantage, moved the audience to enthusiastic acknowledgments. His soulful rendering of "Take a pair of sparkling eyes" in the second act was quite the crowning song of each evening, and the assembly would not be satisfixi without two repetitions of the last verse. Mr. Fred Fieldhouse made a dashing Giuseppe and he was thoroughly at home in both singing and acting the part. His singing especially was effective, although at times his enunciation was not quite clear. Giuseppe's chief effort was the song "Rising early in the morning "descriptive of the arduous day's work of a republican monarch, and considering the length and difficulty of this number, his interpretation was most creditable—so tuuch so that later in the week he was invariably encored. The sparkling duet "We're called Gondolieri " by Marco and Giuseppe found good scope for sweetly blending the voices of the Gondoliers. There was a pretty and animated scene when Marco and Giuseppe, securely blind-folded, chose their brides from the group of contadine, this novel way 'of " letting impartial Fate select their mates," creating no little hilarity. The choice of Gianetta and Tessa was received with great applause for the two ladies who achieved such high honours in last year's opera were again at this moment brought into prominence. The renewal of the acquaintance, in the operatic realm, was a happy one, and all who wit- nessed the performance were loud in their praises of the vocal and histrionic talents of these two favoured of the contadine. Mrs. Arthur Booth greatly enhanced her reputation, infusing just the life and crispness into her portrayal of Gianetta which such a charming part requires. She also made the most of the dramatic side, and the pleading song " Kind sir, you cannot have the heart our lives to part" was given with such exquisite feeling as to be encored at every performance. Her beautifully sweet and flexible voice was here heard at its best, and when the concluding words were reached the rounds of applause that broke forth were seen to be no empty compliment. Miss Mabel Henshall, as Tessa, was vivacity personified. In her case actions conveyed almost as much meaning as words, and from the very first she was a favourite with the audience. There was an irresistible piquancy about her singing and acting which could hardly have been improved upon. Her most successful song was of course "When a merry maiden marries" and so admirably did she treat this number that t'.ere was a emphatic recall each evening. The quartettes by the I four leading characters we have mentioned were delight- I ful features of the opera. Particularly bright and tuneful was the one ending with the lines "Oh! 'tis a glorious thing I ween to be a regular Royal Queen." Thearocalists entered thoroughly into the spirit of the thing, and brisk vocalisation allied with droll actions at once stamped the effort as a great success. The cry of "encore!" was loud and persistent at each perform- ance and had to be responded to. Anlitem decidedly unique of its kind was the quartette "In a contempla- tive fashion." It was laughable in the extreme to see how the calm and sedate method of disentangling the knot developed into a heated wrangle, and having regard to the peculiar technical difficulties attending the in- terpretation of this quartette the fact that it was often encored is a striking testimonial to the excellence of its interpretation A comical character indeed was the Duke_ of Plaza-Toro who despite straitened. I , circumstances laid claim to enormous social in- fluence, and Mr. C. P. Gough who scored such a success last year was thoroughly at home with the part. He was a paragon of dignity and deportment, and his triumphal entry in the second act with the Duchess and Casilda. attired in gorgeous raiment and carrying a huge bouquet was most effective. The orchestra struck up martial music, there was an imposing proces- sion of heralds and halberdiers, and as a spectacle this feature took the fancy of the audience immensely, and each evening there was enthusiastic applause. The Duke's song "In enterprise of martial kind," in which he recounts his valiant deeds in the field, went with a fine swing, although the words were not always quite dis- tinct, and during the week this number has been more than once encored. Miss Emily D. Jeans comported herself with fitting hauteur as the Duchess and showed herself to be possessed of a fine contralto voice by her interpretation of "On the day when I was wedded," which last evening received a well-merited encore. She was associated with the Duke in the duet "Small titles and orders " which as a piquant recital of congenial means of adding to one's income was a great hit. Miss Arline Harrison won unstinted praise as Casilda, the Duke's daughter, and her debut in opera must be re- garded as most promising. She acted with an easy grace moderated by a pleasing self-command, and charmed the audience with her tasteful and expressive singing. The deeply sentimental scene with Luiz, after the Duke's startling disclosure of her marriage in babyhood, was one of the prettiest and most interesting features of the first act. Mr. J. E. Hannan also created a favourable impression as Luiz— at first the dutiful " suite "and afterwards king—and although his voice and style do not constitute him quite an ideal lover, he aquitted himself with much credit. The duet "There was a time" in which Casilda and Liz were alliedavas a gear of harmony,and rounds of applause rewarded the vocalists. The charm- ing gavotte commencing "I am a courtier grave and seri- ous" was encoredat each performance. The courtly way in which the Duke,in conjunction with the Duchess and Casilda, sought to instruct the mcnarchs to "combine a pose imperious with a demeanour nobly bland" was quite a treat', and the closing stately dance provided a charming spectacle. Mr. J. W. Bibby achieved a no table success in the rote of Don Alhambra del Bolero, for which both his fine voice and dignified bearing admir- ably fitted him. His dignity, however, was never oppressive, and even when expressing mild dissent from republican ideas, there was always a sly twinkle in his eyes and a quiet humour in his dialogue. It was indeed amusing to see how the Grand Inquisitorial sternness was relaxed when assailed by the fascinations of Gianetta and Tessa. The Inquisitor also sang with a hearty robustness which found general favour, and he was encored for spirited renderings of," I stole the prince" and" There lived a king," every word being clearly heard. Miss Willcocks ably impersonated Inez, the king's foster-mother, who amid breathless suspense makes the announcement fraught with such import to the main participants in the opera. The other minor characters , were in the capable hands of Miss A. Daunt, Miss M.W. Collins, Mr. Parkinson, Mr. Knowles and others and all did justice to their parts. One could, not fail to be impressed by the spirited rendering of the choruses, all of which indicated a most careful training and patient study. The voices were nicely balanced, the attack was strong, and there was almost always that attention to light and shade which forms so essential a part of an adequate interpre- tation. In the finale to the first act the volume of sound was particularly fine, and the smartness of" se, here we are, at the risk of our lives," when the con- tadine broke in upon the seclusion of the palace was really admirable. But the most beautiful and ani- mated scene of the opera was undoubtedly witnessed amid the picturesque surroundings of the palace of Barataria, when gondoliers and contadine entered into the delirious gaiety of the Cachucha dance. The brilliant costumes of the rapidly moving dancers, the vane- coloured ribbons attached to inspiriting castenets waved aloft, and the lively, swinging music, all com- bined to produce a climax which took the house by , storm. Thrice was "the reckless delight of that wildest of dances " engaged in, and on each occasion the onlookers evinced their approbation by enthusiastic plaudits. With a grand chorus involving a modified repetition of the dance Warrington's second amateur opera—which we hope will be /the forerunner of a long series—came to an animated close. The interest throughout was unflagging, and each evening the principals were called before the curtain to receive a deserved ovation. There were also vigorous calls for the stage-manager and conductor, and they too had a great reception on responding. On Wednesday evening a magnificent basket of flowers was presented to Miss Arline Harrison, on Thursday a splendid bouquet to Miss Mabel Henshall, and last evening there was be- stowed upon Mrs. Arthur Booth an embarrassing wealth of floral tributes—a huge basket of flowers and two lovely bouquets. All the principals are to be complimented upon the admirable restraint and self- command which characterised their performances after the first night, the result being that every humorous point and every quip and crank was brought home to the audience in quite a professional sort of away. The orchestraselected from the society's members was we understand, entirely amateur in its composition. It rendered most capable and loyal service, for like the vocalists the various instrumentalists had devoted them- selves assidugusly to the work of preparation,and it was no doubt gratifying to all concerned to know that they contributed in a most essential measure to the happy consummation. The overture and several introductory passages—notably the one which heralded the approach of the Ducal party—were given in rousing style,and the tasteful accompaniment of the many quieter numbers was much appreciated, although the" wood-wind" and the "brass " were not perhaps always quite in tune with the strings. Mr. F. EL Crossley wielded the baton with customary skill. He has throughout taken the deepest interest in the production of the opera ancl he is to be complimented upon having piloted the company through the many musical intricacies of" The Gondoliers." The society had been fortunate in again securing the services of Mr. E. Thornley-Dodge as stage manager and dramatic instructor. The performance itself was the best testimony to the tact and ability which he had brought to bear in the discharge' of his onerous duties, and a large share of the credit for the

splendid success of the opera will b ..ungrudgingiSi accorded to him.

I Mr. J. Newcombe was an indefatigable hon. secretary ' and treasurer. There were packed houses on Wednesday, Thursday, arid Frilay evenings, and judging from the demand for seats the hall will again be filled to its utmost capacity this evening. The souvenir programme has commanded a ready sale. It contains a summary of the music and story of the opera and is embellished with excellent photographs of the principal characters and of the officials, Mr. F. H. Crossley, Mr. E. Thornley-Dodge and Mr. J. Newco mbe.

One of the most exciting matches of the season is down for decision at Wilderspool to-day. Wigan were here twice last season and succumbed each time, but they are as confident as ever of beating Warrington on their native heath. I can't imagine them doing anything of the kind, though they are admittedly a strong side, in the light of Warrington's form at Old. ham. In any case with a representative team up Warrington should prove the conquerors. Last week the Wiganers had 15,000 spectators, and a good num- ber of them are sure to follow the team to-day.

Tt-e game was one of the best I have seen. Oldham are a side which goes in for scientific football and abhors spoiling tactics, and with Warrington operating on similar lines there was an abundance of open play. When a scrummage was formed in mid- field you would see both sets of backs lined out at the proper angle behind the pack. and not lying up level with the halves in order to scnother one another. The result was a display which would have done credit to Rugby football under any Union. It was a contest of strength, speed and strategy and honours were just slightly on the side of the losers. They were only beaten by a goal kick and long before the close of the game they were completely overplaying the home team in every department. The Oldham crowd was very thankful indeed when the game ended, for a side never look-3d more like collapsing than their own at this stage.

Though Boardmaa's damaged hand will keep him out of the Warringtou team, they should be strong enough in the pack. It is expected that Harris will turn out to-day, and a warm welcome certainly awaits him. Morrison will come into the forwards.

The full side is :—Hallam, full back; FA, Isher- wood, Dickenson and Harris, three-quarters; I Davies and Hockenhall, half-backs; Preston, Shugars,Belton, Thomas, Cooke, Jolley, Naylor, and Morrison,forwards.

It. was distinctly surprising to find Warrington lasting so much better than Oldham. The latter possess a doughty set of forwards, but they were played to a standstill in the last 2) minutes, and the backs had a very streaky time. Warrington ap- peared to come into form almost at a pump. For half the game they were ordinarily good, but never looked like getting the upper hand. Then all at once they settled down to brilliant football and quite outclassed the opposition which was morally beaten repeatedly. Though the spoils remained with the homesters, still it is exceedingly gratifying to look back upon the excellence of Warrington's dis- play. Their perform-mce augurs well for some of the matches nearly at hand.

The expectation that Warrington would be able to put a full team in the field was not realised. Harris found that his:leg would not bear the strain, while Hallam also stood down through injuries received in the County trial match. These vacancies resulted in Short and Knight coming into the team, which was not much weakened by the changes. Perhaps a rest will do Hallam good. He has not shown his best form yet.

There was a strong breeze blowing on the Water- sheddings heights, and Oldham had first advantage of it. Consequently Warrington had to fall back early on, but they were hardly prepared for the goal which Ferguson kicked from cantre in the first three minutes. A. Lees had marked and I, personally, was not much concerned when Ferguson placed for a go il-kick. It is not often a punch from half-way is successful, but this was one of the exceptions. The ball would have sailed over the bar had it been 20 yards further away. It was a fine kick and one could have admired Ferguson's prowess had he not tried again about half a dozen times and been nowhere near the mark. "There was an elephant of luck " about the first kick as the humorist says. Anyhow it won the match, for plor Warrington were just a goal behind at the end. They held their own very well up to the interval and looked like crossing over with only 2 points against them, when Civil dashed over splendidly from a pass near the line and made the figures into 5-0.

There seemed less breeze on changing ends, and Warrington were sometime finding their feet. Oldham made a great effort for ten minutes daring which time Spottiswood eluded Fish and Knight and scored. Either man ought to have stopped him but both failed, and another bir, nail was." driven in" the Warrington coffin. They soon kicked the lid off, however, and proved very lively corpses. Oldham nevbr had another "earthly," for Warrington took possession of the game completely, and the home defence was severely tested. Fish was tackled a yard short once more, and Hockenhall appeared to ground the ball under the cross bar on another occasion, while Boardman undoubtedly beat R. L. Thomas for possession and dropped on the ball. Neither effort passed muster with the referee, and Boardman's reward was nothing but a split hand which caused his retirement from the game. Not even this accumulation of misfortune daunted War- rington and they tried every move possible. At last Fish took a long pass and raced through the home backs, but the try was too far out to be converted. Only about five minutes remained and excitement bubbled all round. Oldham were again beaten back and Hockenhall dodged over brilliantly within five yards of the posts. A. goal was wanted to draw and Fish was the kicker! We Warringtonians felc more than hopeful. He could't miss it, surely? Hurrah! No—the flag has not been raised. Hard lines, only ! And it was— very hard lines. The whistle sounded immediately, and Warrington came away the losing but not the beaten side.

I have already indicated that Warrington played a vastly improved game in the second half. The forwards were splendid and in thoroughly beating the home eight they accomplished a big performance. Hockenhall supplemented their efforts with some brilliant play, while Davies was not far behind. The palm must, however, go to the dark-haired lad. The threequarters were equally good, though Short still shows a want of experience and nerve. He has pluck, but he is not a good substitute for Harris. Knight did very well in the rear and one can almost forgive him missing Spottiswood, except that the slip lost the game. On the Oldham side Joe Lawton was in grand fettle, and A. Lees played very steadily, but as a pair they were hardly up to the Warrington halves. Civil and Spottiswood were the best of a smart lot of three- quarters, while R. L. Thomas was good up to half time. Thereafter he fell away, but the pressure on him was tremendous.

Hockey. [By R.G.P.] On Saturday Stockton Heath visited South *Alan- chester, and were victorious by no less than 4 goals to 1. Although we had hopes of victory, yet this was a result that was unlooked for. The South Manchester ground was not in very good condition, and though the present Stockton Heath ground may be rough, yet it certainly-is much better than the South playing pitch.

L. Fairclough won the toss and selected to face the sun. The first half was even, with Stockton doing slightly more attacking. At half-time the score was 2-0. W. Richardson and C. H. Bond having eath obtained a goal. The second goal should have beet saved, but Bond must be praised for scoring; from a very difficult position. In the second half Stockton Heath obtained two more, and South Manchester scored one.

Although the score is so pronounced, the teams were evenly matched, and up to three-quarter time it looked as if South Manchester would at the worst make a draw. But here a great chance took place,and the Stockton Heath forwards seeming to grasp the nature of the position, played with greater energy, and for the rest of the game kept their opponents busily engaged, clear ing the ball from their circle. As it was W. Richardson and H. Kirkconnel managed to score further goals.

Taken on a whole the best team won, but had . the Manchester backs been as good as their forwards we venture to say •South Manchester would again have come out on top.

All these •guilds were socialistic, in the sense that they aimed at a community of interests for them- The following teams T•-•etcted to play against selves, and the members of their own guilds. I They had not only to manage unskilled and dishonest Pierpoint (backs), J. Houghton, H. Smith, A. Hodgkin- workmen, and ignorant and unruly apprentices, but they son halves):( A. Ferguson, Howard, R. Pierpoint, had to deal with monopolists, who tried to corner the Longdin (W. L.) Skelton (forwards). Train leaves labour market, and buy up their raw material, and they Central Station 1.50. I had to resist the tyranny of kings, and manorial lords. You can take it from me that they had not their,troubles to seek, and theydid not always take the wisest way out of their difficulties, but they did some noble and useful work for the benefit of the workingmen of those days, and they showed us the way to some valuable and far-reaching reforms.l I think I can give you the best idea of their con- stitution and work, by taking a few representative Craft Guildsas object lessons,and giving youa few samples of their rules and regulations. The "Clothiers and Clothworkers' Guild" at Reading included clothiers, clothworkers, dyers, weavers, shuttlemakers, clothdrawers, and ashburners. The first four branches of workmen I have named, paid two pounds each on admission to the guild, and the remainder paid only ten shillings each. Among their rules it is decreed that "no member shall do any work for any customer who is indebted to another brother of the craft." This is a capital idea for the men of our own times, and if we could boycott everybody till they had paid for their clothes, some fine people would soon have to go about in the costume of Father Adam, and Mother Eve, when they had a wardrobe of leaves. In order to prevent the growth of monopolies in any branch of their trade they decreed that "no clothier should weave in his own house cloth for another clothier, under a penalty of six shillings and eightpence for each offence.' Any member using or occupying in his house more than two broad looms was fined ten shillings for each loom. Mr. Aldworth, an ex-Mayor of the town, was by special permission allowed to have four looms. Any weaver proved false in conveying away or detaining yarn or cloth was for ever disfranchised, and he had no claim against the guild. One of their difficulties was the limitation of the rights of the Kings subjects,in the interests of their own craft If a Guild Merchant, who did not belong to their brotherhood,opened a shop in any town, they could only boycot him and persuade the people to have no dealings with him. 'But when they sought further favours from the King, they inserted a clause in their new charter, giving them power to control the shops that sold the goods, as well as the workshops whereithey were made. Under the pretence of searching for dishonest and fraudulent wares, they could enter a shop any time, and makes things very unpleasant for their rivals. • The Tailors' Guild at Newcastle-on-Tyne, in 1536, put a stiff price on outsiders who wanted to4rade there. It decreed that "any person born a subject of the King, and free of Newcastle, may est up a shop, on payment of forty pounds, with a pound of wax and a pot of oil on his admittance, and he must pay eightpence a year to the Miracle Play, and thirteenpence to our Lady Light." Forty pounds was a large sum in those days, and it was a prohibitive price to pay for the privilege of com- peting with the Newcastle traders. One of the rules of this guild says that "no tailor shall work after eight o'clock at night on Saturdays, or OQ Sundays, or vigils or feast days, ' and it discourages night work because the men would not be able to see what they were doing and they might turn out some bad work. The Guild of Cordwainers and Shoemakers at Exeter in 1387 decreed that" every master shall be answerable for his own servants." If you know anything about the doings of the "Prentice Boys " of the Middle Ages you will 71,dmit that this is a large order. It means that the master shall not only keep them in order and make them work while they are in his liouse,but he shall answer for their sins when they are on the rampage in the streets at night. Ale was wonderfully cheap, and they got plenty of it, and as there were neither policemen nor constables to interfere with their mafficking, they a high old time. They were such a terror to peace- able citizens that Corporations decreed that they should "wear their master's badge on their caps or on their coats, that they might be identified and punished. Another rule of this guild decrees that the master and wardens shall search all workshops and houses in the city for badly made boots. If they are made of bad material, or spoiled in the making, they are to be confiscated, and the owner shall be fined, half the pro- ceeds to be paid to the corporation and the other half t) the guild. No craftsman could keep a shop unless he were a freeman of the city and a member of the guild, and he was never allowed to have more than three workmen and one apprentice in his employ. When any apprentice had served his time and paid his fine and been made a freeman, he had to give a silver spoon to the guild, and provide a good breakfast for an the members of his guild. I have no doubt this was the origin of what are called "footings " in our factories and workshops in our own day. lII.—ALL THE CRAFT GUILDS HAD RULES TO REMIND MEN OF THEIR OBLIGATIONS TO THE CHURCH.

{ The butchers and the victuallers imposed heavy fines 'on any brother who killed or dressed any meat during Lent without a special license from the Abbot or the Mayor. The barbers and hairdressers imposed heavy fines oh their brethren for shaving or hair-cutting on Sundays. In towns where'fairs were held three orifour times a year on Sundays, any barber might exercise his calling while the fair lasted. One rule insists that no bull baiting or bear baiting shall be allowed during service at Church. This rule was so frequently broken in some cases that a fine of one shilling each was inflicted on every house- holder in the neighbourhood of the sports. It looks uncommonly like punishing the innocent for the sins of the guilty, but I have no doubt those who had to pay would make it hot for the sportsmen next time. But the great day of the year was the Guild Feast. It began in the morning with mass at Church. The members marched in ptocession' and answered to their names. Those who were, absent from that service were fined, unless they were too ill to attend, or were on the King's service, or were twenty miles away from home. After service they adjourned to the Guild Hall, where the feast was spread. The tables groaned under the load of beef, and mutton, and fish, and fo.wls, and all manner of good things. The beer was carried round in buckets and pails, and every man helped himself to as much as he could put oat of sight. No fines were imposed for absence from that free feed so far as I can make out, but stiff penalties were imposed on those who became quarrelsome, when the feasting and fuddling was over. The rest of the day was spent with mummers and strolling players, and music, and dancing, and miracle plays. The guild feasts were the origin of our modem club feasts, and wakes, and village holidays. The " rushbearings " in Lancashire were a survival of the times when men carried rushes to the church once a year. and strewed them on the floor to warm the feet of the worshippers. My father used to tell a characteristic story of those good oldtimes. He says that when he was a very little boy he used to go to the Parish Church with his grand- mother. When the wakes were overand the church floor was strewn knee deep with rushes he used to carry her footstool, because she had a mortal fear of the mice that made their home there. One Sunday morning, instead of going to sleep under a dry sermon, he amused himself in looking for mice. Presently one of them climbed the footstool, and sat there admiring his grandmother's dress. Fortunately for the peace of that church s e never saw it, but he sprang off Ais seat and made a bold bid to catch it. The beadle followed him with his whip, and lashed him so smartly with it that he gave up mouse hunting and took to crying. How the guilds were plundered and suppressed after the dissolution of the monasteries by Henry VIII. T must tell you some other day.

Angling.

The local clubs have now practically concluded their season's engagements so far as matches are concerned. This season has been by far the most suc- cessful of any recorded during the past few years. Not only have coarse fish provided any amount of sport locally, but the fishing acquired in the Bridgewater Canal has given an impetus to local angling affairs that has been much needed, in that anglers have generally seen the advantage of taking the opportunity of joining some club or other and thus becoming entitled to many privileges not otherwise obtained. Fishing is now con- fined more to individual efforts the capture of specimen fish engaging the attention of those who still continue the pursuit of sport amongst the various classes of fresh water fish.

By far the most importtnt event de3idbd last week end was that of the open contest fished under the auspices of the Broadheath and District Angling Society. The event was decided at Broadheath and 115 competitors took out numbers, and fishing commenced punctually at 3.15. The sport yielded was very fair, considering that only sizeable fish ware eligible for weighing. Result :-Ist, R. Grayson, junr., Salford, 13ozs. sdrs. : 2nd, %V. Goodwin, Pendleton, 12ozs. Bdrs. ; 3rd, R. Melling, Wigan, llozs. 13drs. ; 4th, J. Littler, Broadheath, llozs. 3drs. ; sth, J. Jones, Broadheath, llozs. ldr. ; 6th, F. Watson, Broadheath, Bozs. 15drs. ; 7th, W. Bate, Warrington, 80zs. lldrs. ; Bth, A. Pettitt, Broadheath, 7ozs. 15drs. ; 9th, St. Thomas, Warrington 7ozs. 3drs.; 10th, J. Shepherd, Warrington, 6ozs. 15drs. ' 11th, R. Dearden, Hulme, 6ozs. -7drs. .Altogether; 37 competitors registered their catches, and the largest roach was one of 9ozs. 6drs. by J. Jones. A capable committee, drawn from the Broadheath club and superintended by Jr. G. E. Manby, the secretary, controlled the match and every satisfaction was given. The weighing operations were conducted by Messrs. P. Knight, Pred. J. Emson, and A. Range, whilst Mr. J. T. Bates, of Salford &Hoisted as referee. The prizes were distributed at the Cheshire Cheese Inn, Broadheath, immediately after the conclusion of the contest.

Local waters were fairlf well patronised last week- end. Sport was only moderate, however. The Bridge- water Canal yielded a few roach, the largest being taken by a youngster, Alf Miller, at Grappenhall. The fish scaled 12oz. 12drs. audgeon gave a fair amount of sport. The Old Quay did not provide much sport. At odd times, mostly during the week, the roctch and pike have bitten well in the Old River at Lymm, whilst the river Bollin continues to yield a few decent course fish in the lower lengths. Fish of small proportions have proved the order of things from the river Weaver. Some good pike fishing has been spoken of as having been obtained in private waters in Cheshire.

An open fishing competition will be decided in the waters of the Bridgewk.ter Canal at Lymin to-day Satur- day, the committee having control making the Bridge- water Arms their centre and where the draw will take place not later than 2.30 p.m. This early hour is neces- sitated owing to darkness coming on now so soon. The entrance fee is Is. and non-licence holders will be required to pay a bank fee of 3d. in addition. The contest will be fished under the Worsley Fishery Board rules.

An open contest will also be fished at London Bridge to-day, the proceeds to be devoted to a very deserving cause. The Perseverance A.S. are making all arrange- ments.

[ALL RIGHTS RESERVEDI.

SOCIAL SERMONETTES FOR WORKING MEN.

BY JOHN TAYLOR. Author of '6 Search Lights on Land, Labour

and Social Problems." CRAFT GUILDS.

"Craft Guilds were associations of handicraftsmen, (ir artisans, and were separate from the Merchant Guild. If a town was large enough, each craft or manufacture had a guild of its own, though in smaller towns members of various crafts would form only one guilds."—Gibbin's "Industrial History of England." The Craft Guilds were the forerunners of our modern trade unions, and some of them are of great antiquity. The Goldsmith's Guild dates back to the time of the Saxons, and it claimed to have possessed land before the Norman Conquest. The London weavers are mentioned as a Craft Guild, about the year 1100, and many other artisans, and craftsmen of different trades, petitioned our. Plantaganet Kings for charters to enable them to manage their own affairs. Of course these privileges had to be bought; and paid for, because the kings of those days never, gave some- thing for nothing, and they were very chary about limiting their own power, and parting with any of their prerogatives. Some of thd old guild charters throw a flood of light on those medieval workshops, and enable us to see how the craftsmen of those days settled their industrial problems. I.—ORIGINALLY EACH GUILD RAGULATED

ITS OWN TRADE. Nearly all the petitions of charters complained of in- competent and dishonest workmen, who brought dis- credit on their fellows, and who plundered the public, and they prayed for power to weed them out of their ranks.

At first the real work of the Guild was done by a small executive committee of leading men in any particular industry, who were elected to look after the interests of the members, and promote the prosperity of their own craft. Their fundamental principle was that "a member should work not only for his own private advantage, but for the reputation of his fellow- craftsmen, and the good of his trade." The" Bristol Tailors' Guild" complain that 'the craft of tailors in their honourable town is greatly slandered by ribald and dishonest persons, not skilled in the art of clothing, and not belonging to the business." These men spoil the work, and steal the cloth entrusted to them, to the great slander of the town and craft, and to the great damage of the people. So it ordered its masters and wardens to visit every tailor's workshop in the town, and examine and judge the quality of the work. Any misfit or badly- made garment must be confiscated, and the value equally divided between the guild and the corporation. The tailor who spoiled it should recompense the owner of the cloth, and pay all fines and expenses, "so he shall be better advised to cut Well and efficiently the cloth that is unto him delivered to cut." No man of the craft shall be received into the fran- chise or freedom of the guild, "unless he be able, and of good condition, and of good name, and full perfect in his craft." The master and wardens must examine him, and present him to the Mayor of the town as an able, skilful, and honest workman. He must pay his fines and fees, and he must carry on his trade in an open place, where his work can be inspected, and not in chambers,or in corners. He can have no apprentice, or assistant,till he is enfranchised.

As these Craft Guilds increased and multiplied, they elected a master and two or three wardens every year, to punish bad work, and to secure a supply of compe- tent workmen, and to suppress the production of wares by irresponsible persons who were not members of the craft. They also exercised moral control over the members, and fined or expelled all who were guilty of fraud or felony; and in addition to this social police work, they provided against sickness or accident, and

THE ELECTIONS.

Nominations up to Date. .

Present indications are that the November elections will be much quieter than was at first anticipated. The only:Non-Trading andidate to take the field is as yet Mr. William Benson, and he will oppose Councillor Ellison's I return for Latchford Ward. t is the object of the Non-Trading Association not so much to run candidates of their own and create a separate party in the Council as to aid the return of men of whatever party who share the Association's views on the trading question. We are informed that Mr. James Skelland has been approached by many burgesses of Bewsey Ward with a view to his entering the lists against Councillor Rogers, and he is consider- ing the matter. At a meeting on Wednesday the Trades and Labour Council decided to write to all candidates for election asking whether if elected they would support the adoption of the penalty clause drafted by the Trades and Labour Council, and the retention of the clauses adopted by the Town Council at the September meeting. There will be an interesting contest in St. John's Ward where Mr. J. Fulton Smith will oppose Councillor Thornton. Monday afternoon at five o'clock is the latest hour at which nominations can be received. Up to last evening the following gentlemen had been nominated : TOWN HALL WARD: Mr. W. J. Forshaw ST. AUSTIN'S WARD: Councillor Burton WHITECROSS WARD: Mr. Walter Brough. ORFORD WARD: Mr. John Wilkinson. ST. JOHN'S WARD: Mr. J. Fulton Smith. HOWLEY WARD : Mr. Samuel Davies. BEWSEY WARD: Mr. Wm. Rogers. At a meeting of the Liberal Committee of Latchford Ward last evening Councillor Ellison was thanked for his past services and again invited to become the candidate. He promised to stand. Three nomination papers have been signed on behalf of Mr. William Clegg. of Buttermarket-street, for Howley Ward, in opposition to Councillor S. Davies. We learn that the Liberals of Town Hall Ward have decided to nominate a candidate in opposition to Coun- cillor Forshaw and two names are under consideration.
