THE BENEFITS OF SCIENCE.

It is seldom that any discovery of science is such as will benefit all classes of people. Some of the greatest discoveries of the age, while they have proved directly beneficial to thousands, have also, at least for a time, injured other thousands. Even some of the best medi- cinal preparations, however much they may do to remove one class of disease, or suit some constitutions, at the same time either deepen the hold of another disease, or leave it entirely untouched, or if they benefit one class of patients, they may injure another. Now the great need of the age is some scientifically arranged preparation which will cope effectually with the pre- valent diseases of this country, which will be certain to do good when fairly tried, which will be equally adopted to the needs of the merchant and the workman, the professional man, and he who wins his bread by the sweat of his brow, the student, the clerk, the factory hand, the miner, and the roadside labourer. It should be, too, such a pre paration as contains no injurious ingredients, and which may be taken with impunity by the weakly child, or the delicate lady, as well as by the stronger constituted man. Such a discovery would deserve to be called The Perfection of Medicinal Preparations, and would indeed be an Invaluable Boon to Suffering Humanity. Now this much needed boon has been found, has been tried, has been proved, and wherever proved, it has been found satisfactory. We refer to Gwilym' Evans' Quinine Bitters, The Vegetable Tonic, which when once tried, has been always recommended, and has proved successful when all other medicine has failed to give relief, and we may say further, that it has proved per- manently beneficial, when other preparations at best gave only temporary relief. It is strongly recommended as The Best Remedy of the Age for Indigestion in its different forms, such as Sick Headache, Pains in the Side, Giddiness, Loss of Appetite, also for Nervousness and Nervous Disorders, Sleeplessness, Neuralgia, Low Spirits, and all kinds of Weakness. It has often proved very beneficial to persons suffering from great Weakness, either after an illness, long con fineinent to ill-ventilated rooms or any othe cause. it strikes at the source of the Disease, removing the cause of the illness, and strengthens those parts of the system which have been weakened by it, and therefore most liable to colds and other ailments. Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters being purely vegetable, it is a Natural Tonic, and suited to all ages at all seasons of the year. It is everywhere acknowledged to be a powerful aid in restoring the body to a healthy state when deranged by disease. If you suspect that your health is beginning to fail, brace up your nerves, and fortify your constitution by now a course of this excellent Tonic, which is sold everywhere in bottles 2s. 9d. and 45. 6d. each, but should any difficulty be experienced in procuring, it, the Proprietors will forward same for the above prices, carriage free. Avoid Imitations. When purchasing,see the name, ‘Gwilyui Evans," on Label, Stamp, and Bottle. Sol!' Proprietors: Quinine Bitters Manufac- uring Company, Limited, Llanelly, South Wales.

BEFORE tHE MAGISTRATES.

SHOCKING WARRINGTON HOME. Some shocking revelations were made at the Warring ton Police Court on Monday, when Kate Riley, 14 Leicester-street, was summoned for causing a nuisance by allowing her house to be in a filthy condition.— Inspector Flood explained that the reason the woman was summoned instead of her husband, was that she was responsible for the existence of the nuisance.—Miss Hoyle, nuisance inspector, stated that she visited the defendant's house on the Bth August and found it to be in a filthy condition. She gave instructions that it must be thoroughly cleansed. She paid several later visits and finding no improvement was made reported the matter to the Medical Officer of Health. The walls and the contents of the house were covered with dirt. William and Kate Riley were then summoned at the instance of the N.S.P.C.C., for neglecting their lour children.--Mr. A. Browne prosecuted, and stated that although the husban4 was summoned it was the wife who was to blame for the neglect. Inspector Hackett visited the house on the 22nd June and found it to be in a shockingly dirty condition. The children were also dirty and verminous. The baby was in such a dreadful condition that one object of the prosecution was to get it in to the unionhospital where it could receive medical attention. If that was a case in which their worships thought the woman should go to prison he suggested she should be sent in the second division so that the child could go with her and while in gaol be medically attended. Mr. Browne pointed out that the woman received from her husband and son 365. per week with which to carry on the house.— Inspector Hackett gave evidence in support of Mr Browne's statement. He pointed out that there was very little furniture in the house and that what little there was,was in a filthy condition. On one occasion when he visited the house he found a quantity of partly rotten vegetable 'refuse lying in the back kitchen.— Kate Riley who pleaded guilty to both charges WaS sent to goal for two months in the seeond division.— Upon William Riley promising to have the house cleaned at once the charge against him was withdrawn. —The magistrates made an order for the defendants' six year old son to,be sent to the Workhouse hospital.

Warrington Borough Police Court.

Minor Cases.

MONDAY.—Before the Mayor (in the chair), Aldermen Smethurst and Francomb, Councillors Thornton and Tinnion, and Messrs. T. Birchall, G. Paling, J. W. Hayward, and S. Wright. LODGING HOUSE KEEPER ASSAULTED. Martin Ireland was charged with assaulting Mary Rogerson, lodging-house keeper, Ship Yard.—Mr. A. Browne, who prosecuted, stated that the prisoner lodged in a house kept by Mrs. Rogerson in Cropper's Brow. On Sunday, the 9th inst. contrary to the lodging-house regulations he went to bed in the afternoon. Mrs. Rogerson was informed of this and she went and told him to getup. He did so and while going downstairs used obscene language. Mrs Rogerson thereupon ordered him out of the house when he struck her a violent blow under the heart. She went to look for the police and during her absence prisoner left the house. Since the blow she had been attended by Dr. Liston. Her ribs were injured and she had suffered very severely from the blow, being 'unable next morning to go upstairs without assistance. —Defendant stated that he was defending himself when he struck Mrs. Rogerson.—A fine of 10s. and costs, together with advocate's and doctor's fees, was in- flicted. BEGGING.—John O'Brien, last from Wigan,was sent so goal for seven days, for begging in Horsemarket-at. on Saturday night.—P.C. Baxendale proved the case. ASSAULT Os THE POLlCE.—Thoma,s Green, last from Chester, was charged with being drunk and disorderly and also with assaulting P.C. Tolley, on Saturday night. —P.C. Jolley said he had occasion to arrest Green for his disorderly conduct, when he struck him several times in the face.—The Chief Constable remarked that prisoner was one of the begging tramps with which the country was infested.—Prisoner: And you are another of them.—Prisouer was fined 10s. and costs (11 Is. in all) or in default 14 days' imprisonment with hard labour. "LAWYER" BECKET AGAlN.—Alfred, or " Lawyer " Beckett made his 16th appearance in the Warrington Police Court, ona charge of fighting on the 9th Oct.— He was fined ss.and costs or in default 14 days' imprison- ment with hard labour.

Reynolds. CRUELTY TO A HORSE.—John Priestner, farmer, Appleton, was fined in the costs (s. 6d.) for causing a horse to b 3 worked in an unfit condition.—lnspector Gibson, of the R.S.P.C.A., stated that the horse in question was very lame;and was suffering from ringbone of long standing. When he saw it, it was attached to a light dog cart.—Defendant promised not to lock the animal again.

. Fare Driving : Cabby Inside. At Lymm the other day the unusual spectacle was to be seen of a hansom cabby sitting inside his vehicle, while his farelattired in the driver's livery, tooled the som.—The sequel to this was heard at Lymm on Wednesday, when Ezra Briers, of the Farmers' Arms, Heatley, was fined £4 14s. 6d. including costs for selling drink to a drunken person, viz., Charles Bromley, architect and gentleman :of independent means of Warrington, who was fined 18s. for being drunk in charge of a cab.—A police officer saw Bromley on the hansom, reins in one hand and a glass of beer in the other. He was wearing the cabby's coat and hat, the latter bashed in, and his face was smirched with- mud. On seeing the constable he drove off with the cabman inside, both being drunk. The vehicle swayed about the ,road; and the drive ended at Lymm, where the police had been notified.—For the defence it was denied that Bromley was drunk. rhe cabman had been up all night and was tired, and his fare for fun put on his livery and, smudging his face to avoid recognition drove the h.. Isom.

LYMM GAS REVENUES. An Inquiry and Some Allegations.

Mr. Edmund Pearse Burd, Local Government Board Inspector, held an inquiry at the Lymin Urban Council offices on Tuesday morning into the application of the Urban District Council for the issue of a provisional order to enable them to carry to the district fund all or part of the ,profit arisinr, from their gas undertaking. Mr. Ridgway represented b the Council and Mr. A. Browne appeared on behalf of a large number of gas consumers. Those present included a deputation from the Tradesmen's Association: Doctors Brabazon and Lunt Messrs. W. G. Groves, F. 0. Moston, G. Hoult, and G. Gregson, J. Mullard (clerk to the Council), and others.

Mr. Ridgway stated that when the gas works were taken over in 1872 the price of gas was 6s. Bd. per 1,000 feet, and to-day it was 3s. 3d., though it was 3s. 6d. up to September. For cooking purposes the price was 2s. 9d. The Clerk of the Council had been to a great deal of trouble in obtaining the price of gas charged by other district councils, corporations, and companies with the same output as Lymm, and out of twenty cases there were only two lower than Lymm. The Council must always keep the rates in mind and their intention was to devote such surplus as the Local Government Board might decide might be appropriated from the gas profits to the relief of the rates. Mr. Mullard stated that the profits on the gas under- taking in 1900 were £294 17s. 9d., and in 1904 (all the instalments having then been paid) they were £674. There were 12 miles of mains. He did not know that there was great opposition .to the application in Lymm. There were several gentle-nen highly rated stated to be in opposition to it. Replying to Mr. Browne, Mr. Mullard said a three- penny reduction in the price of gas would mean a loss of profit of £l7O. The future profits would be £5OO per annum. All the amount of the gas works loan of £lO,OOO had come out of the gas consumers and not the ratepayers as ratepayers. Mr. Browne: I think there has been some attempt by the Council to use some of the money, without having power, for the isolation hospital ?—Certainly not. Mr. Browne: Well, where is the mom*,; that is what we want to know ?—ln the hands of the Council,

The Inspector: What about this £2,000 profit? Where is it? _ _ _ .

Mr. Mullard : Partly in the bank and partly due from general account. The Inspector: Then is your district fund over- drawn ?—Yes. _ _

. _ As a matter of fact you have applied some of the gas profits ?—Yes. How much is there now in actual cash in the bank?- 2745 and £1,451 is due from the general account. Mr. Browne: Is the object of the application to pay for the Isolation Hospital ?—I cannot say, but the hospital will have to be paid for. Money might be used for that. When we get the money we will not have the domineering of the Local Government Board, and avoid the necessity and expense of applying for borrowing powers. Mr. Brown.e : Exactly, you want to evade the Local Government Board.

- - Mr. W. G. Groves also gave evidence in support of the application, and said, in answer to Mr. Ridgway, that this application was made with the best intention of benefiting the district and was on the unanimous decision of the Cluncil. Mr. Browne said it was evident on the face of it that the Council had been acting illegally for some time— (" No" from Mr. Wellford and Mr. Moston)—by using gas profits for the general fund instead of money which ought to have been 'obtained directly from the rate- payers. They had only got £754 in the bank from a gas account of £2,000, s Inch showed clearly that £1,400 had gone for general purposes. The true reason of the application was that the Council found themselves in a complete hole and did not know how to get the £1,400 back again without exposing themselves, not only to the ratepayers in general, but to the Local Government Board. He submitted that the Council had not been carrying out their Act, because it said that any surplus should be used for the extension of the works, mains, £c. They had paid the whole of the money they had borrowed to purchase the gas works out of what they had got from the gas consumers, and now, when they had no more to pay, they wanted to take the' surplus profits and use them for the benefit of the ratepayers at large. Mr. Ridgway said the Council had acted in a strictly proper way, and would continue to do so. They were seeking what would result in the greatest good, for the greatest number. On the motion of Mr. Groves, seconded by Mr. Browne, the Inspector was cordially thanked for his courtesy, and the inquiry closed. 'A CONSUMERS AND THE COUNCIL'S APPLICA.TION.

A meeting of the gas consumers was held on Monday evening in the Plough Hotel Assembly Room to con- sider the application of the Urban District Council with respect to the disposal of the gas profits. Mr. A. Watkin presided over a fair attendance. Mr. Watkin at the outset explained the nature of the ' Council's proposals, and went on to say that it was sug- gested that the Council were applying for these new powers with the object of paying the cost of the land and building for the new isolation hospital. Neither he nor any of his friends would object to paying their share for the isolation hospital. but was it fair that the consumers of gas alone should have to pay for it? ("No.") Let everybody pay their share—it was for the ratepayers as a whole. It was certainly not right to make the money entirely out of the gas consumers. (Hear, hear.) Mr. E. R.ansome moved: "That this public meeting called together by the Lymm Tradesmen's Association, desires to record its strong sense of disapprobation of the manner in which the Urban District Council pro- poses to deal with the surplus gas profits, and is of the opinion that the Council's first duty, in view of such surplus and the present high price of gas, is to reduce the price to consumers to 2s. and 2s. 6d. per 1,000 cubic feet for cooking and lighting respectively, with a corresponding reduction on the price charged through slot meters, and that this reduction should come into force before the appropriation of the surplus to other purposes is considered." Mr. A. B. Boumphrey seconded. An amendment moved by Mr. Br.earley that the Council be supported in their application was not seconded, and Mr. Ransome's proposition was carried

A letter was submitted to the School Attendance Committee from the managers of the Sacred Heart (R.C.) school, complaining of the overcrowded state of the mixed department of that school, and in view of the fact that provision had already been made for the sitting accommodation of that department to be in- creased by some 26 places,the director was instructed to intimate to the managers of the school, in reply, that this and a re-organisation of the classes ,wonld probably meet the present pressure upon the ao commodation of the school.

THE " BOLTON " Sc HOOLS.”

At a meeting of the Sites and Buildings Committee itiwas resolved, that an advertisement be issued invit- ing architects, practising within the County Borough of Warrington, to furnish competitive designs for the new " Bolton" schools to be erected in Latchford. Draft conditions for the competition, together with in- structions to the architects sending in designs were considered, and,after various alterations had been made, were approvectand adopted. It, was resolved that the site for the proposed new " Bolton" schools be pur- chased outright.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

POACHER CONVICTED AT RUNCORN.

At Runcorn on Monday, a poacher named Charles Stubbs, of Cligton, was summoned for trespassing in search of gams on the Aston Hall estate of Mr. A. Hervey Talbot J.P. The evidence of two gamekeepers, Peter *Lockett, jun., and W. Read, showed that on the 6th inst. a trap, containing a hen pheasant, was found in the birch wood at Sutton. A watch was set and on Saturday morning, the Bth, the defendant came along the railway embankment put his arm through the fence, and picked up the pheasant. The keepers appeared, and the defendant dropped the pheasant. The defendant sworn said he was on the railway embank- ment searching for mushrooms, and he did not kno w anything about the pheasant.—The defendant was fined 40s. and costs 10s. 6d., or a month's hard labour. He went to gaol. ALLEGED HOUSE-BREA.KING AT WIDNES.

At Viiiiines. oa Monday, Frei Ford, of no fixed abode, was sent to the Liverpool Assizes for trial on a charge of breakin,, and entering the dwelling-house, 55, Bulk street, and stealing a gold watch, two silver brooches, ss. in copper, and is. 4d. from the slot gas meter, the property of Ellen Morris, on Thursday morning. —On Friday morning prisoner was arrested on a charge of being drunk., and upon a search being made the articles were found in his possession. The police had previously received a leport of the robbery, but could not trace the thief. The house was securely locked by Mrs. Morris on Wednesday night, prisoner gaining access to the place by breaking the window. SMART SENTENCE FOR THEFT.

At the Cheshire Quarter Sessions on Monday Enoch Rollinson' sixty-two, boatman, for stealing wearing apparel at Runcorn, was sentenced to twelve months' hard laboar. It was stated he had served one term of twenty years' penal servitude.

FREEDOM!

By Ona Win Balievas in It.

"License th3y mean when they cry liberty."

—Milton.

"Whit is most n3cessary to our present conditions of life would probably evolve if Nature were left a fair field. Genius is a sulerfluity of brain. . . . It is an aclideut, and c innot b 3 directly proincel. It can however, be stifl9l by ov3r regalation, and by so:lti things as compolsary eduAtioo or compulsory military service. . . . Compulsory education has been fre- quently alluded to in thes3 pages, It will be enough to say that the forcible stu fling of facts in the head of an ill-fed child is har nfal, aid also that the stereotyoal form of St ate elac allot' is opposed to the law of progress, which is variety. In addition to this, compulsion is always,wrong. . . . ." "Another part Casa of physic al deterioration not alluded to at the Cambridg3 meeting of the British Association is the Workmen's Composition Act. Since this piece of compolsory rob'33ry, as pointed out in the "Liberty Review." employers have been afraid of employing married men with families. Hence married men have a diliculty at times, in finding employment, and their children are in consequence less well nourished, the children themselves being forbiddin by law to earn money in school hours. Anothenpart cause is the substitution of compulsoryldrill, compulsory gymnastics, and com- pulsory swimming for the free vent of various faculties in games, labour, or inclinations of an artistic or in- ventive nature. Actions that are pleasurable prove beneficial to the owner's general organism. 'Happiness is the most powerful of tonics.' Swimming, however, at certain times—say after some unripe fruit—may be very injurious, and yet the only recognised excuse is a doctor's certificate. Drill and gymnastics are repul- sive to certain natures, and are therefore harmful. Something such natures liked and took to of their own accord, especially if it earned money, would he in every way better and more serviceable. Then, too, the vari- ous sanitary arrangements and building by-laws prevent the erection of small cottages and cause overcrowd- ing [!] In fact, it is not too much to say that quite half of the national deterioration is the direct result of legislation and mischievous meddling. The forms of life and the conditions of life may be less healthy than formerly. Do not let us add less freedom as an additional evil. As a great bishop said, England free•is preferable to England sober. Freedom is preferable to over-regulation and over-sanitation. If we have get to go to'the wall, let us go to the wall our own way." "The true remedy for national insipidity is not forced military service, but the abolition of the workhouse and all such leprous institutions, the freedom of the people to:spend the money they earn as they like, the abolition of the State schools, and freedom for chil- dren to go to work as st,on as they desire to go." ," Compulsory service, too, would rob the profession of arms of its facination for the true soldier, and we I should lose the strongest weapon against the ambitious schemes of the universal arbitrationist, and the inter- national labour restrictionist."

The above extracts are not, as might be supposed,the inventions of a supporter of compulsory service to prove to what fatuous depths people can sink in their adoration of what they imagine to be freedom. They are genuine excrepts from two articles in the September number of the Liberty Review. We see hear the ravings of that type og mind which loathes and abhors dis- cipline, and law and order, even if it be for the protec- tion of defenceless women and children. In the name of freedom 'children should be allowed to work any number of hours that a drunken parent, or a ruthless employer, can impose upon their weakness! The ruffian and the hooligan should be allowed to indulge, without let or hindrance, in all the" inclinations of an artistic or inventive nature ! " For "actions that are pleasur- able prove beneficial to the owner's general organism." How happy then, the young scoundrel who can kick or batter some woman or old man in the street! Let us not interfere with this free development of " an inventive nature "let us rather nurture the artistic instinct which finds vent in such pleasing forms. Swimming, too, we are told, is "at certain times—say after some unripe fruit—very injurious." Let no child therefore. be compelled to learn to swim. Let him rather be drowned freely, and in his own way, when occasion arises. And if any man be so foolish as to try to save him,, why, obviously he is trying to compel him to live, and so will deserve to be drowned with him—

thus exemplifying in a very beautiful manner the way Nature avenges any interference with the sanctity of individual liberty. Drill and gymnastics,' we are interested to hear, "are repulsive to certain natures, and are therefore harmless "—aote the "there- fore." So, we believe, are soap and water, air, decency, truthfulness, self-restraint, and all the qualities which distinguish man from the beast. But, in the sacred name of liberty, let us see that no compulsion is used to secure the safety and comfort of the community, and at least decency and cleanliness, from such natures ; for "compulsion is aLways wrong." I We are grateful to the "Liberty Review" for pub- lishing these articles. Particularly are we pleased to find compulsory: education and compulsory rqilitary service damned in the same breath. Diem is much hope in this circumstance, which bears out our contention in another column, that the same forces which opposed compulsory education, and which have now, with the exception of a few lunatics, recognised the inestimable boon it has conferred upon the nation, are those watch now oppose compulsory military training, which is but the completion of civic education. We note, too, with gratitude, the conviction of one of the writers we have quoted, that compulsory service is the natural rout to universal arbitratiou—and should therefore be resisted. We had not thought to find this gleam of wisdom amid so much rubbish. But, fas et ah hoste, doceri—And we recommend this startlingly frank confirmation of our views, expressed elsewhere, to the notice of the Trades Unions and to all lovers of peace.—Prom the "National Service Journal."

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly ine3ting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians waR held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, when there were pre3ent M.essrs. A. R. Nortna.n (chairman), T. Baxter, T. Wright C. E. Liniker,Williaui Nforris S. Taylor, J. W. Timperley, T. Lee, J. Douglas, C. E. Cinaker, jun., C. Roylance, J. Hallows, J. Bolshaw, J. Pilling, Admiral Jones-Parry, J. L. Jones, J. Billinge, T. Antwis, J. Broome, H. M. Davies, J. Mountield, J. B. CAlier, J. Whitley, .T. Spruce, T. Eyes, J. Ff. Davies, J. Whitlow, J. Smith, E. Bretherton and W. Chimes, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (deputy clerk), J. Cunnah (master), T. Cooke, S. Riley, and W. R. DePrez (relieving ()facers). .

OFFICERS' SLRtES. CONTRIBUTIONS BY COUNTY COUNCILS.

The Clerk to the South Stoneham Union wrote call- ing attention to the provisions of the Local Govern- ment Act, 1838, section 26, sub-section 1, providing for the grant by the County Council of an annual sum for the costs of the officers of the Union which, until Parliament otherwise determined, was such sum as the Local Government Board, certified to have been ex- panded for the financial year ending the 25th March, 1888. The increase of population and work with the Union had led to increased salaries, now more than £4OOO in excess of the amount paid in 1888, while the grant remained unaltered and it is urged that a revision of the scale of payment should be brought about and ar further revision made every five years. They asked for the co-operation of other Unions in this matter. Mr. Patin; moved that the Board support the Stone- ham Union in their application to the Local Govern- ment Board for the revision of contributions by County Councils to the salaries of Union officials. Mr. Antrobns seconded and the motion was carried.

VALUATION BILL.

The Clerk read a letter from the secretary to the Conference of representatives of Boards of Guardians and Union Assessment Committees announcing, as the result of the conference, in opposi- tion to the Valuation Bill 1901, the Bill had not been proceeded with. It was deemed desirable that an adjourned conference should be held, at which the palicy of Boards of Guardians and Union Assess- ment Committees with respect to the reform of Valua- tion Law, should be carefully and fully considered and determined upon. The adjourned conference would be an important one and should be attended by representa- tives from all parts of England and Wales. The Chairman explained that the original Bill which it was proposed to introduce into the House of Commons had been dropped, and that no. new Bill had been re-introduced. He therefore thought it rather premature for them to do anything, as the next Bill might be entirely different from what was originally proposed. Mr. Pilling pointed out that the idea of the second Confereace was that the Boards of Guardians and Assess- ment Committees should draw up a Bill which, he was led to believe, if brought forward would receive atten- tion.

Hr. Baxter was unanimously appointed es a delegate from the Board to the adjourned conference.

APPOINTMENT OF A COOK.

Miss Ellen Caldwell was unanimously. appointed the Workhouse cook at a salary of £35 per annum with rations, washing &c.

NORTH WESTERN POOR LAW CONFERENCE.

Messrs, J. Mountield, 5.1. Roylance, and the clerk, delegates to the North Western Poor Law Conference, presented their report. They stated that the president in his opening address gave interesting statistics on pauperism and stated that there was an increase of persons in receipt of relief throughout England and Wales as compared with last year of 22,895. The Com- mittee appointed at the Law Conference to consider the question of combination and classification of work- houses submitted a report on the progress they had made. Papers were read on the position of Poor Law Unions and Guardians as affected by recent legislation on the position of poor law in relation to the fallen and on the advantage of training ships for poorlaw boys. Mr. Mounfield remarked that the Runcorn Union was the only union in Cheshire or Lancashire which was able to report a decrease in pauperism during the last year. (Hear, hear.) The report was adopted. THE EDUCATION OF THE CHILDREN. MR. NORMAN AND THE DUTIES OF THE BOARD. Mr. Spruce reported that the Pantasaph Orphanage Committee had visited the Orphanage and found every- thing in order. They were very pleased at the appear- ance of the children and surroundings. One thing which struck him was that a number of girls in the institution appeared capable of earning their own living and if they had been in the workhouse would have been put out to service. He thought the matter should be looked into and he did not think it fair or right that girls should be kept in the orphanage when they were old enough to go out to service. Mr. Collier said he was pleased to note the facilities which were afforded the girls of getting excellent situations in after life as a result of the admirable training in the Institution, many of the former scholars now occuping very good positions. Mr. Baxter remarked that the orphanage deserved support as there was no doubt it was doing a. good work in taking children out of the gutter and giving them an opportunity of learning a trade, but he must say he did not see any great improvement upon what was be- ing done for the children in the Workhouse. He thought the latter children were as well cared for in every way as they were in Mao Orphanage. Still the Orphanage was doing a great deal of good by taking children from the Workhouse, and the streets and edu- cating them. Mr. Roylance pointed out that a number of girls in the Orphanage paid their own expenses. He did not think there were any girls from their Union who remained in the Orphanage when they were old enough to work. Those who remained in the Orphanage after a certain age were children who were placed there and paid for by their parents. Mr. Collier reported upon the visit of the Buckley Hall Orphanage Committee to the Buckley Hall Orphan- age. In that Orphanage, he said, the boys received an excellent education which gave them a chance of occu- pying good positions in after life. He looked forward to the day when the Protestant children in the Work- house would enjoy the same advantages as the Roman , Catholic children. Sometimes the shoe was on the other foot, but in that particular instance it was the Protestant children who were at a disadvantage in not being allowed to go to such excellent institutions, (Hear, hear.) The Chairman remarked that when it was decided to send some of their children to the Buckley Hall and Pantasaph Ophanages it was thought that there were no such homes to which they could send Protestant children. They had however since found that there were Protestant orphanage's and he hoped they would soon get all their children into such institutions. (Hear, hear.) Seeing they were practically the parents of the children and responsible for their welfare he thought they should begin to work up to a high a standard as was possible. (Hear, hear) While he sympathised with the workingman who had to educate his own child and also contribute to the educa- tion of the children in the Union he felt that they were compelled to do all they could to give the workhouse children a good start in life by giving them a good education. (Hear, hear.) They would be very much to blame if they fixed the standard of living in the work- house on the lines of the standard of the poorest cottager. The same thing applied to the education of the children. Irk many respects the Runcorn Union was in the front in matters of progression but he thought they were behindhand in their education and training of the children. They must remember that it was their: duty to do all they could to relieve the children of the stain of pauperism. There was another'point which Mr. Spruce and Mr. Baxter lost sight of which was that it was their duty to lift the children into a higher plane , of life, and give them a chance of occupying good , , positions in after life. _ . -

Mr. Hallows said no one cared for children more than he did but he did not think it right that they should keep the children when they should be out working. The Chairman said he would be surprised to learn that they were paying for children who were old enough to work. He thought however that until the clerk had obtained information on the subject discussion would be premature.

CHILDREN'S COUGHS.

A WEIOLE FAMILY CURED BY VEND'S LIGEITNING COUGH CURE.

Mr. A. I)EICTEI, Waolesale Fish Merchant, LOWI3 s- to ft, writes —"My: children have suffered from bronchitis and bad colds at this time of the year for several', years, but, thanks to VENG'S LIGHTNING COUGH CURE they are much better and we very seldom hear them cough now. Warrington Motorist Fined.

At Shap Police— Court, on Monday, Thomas R. Garton, of Stockton Heath, Warrington, was charged with driving a motor car, on the 31st ult., without giving sufficient warning of his approach; he was also charged with driving negligently.—Dr. T. H. Gibson, Orton, a magistrate for Westmorland, said he was driving with three friends from Tebay to Orton, when the defendant's car dashed past him without any warning being given. The doctor's pointer dog, which was a short distance in front, was run over by the car and injured. Defendant admitted to witness that he was going eighteen miles an hour, but said he had no occasion to stop for a dog.— Defendant said he driving from Kendal to Appleby with Mr. Joseph Jenningi, Warrington, and they sounded the horn before rounding the bend. The car was under perfect contrll. Afterwards he told Dr. Gibson that he could not be held responsible for a dog, and if it was a valuable one it should have been undor proper control. Dr. Gibson said, "We have made one man pay £3O at Shap for running over a, dog, and we shah make you pay the same." De fendant added that he steered the car so that the body passed over the dog. He did not see that he should have tried to avoid the dog as to have done so would' have endangered the lives of the occupants of the car. Had a child been in the roadway he would have taken the risk.—The bench said they concluded the defendant did not sound hislorn, and so was guilty of negligence. He was certainly guilty of improper driving, because there was a steep incline. For negligently driving the de- fendant would be fined £2 and costs, and his licence would be endorsed. The other charges would be dismissed.

The defence of Kleptomania was made in the case of Ann Buckley, who pleaded guilty, and was bound over at Oldham on a charge of stealing toys from a penny bazaar." It was stated that her home was crowded with similar useless articles.

Vice-Admiral Douglas has hoisted his flag on H.M.S. Victory as commander-in-Chief at Portsmouth. The Colossus fired a salute of sixteen guns, to which the Hercules replied with eleven. Rear-Admiral Henderson has transferred his flag to the Asia.

A wide in-off into a top pocket. Red ball on a Spot inches from the side cushion, and 56# ißehes from the top cushion. Cue ball at the ex- treme end of the D. This in-off is on with side only with crystalate balls, though not with ivory

_ Diagram 18 shows a wide top pocket in-oft the red ball being situated on a spot which is 221 inches from the side cushion and 564 inches from the top cushion and the cue ball at the extreme end of the D. As in the previous example the in-off can be got with crystalate balls by means of a medium-pace stroke with plenty of running side. With ivory.balls such a stroke would not be possible, and in such a position the only way to get the in.-off would be by .means of a forcing stroke.

Long in-offs of this nature (when they can hl got by the use of side without having to resort to a forcing stroke) should be played with plenty of drag, and aim should be taken just a little fuller than half-ball to allow for the pull of the nap after the drag on the cue ball has been ex htn sted. The in-off shown on diagram 17 is an extreme case of what is possible with crystalate balls. Generally speaking, it would not be an easy stroke to play, as in all long slow strokes it i, somewhat difficult to estimate the combined effect of side and nap before the object ball li reached. To have made the in-off on diagram 1€ a little less wide would have made the stroke comparatively easy with crystalate balls, but it might then have been just on with ivory balls. To sum up, therefore, many a shot that is absolutely not on with ivory balls as a half ball stroke with side is quite possible with composi- tion balls, and many others that are only just on with ivory balls are comparatively easy with composition balls. Transmitted Side. ""'"1

The question as to whether it is pozisible to impart side to an object ball is a very vexed one with many amateurs, but it is an un- doubted fact, well known to professionals and good class amateurs, that under certain con- ditions side imparted to a cue ball is transmitted to the object ball, though of course in a very ranch reduced degree. Many players are, how- ever, under the impression that the amount of side which may be transmitted to an object ball, though very much lees than that imparted to the cue ball, is still a considerable quantity. This view of the question is, however, quite in- correct, for the amount of side which a cue ball by itself can transmit to an object ball can never be more than a minute quantity, and that this is ao I hope to prove conclusively. In the first place (as is well-known' side on a ball is another term for spin and when a ball carries a lot of side this spin or rotation is very marked indeed, and even when a ball carries only a comparatively small amount of side a certain amount Of spin is quite notice- able if the ball be travelling slowly and often the spin on the ball can be watched until it gradu- ally becomes exhausted and stops altogether altheugh the ball is still tr=eiVelling along the table.

Now,, were it possible to impan; a considerable amount of side to an Object ball, a certain amount of spin should be observable on an ob- ject ball whenever it was struck by a side-laden cue ball. What player, however, can say that he has ever seen an object ball rotafing with spin simply. as the result of being struck by a cue ball carrying a lot of side (an object ball may sometimes spin in a very noticeable mannet if it has been caught between the other two balk and squeezed out after having been practically held in a vice for a fractional part of a second, but this is of course an exceptional case and has no bearing whatever on. the question of transmitted side). As is well known the effect ,ol' side on a cue ball is most clearly shown when the ball strikes a cushion at a right angle and therefore it clearly follows that the easiest way to measure the amount of side that it is possible to impart to a cue ball, is to cause the object ball to hit a cushion at right angles after being struck by a cue ball carrying aide. The best method of play- ing such a stroke is to place a red and a white ball exactly on the baulk line, and a few inches apart as shown on Diagram 19.

A test for transmitted side. If two balls be Dlacei on the baulk line, and the cue ball be Alayed dead full on to the object ball so that tile latter travels exactly on the line, right hand side on the cue ball will impart slight left hand side to the .object ball and left hand side, slight right hand side.

Strike the cue ball with as much side as pos- sible, and play quite full on to the red (the baulk line will be a guide as to whether the ob- ject ball has been struck absolutely full or not). If the stroke be played from the left side of the table and the cue ball be struck with plenty of /ight hand side, the red ball will (provided it has been struck so truly that it keeps exactly on the baulk line all the way to the cushion) after striking .ithe cushion, rebound slightly out of baulk mare or less as shown by the continuous hue on the diagram. If instead the. cue—ball be r stuck with plenty of left.hand side (still-cilaying from the left side of the table), the red ball, pro- vided of course that it has been truly struck, will 'bound from the cushion slightly into baulk.

ST. PAUL'S CHURCH.

The thanksgiving services werecontinued in the above church on Sunday, and throughout the day were well attended. in the morning the preacher was the Rev. D. Morton, vicar of St. Paul's, Widnes, and in the evening the vicar occupied the pulpit. A well attended child- ren's servied in the afternoon was addressed by the Rev. T. W. Barber. At the men's service in the afternoon the Rev. R. H. Bowen addressed a large congregation. The collections for the whole of the services were on behalf of the endowment fund and arlized altogether £36 3s. 4d.

HILL CLIFFE BAPTIST CHAPEL. For the thanksgiving services which were held on Sunday, the interior of the above chapel had been beautifully decorated, and presented an exceptionally pleasing appearance. Those responsible for the work and to whom great credit is due were :—Mesdames Hughes, Dodd, T. F. Moss, Williams, King, Englands James Strange, jun., and V4ilkinson, and the Misses, Mather, Hallas, Jones, England, Anslow, Worrall, More ton, Dodd, Hall, M. Boucbierl and Bell, together with Messrs. Joseph Moss, sen., T. F. Moss, A. Arnold, W. Wheeler, G. S. and B. Moss. and James Strange, jun. Hughes) occupied the pulpit in the morning and evening, deliver- ing interesting addresses on each occasion. The after- noon service wasalmost entirely choral, and consisted of the rendering of the cantata" A Daughter of Moab" by the members of the Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church Choir. The principals were the Misses A. Bridge (Ruth), A. Woodland, A. Mansell, L. Stronach, of Earlestown (Naomi), and L. Snelling (Orpah), Mr. J. H. Corrin and Mr. E. H. Pearce (Boaz); conductor, Mr. A. Spruce, and organist, Mr. F. Podmore, MI.U.M. and their singing of the parts allotted to them was exceedingly good. Miller officiating as organists. On Monday evenicg a fruit social was held in the schoolroom, when there was again a good attendance. The refreshments and fruit were served by Mesdames Hughes, . F. Moss, and Wilkinson, and the Misses Mather, Williams,"ones, England, Dodd, and Worrall. Mr. James Strang jun., and Miss Bell favoured the company with songs. The sale of fruit and vegetables, of which there vvas a large quantity, proceeded under the direction of Mr. Birch, of Moore. Thanks were accorded to all who had helped to make the festival so great a success. The proceeds amounted to nearly 211 WYCLIFFE CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH.

The harvest thanksgiving services in connection with the above church were held on Sunday, the preacher both morning and evening being the Rev. John Yonge. In the morning he took for his subject "The feast of harvest" and in the evening" Our Lord's text at the harvest festival." During the course of his evening address he remarked that in 1897 some very remarkable discoveries were made in the east by some very learned investigators, who were searching among ancient ruins. They found among a pile of rubbish some stained and tattered papyrus, which had written upon it some sentences purporting to be records of some sayings of Jesus. Again, it February, 1903 some more documents of a similar kind bad been unearthed. But among those precious discoveries, all of which, like the new ones, were of the greatest possible interest, there was one in particular, which, viewed by the side of their present text, became ino.t remarkably striking and impressive. It ran thus: "Jesus says, 'I stood in the middle of the world and in flesh, I was seen of them, and I found all drunken, and no one thirsty among them. And my soul grieves over the sons of men because they are' blind in their heart and they do not see.'" They could certainly not decide whether that was the record of an actual saying by our Lord, but their atten- tion was especially drawn to it by the report of Dr. I Horton's recent Sunday evening's lecture to working men, of which he made those recently discovered words his text. Dr. Horton had remarked that while it was of course, impossible, to say whether these words were actually spoken by Jesus when he was upon earth, it did not seem impossible to say that they were spoken by Him at the present day. Their text that night presented the Lord Jesus as appealing to human souls who were thirsting for what this earth could not give. "If any man thirst let him come to Me and drink." But that new word of Jesus presented Him as grieving in His soul, because although He had stood in the middle of the world, and in flesh had been seen by men, yet they were not thirsting for the reall true eternal good. "He found no one thirsty among them." There were none who longed for the life that was true, none who cared supremely for the things of God, none who had any desire for the Infinite and the Eternal. Why, and how was this? "Because," said Jesus, "He found all drenken,"—intoxicated. with thel passing and the perishable, caring only for what this world could give, its gains, its Ambitions its pleasures. It seemed as if to the old penetrating eye of Jesus the greater part of mankind was given up to the temporal. Those who were success- ful in the pursuit, were indulging in nothing but the enjoyment of their gain, and those who had failed were still following after, with the insatiable passion of desire, all alike absorbed by the present, none caring or heeding about the future. "He found all drunken and no one thirsty among them." That newly-found word, which claimed to have been sometime uttered by Him, found only too palpable corroboration in common life to-day. He (the preachex) did not desire them to point to its only too frequent literal fulfilment. That was patent and universal enough, but there were many forms of intoxication,all of which were evil, and were keeping multitudes away from God. There was the intoxication of worldly ambition. Men were lost to everything which did not make for wealth. orplace or power. They would do anything or everything to achieve those obiects. They bent all their energy to them, gave all their thought, their ?lent, and their time to them. The passion for those thins made them utterly oblivious of all that was high and noble and spiritual. There was the intoxication of pleasure. Did they not see it on every hand ?—men basting afte excitements, after ever now sensations, going from one kind of excitement to another, always on the rush, here there, and everywhere. No time •for thought about life's Divine purpose. no desire to render service to men less favoured than themselves, n 1 care for an-ythinr except for the selfish, i!nd the transient, and the perishable. Dr. Horton. in his lecture, illustrated that, and among others, told of being a fellow traveller recently with a gentleman who began to talk to him about the game of golf. He expressed his extraordinary devotion to it, saying that golf was a game which made, a man neglect his health, his wife, his business, and his religion.lf that assertion had any considerable truth in it, and doubtless other recreations might be similarly spoken of, what did it mean? It meant that even educated, intelligent and religions people did sometimes become possessed of their pleasures. Things which were inno- cent and commendable in themselves came to exercise a tyranny over men. They became intoxicated with them. and had awakened in them a thirst which, ver demanded increasing draughts of the same intoxicant, until men could become lost to all worthy objects in life. During the day special anthems were rendered by the choir at the morning service Stainer's composition Ye shall dwell in the Land "and in the evening "Sing to the Lord of Harvest "(Barnby), and" The eyes of all wait upon Thee " (Elvey) being sung. Mr W. H. Payton presided at the organ. The collections, which were on behalf of the maintenance finid amounted to £23.

HANDICRAFT EXHIBITION AT MANCHESTER. The Clarion Guild of Handicraft Exhibition to be opened at. the Athemeum, Manchester, on October 28 by the Lord Mayor of Manchester, and on subsequent days by such well-known social reformers as the Duchess of Sutherland, the Countess of Warwick, Mr. Walter Crane, and Mr. Robert Blatchford, affords striking evidence of the growth of the Arts and Crafts movement in this country. Forn3ed three years ago, through the medium of the "Clarion " newspaper, the Clarion Guild has now extended largely, having branches in London, Liverpool. Manchester, Birmingham, Leeds, Newcastle, Bristol, Chester, and other large centres. Each branch works independently. but a joint Annual Exhibition is held of the work of all the branches. At the present exhibition, in addition to the work of the members, exhibits h ye been received from many of the first craftsmen in the country, as well as interesting and valuable examples of old work, so that it will form a collection not only interesting but of high educa- tional value.

LICENSED VICTUALLERS' DEFENCE LEAGUE.

Meeting at Warrington.

The half-yearly meeting of the No. 3 district of the Licensed Victuallers' National Defence League was held at the Lion Hotel, Warrington, on Wednesday, representatives attending from Ashton-under -Lyne, Barrow-in-Furness, Birkenhead, Blackburn, Blackpool, Bolton, Burnley, Bury, Chester, Crewe, Darwen, Dukin- field, Lancaster, Liverpool, Manchester, Macclesfield, Morecambe, Northwich, Oldham, Ormskirk, Preston, Rochdale, Southport, Stalybridge,Stockport, St. Helens, Warrington and Wigan. Alderman A. W. Siddall, J.P., of Ashton-under-Lyne, presided and the officials present included Councillor Isaac Turner, of Liverpool (vice-chairman), Councillor John Ingle, of Manchester (hon. treasurer), and Mr. Fred W. Cooper, of Manchester, (secretary).

HONOUR FOR ALDERMAN EVANS. The meeting passed a vote of condolence with the relatives of the late Mr. William Broadbent,of Oldham, who was an active member of the committee.

Voting then took place for the election of a represen- tative on the Council of the League in place of the late Mr. Broadbent. The result was as follows :—Alderman Evans, Warrington, 729; Mr. H. Rowley, Oldham, 715; Mr. McLeevy, Birkenhead, 215; Mr. Marshall Bardsley, Dukinfleld, 211; Mr. J. H. Knott, Bolton, 205. Alderman EVANS briefly returned thanks for the honour conferred upon him. Mr. HENRY ROWLEY, president of tha Oldham Licensed Victuallers' Association, and Mr. James Pax- ton, of Wigan, were elected members of the committee.

THE LICENSING ACT.

The CHAIRMAN said that at the last meeting of the District he expressed the hope that the Licensing Bill would be such a one as they might support, and that it would contain clauses dealing fairly with the mem- bers of the trade and giving adequate compensation to all persons interested in licensed property and to licencees for the loss of their licences through no fault of their own. He was pleased to say that his hopes had been fulfilled to a very great extent. Although the Bill when it was introduced did not exactly satisfy the trade, it had, through the tact and perseverance of the Licensed Victuallers' Joint Parliamentary Committee during the Parliamentary proceedings, been altered in many respects to their reasonable satisfaction. The tax on spirits which was put Oh in 1890 still con- tinued to be paid and produced £440,000 per annum. The tax imposed on beer and spirits as a war tax during the South African campaign was also still being paid thereby crippling the trade to a very serious extent. The new Licensing Act contained the high-sounding word compensation, but the money had to be found by the trade—they were, in fact, compensating themselves with their own money. That made a third tax imposed upon the trade. The time limit suggested during the progress of the Bill would have been ridiculous had it been passed. Suppose a clause had been inserted limit- ing the time to fourteen years it would not only have been unfair, but it would have been another method of robbing those connected with the trade. The Bill had now become an Act of Parliament, and it was the duties of the members of the League tb asssst in carrying out its provisions. If in the course of time it was found there was any injustice to thPm, there was no doubt that on a proper representation being made Parliament would be willing to rectify it.

===== 0002601_19041022_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
LONG PULL CONDEMNED,

The Preston and District Licensed Victuallers' Asso- ciation asked the district for an expression of opinion on the "long pull" system, and the Rochdale and Dis- trict Association asked the meeting to use its influence against the practice of giving "three-halfpenny pints" which had recently become prevalent in that town. Mr. RUSHWORTH, of Rochdale, asked the meeting to pass a resolution denouncing the sale of cheap beer and drawing the attention of magistrates to the matter. Councillor TURNER thought that in view of the altered conditions brought about by the Licensing Act it would be a very dangerous thing to approach the justices. The meeting adopted a resolution protesting against the long pull and cutting in prices and asking affiliated societies to do their utmost to prices, the practices.

THE ADMINISTRATION OF THE NEW ACT.

Councillor TURNER alluded to the remarks of Sir Horatio Lloyd at the Cheshire Quarter Sessions with reference to "setting the house in order" for the 'incoming of the new Act. The Act was almost a skeleton until it was clothed by the rules governing its administration which had to be arranged by the Home Secretary. He. thought repre- sentations should be made to the Home Secretary asking him to inform the trade at as early a date as possible what kind of rules would be adopted. He (Councillor Turner), did not see why one district simply because it had a different licencing policy from another should be allowed to form a separate area. Mr. McLEEvv, of birkenhead, thought every associa- tion should petition the Chancellor Of the Exchequer to relieve the trade of the war tax of 6d. per gallon on spirits and Is. per bartel on beer. The meeting closed with a vote of thanks to the Chairman, Alderman Evans, who proposed it, expressing regret that the Mayor was unable to welcome the delegates to Warrington.

WARRINGTON CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

Piano for the Workhouse Inmates.

The quarterly meeting of the Warrington Co-opera- tive Society was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sanke,- street, on Tuesday evening, Mr. Birchen presiding over a large attendance.

===== 0002601_19041022_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. S. GOUGH THREATENS LITIGATION.

Mr. RATHBONE reported that Mr. S. Gough had written threatening to take proceedings against the Society if they persisted in the erection of the addi- tional buildings at their Winwick-street branch. Mr. Gough contended that the extention of the premises was an infringement of the light and air of the back portion of his property. He also contended that it in- fringed the light and air of a number of cottages. Mr. Rathbone pointed out that with regard to the cottages the windows did not face the Society's premises but faced Mr. Gough 's own premises. Mr. Rathbone further reported that the matter had been taken up with Mr. Gough but that nothing futher had transpired.

THE QUARTER'S PROGRESS.

The quarterly balance sheet showed that as com- pared with the corresponding quarter of last year tho sales had increased by £2,000. Mr. BIRCHALL remarked that the sales during certain weeks in the quarter were the largest on record in con- nection with the society. The balance sheet was adopted. PIANO PRESENTED TO WORKHOUSE INMATES..

Mr. RAntuo:cr...reic,Or-t-ed that the committee had re- ceived a request from Mrs. Clay, a lady member of the Warrington Board of Guardians that the Society should present a piano to the inmates of the Warrington workhouse. Mrs. Clay pointed out that a musical in- strument was greatly needed. Mr. BIRCHALL remarked that the commiteee felt that the provision of a piano as suggested would supply a long felt want and would do a great deal towards the brightening of tho lives of the inmates of the Work- house. - -

Mr. W. J. SHAw endorsed Mr. Birchall's remarks,and on the question being put to the meeting it was unani- mously decided to purchase a piano for presentation to the inmates of the Workhouse.

ELECTION OF COMMITTEE MAN.

Mr. Malone-;;;;bia-larg; majority elected to fill the vacancy on the committee caused by the resignation of Mr. J. W. Bouchier.

Mr. F. MONKS. whose presence was heartily welcomed alluded to the completion orMr. Rathbone's 40th year of service to the society, and said be would like to add his testimony to the value of his services during that time. The committee of the society were like a Board of Directors, and were of great value, but unless they were backed up by a capable manager they would be of little value. He had known Mr. Rathbone for a con- siderable number of years, and he had never met anyone who possessed a greater sense of integrity and upright- ness,or one whose character would beam a closer investi- gation. He suggested that, as Mr. Ratbbone bad com- pleted 40 years of service to the society, his work should be recognized in a substantial manner.

Mr. HAIGH proposed that the matter of the proposed testimonial be left in the hands of the committee. This was seconded and heartily carried. _

Mr. RATHRONE thanked the meeting for their kind- ness, but expressed a hope that the question of the testi- monial would be left over until he had actually severed his connection with the society. He felt he could not continue in office many years longer.

Church Sunday School Teachers' Association. The quarterly meeting of the Warrington and Dis- trict Church Sunday School Teachers' Association was held in St. Peter's School, John-street, on Monday evening. The Rev. B. Percival presided over a large attendance of tetrchers. A paper was read 14y Mr. T. E. Gibson upon the position of teachers in church,and a suggested phase of their work was given. The leading idea suggested by Mr. Gibson was that -necially selected and recognized teachers should take charge of young people, after passing the classes held by the clergy for preparing for 'confirmation ; and also for the banding together of the adult members of the Church. This, the speaker contended, would en- able a much larger use to be made of the laity in con- nection with the Church, would tend to retain those who had been confirmed in communion with the Church, and also assist in providing a larger body of lay workers. The Rector, and the Revs. E. C. E. Carleton, James, Davies and others, most favourably commented upon Mr. Gibson's paper for which he was thanked.

WARRINGTON MANUFACTURER FAVOURS GARDEN CITIES.

linoler the heading " Day to Day in Liverpool " the following notes appear in Wednesday's " Liverpool Mercury ":—Mr. A. H. 'Lee, who was present on the platform at the P.S.A. demonstration in the Philhar- monic Hall, the other evening, is a Warrington manu- facturer, who escapes each evening from the smoke of that busy manufacturing town for the more salubrious air in West Kirby, where his home is situated. Mr. Lee's father is a leading commercial man in Manchester and at one time sat in the House of Commons in the Liberal interest as one of the members for Southamp- ton. Politics do not seem to have the same attraction for the son: but he is deeply interested in religious and philanthropic movements. At Warrington, Mr. A. H. Lee has taken a prominent part in establishing a local branch of the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, while he was the first to provide poor boys with a fortnight's holiday at the seaside. He introduced the tapestry industry into Warrington. Following in the wake of the Morris school, his productions are characterised by a harmonious blending of colours, besides being artistic in design. Mr. Lee views with a favourable eye 'the Garden City movement. As a result the people of Warrington are hoping that this enthusiasm will not lead him to transfer his manufactory to any other centre, however beautiful its boulevards and surroundings. If Mr. Lee thinks well to remove his factory, he would find a welcome for such an enterprise either in Liverpool or in Birkenhead. At Aintree, he would find a neighbour of like mine in Mr. NV. P. Hartley.

THE NOtinaT' ADING

Review of the evidence by Mr. S. Norbury Wifflanis

Councillors Pemberton and Horrocks.

Direct and Indirect Trading detailed

and condemned.

===== 0002601_19041022_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
REMARKABLE DISCLOSURES.

with the Corporation during the period covered by the The report of the three Commissioners appointed by inquiry the Municipal Non-Tracing Association to inquire into all trading between past and present members of the Council or officials of the Corporation with the Corporation, either direct or indirect, was presented to His Worship the nayor yesterday. Its production 1 has involved an enormous amount of labotir and no time has been lost in completing the report so that it might be placed before the ratepayers. We append herewith an almost verbatim account of the report which runs as follows :

£ s . c:>. Arpley Chemical Co. ........... —..... 7 7 11 Artingstall and Co. BO 7 4 'Buckley and Co. 'Castle Rubber Co 30919 9 Collins Green Colliery Co. Warrington Express Billposting Co 43 0 8 Warrington Fish and Game Co 3l 15 11 Warrington Syndicate Warrington Cafe Alderman J. Shaw Green.

Gearre e la EN,— HEREWITH WE BEG TO PRESENT OUR REPORT. The Inquiry which has just been conchuled has been the outcome of a series of public meetings held in Warrington, -each of which has been very largely attended. At these meetings, and in communication to the Press, demands were repeatedly made for an inquiry into the whole of the trading which had been carried on between members of the Town Council or the companies with which they were associated, and the Council itself. Sound public policy has always dictated that there should be no personal interest accruing to public repre- sentatives as a result 'of 'their membership of public bodies. The House of Commons, in reference to the conduct of its own members, is scrupulously particular in this zegard, and the Municipal Corporations' Act of 1835, and the amended Act of 1882, have specifically enacted that trading, or the receiving of profit by mem- bers of Corporations, -shall render such members in- eligible to hold office. It is true that the Act of 1882 contains clauses which enact, amougst otherthings, that trading with limited liability companies shall not disqualify members of Borough Councils holding-shares in such companies; but the people of Warrington have been convinced that 'the trading carried on for years between the Council smLcompanies in which a number of its members are largely intere,sted,although it may not have been illegal has been so extensive and systematic, and has so seriously demorafised public life there, that the present Inquiry has been Tendered absolutely necessary. At the public meetings to which reference hat already been made, the speakers have referred time after time to the trading which has been carried on between limited liability companies ill the town and the Cor- poration itself. In the course of these meetings, ten or more of such companies have been specifically named. The members of the Town Coencil connected with such companies have also been named, and it is largely asn result of these etatements that this Inquiry wasi de- manded. For months the Town Council steadily and repeatedly opposed the bedding of the inquirv, and it was only by force of outside public opinion thitt the Council was 'driven to assent to such an inquiry., Ultimately it consented to the appointment of three members of the Council and of three members of the Non-Trading Association who should act as Comnfissioners and hold the Inquiry. After certain preliminaries, the terms of the inquiry 'were settled as 'follows : ' That an Inquiry beheldieto all Trading by past and present members of the Council, or officials of the Corporation, with -the Corporation, either direct or indirect, through limited companies, cr officials of such companies, or as contractors or sub-con- tractors,cr any dealings or transactions of any kind whatever between members of the Town Council or officials of the Corporation and the Corporation. That the period of Inquiry shall be from the Ist April. 18P6, to 31st March, 1903." Under these conditions the Commissioners have held ,69 sittings, extending from September 16,1203, to Sep- tember 5, 1904. The three Council Commissioners— Messrs. T. Birchall, J.P., A. J. Jolley, J.P., and L. Greening—were members of the Inquiry Committee up -to the 56th Sitting, held en July 21,1903,and on the 29th July these tksee gentlemen:waned their Report. In our opinion there were at that time so many points that had not been fully inquired into, that the action of our colleagues, in closing the Inquiry, was a matter of great surprise to us. We were and are emphatically .of opinion -that this action on the part of our colleagues was, to say the least, premature. To prove that it was so, we need only point to their very meagre report. Our definiteinstructions were to inquire Into the Trad- ing, both direct and 'indirect, and yet in the Report published on the 29th of ffnly by our three colleagues, no allusion is made to that most serious item of indirect trading on the part of many individuals and companies who have been engaged in •It. This was one half, and probably the more important half, of our work, and yet our colleagues make no reference to it in their Report. On this point, of course, we dissent from their Report. We forwarded a protest to the Mayor of the Borough against this sudden, unexpected, and we may almost say, unpardonable closing of the Inquiry. The matter was then laid by His Worship before the Council, and the Council decided unanimously that the Inquiry should be continued. Our three colleagues refused to take any further part in the Inquiry, and three new Commissioners—Councillors James Evans, Arthur Peacock, and John Wilkinson, J.l).—were appointed in tkeir stead. These gentleman have sat with us during the 'last 13 sittings. 'The points which we have to report as coming out, in our opinion, most definitely are these :- 1) That the trading, both direct and indirect, which had been alleged at the public meetings in ques- tion, has been amply proved. We include in this Report a list of the persons and the companies with whom we find that this trading has been carried on. We are convinced, from the evidence that has been laid before us, that this network of trading has demoralised the municipal government of the town. The opinions and acts of both officials and members of the Corpora- tion have, as a consequence of it, become-loose and unsatisfactory, and a condition of things has been pro-j duced which has been of infinite harm to Warrington,' and which, if it prevailed on the same scale throughout the boroughs of the country, would render municipal government a byword and a scandal. (2) We have to complain—and to complain most seriously—that the burgesses who knew most, about the trading which has been carried on, many of whom ha-I themselves been engaged in it, and upon whom we have called to give' us evidence, have, almost without exception, reneged to hlp us. This we con- sider a most serious phase of this Inquiry. The help of those who knew most has been steadily withheld from us, and as a result we have found it most difficult to ascertain anything more than a small portion of the facts which would have been brought out, had the assistance in queetion been rendered to us. This fact, we &milder, shows a lamentable absence of public spirit, and reflects great discredit upon all the persons concerned. (3) The ignorance of the officials, as shown throughout their examinations, isastounding. Had this been a Corporation Inquiry, and had the officials been instructed to state all they knew, there is no doubt that they could have given full information. But in our case, such is the condition of affairs in Warrington, and such, in our opinion, is the repugnance of the Council as a whole to this Inquiry, that it has been most difficult to extract information from the gentle- men in question. The part played by them in this Inquiry is, in our judgment, a discredit to them. We have appended extracts from their evidence in order to show the "ignorance" of which we complain. We agree with the finding of our three colleagues in the five specific cases upon which they have reported, and we find that the following members of the Council and officials have traded either directly or indirectly with the Corporation within the seven years embraced in our instructions : List of Members of Town Council and Officials of the Corporation, from April let, 1896, to March 31st, 1903, who were shareholders in limited companies trading with the Corporation : Bennett, Arthur Bolton, William Brough, Walter Burton, Thomas Cannell, James Crosfield. John Davies, Percy Fairclough. James Forshaw, W. J. Francomb. R. W. Gomel', J. H. Gornall, J. G. Green, J. Shaw Greenall, Edward Greening, Linmeus Haddock, W.:I Many of the cases are trivial, and are-included here solely for the purpose of carrying out our instructions; but we have dealt at length, in the body of our Report, with such cases as appear to us serious, and to call for the condemnation of the burgesses of Warrington. We hear add a list of the Limited Companies in which members or officials of the Town Council were interested with the amount of the transactions of each Company

117 15 6

We find that this gentleman (since dead) was a share- holder in, and also secretary of Jolla Buckley and Co., Ltd., carting contractors, which did business with the Corporation in each of the seven years in question. We find that in the two years 1898-9-1900-1 there was no competition for the business which was given to them. The seven years' trading amounted to £5,430. We have no evidence as to why there was no competition, but we hold-strongly that it was contrary to the best interests of the town that a prominent member of the: Corporation should be a considerable shareholder in and the secretary of a business concern trading so largely with the Corporation of which he was a member, and, , for along period, chairman of its Finance Committee. Mr. Green was also a director of the Warrington and District Billposting Company, Ltd., in which Aldeeman Sutton was also a shareholder. This latter company likewise did business with the Corporation during each of the seven years. We further find that Mr. Shaw Green received £lO 10s. on July 21st, 1900, for negotia- tions carried on by him in reference to an exchange of land with the Corporation, and, what makes the case worse, we find that Mr. Green was Deputy-Chairman of the Committee on whose behalf the negotiations were carried on, and.from which he received this payment. The Committee had appointed Mr. Green along with Mr. Robert Davies, solicitor, to negotiate. This sum was paid to him by a cheque of the Corporation. This transaction we regard as reprehensible. No official ought to have been a party to it, and no member of the Corporation ought to have agreed to the cheque being issued. - - - -

We find that Alderman Francomb, Dr. J. ===== 0002601_19041022_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
company. Alderman Francomb and the Silk Case.

- -- In the year 1900 Mr. Richard Silk was a clerk in the employ of Alderman Franc,omb, and between August and December the Corporation paid to Silk sums of about £521 for cannel supplied to the Gas Committee. A Committee was appointed by the Town Council on May 24th. 1901. to inquire into the circumstances under which those payments were made. This Committee received the evidence of several witnesses, and after- wards reported that Silk was during the whole period a servant of Alderman Francomb, and that the cheques paid to Silk by the Corporation for this cannel were afterwards endorsed by him and paid into Francomb's bank account. We have seen those cheques. We have asked Messrs. Francomb and Silk to appear before us. Both of them have refused to come. Mr. Haddock, the gas manager, was found by this committee to have given a verbal order for this coal without the knowledge of the Gas Committee. We called Mr. Haddock, who gave us the following -striking evidence :- 2256 Mr. Williams: Can You explain briefly as to the coal that was received through Mr. Richard Silk. Who gave the order for that stuff ? A. I did. 2257 Q. Did you give it to Richard Silk? A. No. 2258 Q. To whom did you give the order? A. To Mr. Fmncomb. 2259 Q. He was a member of the Council? A. Yes but it was not given to him. He said he was not 'doing business; he had transferred his business. I asked that question. I said we could not give him the order if he came as an Alderman, and he said he had transferred his business to Silk. It is some cannel. :they might have it or they might not. Sometimes you can get it and sometimes you cannot. It was just at the time we were short of cannel. They had bad a failure in the colliery we were dealing with—Turner's Colliery. They had had some falling 'in of the shaft and we could not get our cannel from them, and we were very glad to have an opportunity of taking an odd lot. 2260 Q. Tell me how this came about? A. You have the shorthand notes taken at the time. 2261 Q. But they are not full enough for our satisfaction. A. It is such a long time since, I ' have a difficulty in remembering exactly. 3262 Q. Alderman Francomb cams to you aid mentioned this stuff? A. Yes. 2263 Q. And told you this stuff was in existence and he could let you have it? A. Yes. 2264. Q. I said we could not deal with him for it directly, and he said he had no interest in it whatever. He had transferredhis business to Silk, and Silk would do the business. He had merely asked 'on Silk's behalf. 2268 Mr. Pemberton: That vvas after you pointed it out? A. After I mentioned it. 2269 Mr. Williams: He practically said to you, "I am simply canvassing for a man named Silk"? A. Yes. 2270 Mr. Pemberton: After Mr. Haddock had pointed out he could not trade, being an Alderman. 2271 Mr. Williams: You thought the transac- tion was perfectly square and straight? A. The transaction from our point of view was very straight. because we got the cannel at our own price. 2272 Q. You got the stuff you wanted? A.
