" Alderman F. W. Monks. According to the figures contained in the Corporation books, the total trade done with Messrs. Monks Hall, and Co., Ltd., was'only £9 12s. 9d. in the seven years We called Mr. Haddock, the gas manage; 'before 'us, and inquired of him on this point. We • append passages from this evidence :- 6088 Mr. Pemberton: You have bought a great many tubes, Mr. Haddock, have you not ? A. Yes, we have bought tubes from Caldwell's. 6089 Q. Who are the makers? Hall, and Co. 6090 Q. Was not Mr. Monks a member of this Council? A. Yes, he was a member of the Council; but I do not know that that has anything to do with it.' 6091 Q. That is what we call indirect trading. We do not want you to give us any opinion, but to tell us, as far as you know, of any goods that have been brought directly or indirectly into your department in that way? A. Ido not remember any. We know they have come from Mr. Monks because they have been delivered by their lurries. 6092 Mr. Williams: In Mr. Monks' own lurries? A. Yes. 6093 Mr. Pemberton: That is, as far as you know? A. Of course in that case of Mr. Monks it is, but whether he is a member of the firm Ido not know. 6094 Mr. Williams: At any rate that you have seen. You knew, first of all, that Mr. Alderman Monks was a member of the Council, and you have seen his carts? A. No, not that I have seen his. 6095 Q. Yes, the carts of Monks, Hall, and Co. go into your works with tubes? A. Yes. 6133 Mr. Peacock: How long have you been buying tubes for the Corporation? A. Ido not know when we began to buy. Soon after Monks and Hall commenced to make, because they quoted a lower price than the ring price at the time; we bought odd lengths just to satisfy our require- ments; but we kept on with Russell's. From these passages it would appear that the figure given above (£9 12s. 9d.) isibut a very small portion of the trading done by Messrs. Monks, Hall, and Co. It is evident that the amount done indirectly with Messrs. Monks was a serious item, but we are unable to state it in figures. We find that the total trade done by tfie Corporation with Messrs. Caldwells in the seven years was £8,562, but what exact portion of this was supplied by Messrs. Monks we are unable to report. We are of opinion that the fact that Messrs. Monks sent their goods on their own carts direct into the Corporation Gasworks, and that the officials were aware that Alder- man Monks was a member of the Corporation, presents emost unsatisfactory and demoralising condition of things. It is, in our view, contrary to sound public policy that materials of any sort should be supplied to Corporations by firms in which members of the Corporation are largely interested, seeing that those who have to receive and "pass " the goods are practically their own servants, and natur- ally would find it the difficult to refuse or reject.the materials s ipplied oy those whom they regard as their " masters."

We have carefully investigated nine specific cases in which a large quantity of bricks has been required in works carried out for the Corporation. We have beep surprised to find that, in each of these cases, although various other bricks have been definitely specified bik five architects and four officials, the invariable result has b3en that the bricks actually used have been those known as Greengate, for which the Warrington Slate Company,Ltd., are the sole agents in Warrington. We do not profess to be able to explain this remarkable change, but would impress on the ratepayers the advisability of their carefully perusing the whole of the evidence. They will then be able to grasp more clearly how this matter has worked. We have evidence that in one case alone, where four tendering firms were asked to give their prices for local bricks and for Greengate bricks respectively, it was found that they would charge for Greengate bricks £375, £3OO, £390, and £430 more than their price for common or local bricks. Seeing that this marked difference in price exists, and that Greengate are so much more expensive, we have to report that we have not been able to satisfy ourselves as to where the money has come from to pay the difference in the numerous cases where Greengate bricks have been substituted for the cheaper qualities. We had great difficulty in ascertaining who were the agents for these Greengate bricks. The officials seemed entirely ignorant on the subject, and the answer to the question was at last supplied, to our surprise by one of our colleagues. The following passage is taken from the evidence of Mr. Longdin. 5820 Mr. Pemberton: No one wishes you to do that Mr. Longdin. There are thousands of Green- gate bricks used in Warrington are there not? A. Yes. 5821 Q. Do you know who has the agency for them? A. Ido not. 5822 Q. Do you know who generally supplies them, then? A. No, I cannot say as ,to that. 5823 Q. Could you get them from Clare and Ridgway's? A. Ido not know whether you could or not. 5824 Mr. Evans You have had no direct orders yourself? A. No. 5825 Mr. Pemberton: It is not within your knowledge to say who is the agent for Greengate bricks in Warrington? A. No, it is not. 5826 Q. You have been here all these years- -27 years, I think you said—and rat you do not know who is the agent in Warrington for these bricks? A. No, Tdo not. 5827 Mr. Wilkinson: Will you ask me that question, Mr. Pemberton? 5828 Mr. Pemberton: No, I shall not. 5829 Mr. Wilkinson: Because I can tell you at once. It is the Warrington Slate Company. 5830 Mr. A. Peacock: I did not know that. I know that they are agents here, but I could not say what for, and yet I have bought bricks a great many times. 5831 Mr. Pemberton: There are half-a-dozen bricks in St. Helens that no person living could tell one from the other. They are made from the same material. 5832 Mr. Williams: Well, now, we have this information from Mr. Wilkinson that the sole agency in Warrington for the Greengate bricks is the Warrington Slate Company. 5833 Mr. Wilkinson: Yes, the Warrington Slate Company. Every little boy in Warrington knows that. 5834 Mr. A. It has nothing whatever to do with the Inquiry. WARRINGTON SLATE COMPANY.—TRAMWAYS SUB-CONTRACTORS.

The total of the 15 contracts named amounts to £171,476. In every one of these cases the. Warrington Slate Company, as sub-contractors, supplied materials in large quantities. We give later on a number of instances showing how this information has had to be positively dragged out of these gentlemen. WARRINGTON SLATE COMPANY—CEMENT.

In further illustration of the difficulty experienced by us in arriving at correct figures of sub-contracting, we may instance a case in which we requested Mr. C. W. Davenport to give us the value of materials supplied to him on six of his large contracts. This gentleman sent us the particulars, one of the cases reading thus,:—

HILL WARREN RESERVOIR : Total of the Contract £6748 0 0 Materials for this Contract sup- plied by Warrington Slate Co. £52 10 0 Having accidentally learnt that Mr. J. B. Johnson, of Liverpool, was a sub-contractor on this work, we in- vited him to give evidence, and were surprised to learn from him that, in addition to the £52 10s. received by the Warrington Slate Campany from the contractor (Mr. Davenport) he had himself paid the Slate Com- pany for cement alone, supplied to him as one of the sub-contractors upon this job, the sum of £670 14s. 4d. Another typical cement case is that of Mr. James; Lawson, the contractor for the well-sinking and adit- driving at Delph-lane. We heard of him, again by pure accident, and then wrote requesting him to attend and-give evidence "in reference to sub-contracts arising out of your contracts with the Warrington Corporation. He did not attend, but wrote as follows : • Water Works, Croft, Near Warrington. May 4th, 1904. To the Commissioners, Trading Inquiry, Town Hall, Warrington. Gentlemen,—l have yours of the 3rd instant, and in reply to same beg to say there are no sub- contracts in connection with the works here.— Yows truly, JA BIES LAWSON. We wrote again to this gentleman, and he replied thus : Water Works, Cioft, Near Warrington. May 6th, 1904. The Commissioners, Trading Inquiry, Town Hall, Warrington. Gentlemen,—l have yours of the sth inst. The amount paid to the Warrington Slate Company for materials is .£649.—Yours truly, JAMES LAWSON.

We would call special attention to the evidence of Mr. Daniel Cooper, brick manufacturer, of Woolston. There has been no legal work since. We find that this company obtained the order from the Corporation for painting the Town Hall, although their tender was £3O higher than thpt of Mr. F'. J. Dell. Warrington Fish and Game Company, Ltd.

Of this company, Councillors William Roberts and F. R. golferts and Alderman Henry Roberts were share- holders. Messrs. W. and F. R. Roberts were direc tors. The company traded with the Corporation, and we find that Alderman H. Roberts, a shareholder in the company signed as chairman of the Health Committee,on behalf of the Corporation, one of its invoices. We have already pointed out how very pernicious is this practice of act- ing in two capacities ; in fact, in being both seller and buyer. This company began to trade with the Corpora- tion five months after its registration, but the trading ceased on larch 29th, 1901 Councillor W. Roberts was the paid secretary of the company, and Councillor F. R. Roberts was its solicitor. The trading teas with the Hospitals, and was necessarily on a small scale.

===== 0002601_19041022_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
CONTRACT.

As indicating the serious laxity which obtained in the general administration of the affairs of the Borough at the time of the public unrest which culminated in the outbreak of the agitation in 1901, the history of the occurences which led up to the letting of the Tramways Permanent Way Contrast is of the gravest import,and lurid light is thereby cast upon the conduct of some of the parties then responsible for the government of Warrington. The contract, was for the laying of the permanent way for the first section of the Warrington Tramways, and was ultimately let to Mr. J. A. Ewart. In the printed notices sent to the firms who proposed to tender, the following clause was included: (3) The names of the manufacturers of any portion of the material not made by the firm tendering must be given; also the names of the quarry from which it is proposed to obtain the paving setts." In another printed form supplied to the firms tendering, appears the following clause (also numbered 3) : "The names of the manu- facturers of any portion of the works not made by the firm tendering must be given." The tenders were to be delivered at or before 12 o'clock noon on Wednesday March 27th, 1901. Ten tenders were received,and they were opened by the then Mayor, Alderman lienyy Roberts, as is proved by the following letter from the Town Clerk to Messrs, Preece and Cardew, dated March 37th, 1901:

WarrinAton Tramways. Dear Sirs,—l have dispatched by the London and North-western Railway this afternoon all the tenders and plans received by me. The tenders have been opened and initialled by the Mayor in accordance with the Standing Orders of the Council. Yours faithfully, (Signed) J. LYON WII4TTLE. Mr. Henry Roberts has denied that he opened these tenders (in his letter to the Commissioners dated June 23rd last), but the above letter of the Town Clerk is conclusive on the point. Mr. Roberts not only opened the tenders, but he was the only member of the Com- mittee or of the Council who was present when they were opened. A short time afterwards a detailed account of the occurrences connected with the opening of the tenders was given by Mr. J. A. Ewart to Coun- cillor %V. J. Forshaw and Mr. James Dodds, who together heard his narrative in the railway train between War- rington and Liverpool. Mr. Ewart has been invited to attend before the Commissioners and give evidence in regard to his then grievances, and generally, but he has deqlined to do so. Under the eircumstances, and in view of the seriousness of the allegations which Mr. Ewart made to him and Councillor Forshaw, Mr. Dodds, on pressure from Messrs. Birchall and Greening, members of this Com- mission, consented to attend before the Commission and repeat what Mr. Ewart had stated. Before attend- ing, Mr. Dodds called by appointment upon Councillor Forshaw, accompanied by a witness (a member of this Commission), and read to Councillor Forshaw the evid- ence (in regard to the Ewart contract) which he pro- posed to give. Councillor Forshaw confirmed the statements as read by Mr. Dodds (in the presence of a member of this Commission). He accepted from Mr. Dodds a press copy of this statement, and undertook, if called upon, to confirm Mr. Dodds' evidence. There- upon Mr. Dodds attended the next meeting of the Com- missioners, and gave the evidence as confirmed by Councillor Forshaw. In consequence of the serious nature of Mr. Ewart 's charges as made to Messrs. Dodds and Forshaw, it was decided that a copy of the evidence of Mr. Dodds should be sent to Mr. Ewart. and to three of the gentlemen named by Mr. Ewart, namely, ex-Aldermen F. W. Monks and Henry Roberts (who had both before declined to attend and give evidence), and Mr. H. G. Roberts, with an invitation to each of them to favour the Commissioners with any comments they might desire to make. They all replied by letter, and more or less denied the accuracy of the statement of Mr. Ewart as related by Mr. Dodds. On receipt of their replies, copies of the same were sent to Mr. Dodds, together with a copy, for , the first time, of his own evidence. From the latter he I discovered that he had by inadvertence wrongly given the date of an important letter written by the Warring- ton Slate Company, and shown to him in the railway train by Mr. Ewart. Apart from this error, which did not affect Mr. Ewart's st9.tement, Mr. Dodds, in a rejoinder dated the 6th July, 1904, adhered to every detail of his evidence. Copies of this rejoinder were sent to Mr. Ewart, Mr. F. W. Monks, and Mr. H. Roberts, with the remarkable effect that, although every attempt had been made to discredit Mr. Dodds, the net result has been to completely establish every essential link in the chain of his evidence, which is shortly : (1) That on March 27th, 1901, Mr. Henry Boberts, whilst the Mayor and an Alderman of the Borough, opened the tenders for the Tramways Permanent Way, no other member of the Committee or Council being present. We cannot find any recorded minute authoris-

ing such opening. (2) That on March 28th, 1901 (the next day), the Warrington Slate Company, Ltd., wrote a letter to Walter Scott, Ltd., Leeds Steel Works, Leeds. who had tendered as sub-tenderers, to Mr. Ewart and several other of the chief tendering contractors, five days before, asking for their very best merchant terms for steel rails to specification, the quantity tevired being about 750 tons,and these being for the Warrington Cor- poration Tramways. (3) That on March 30th, 1901, three days after the opening of the Tramways tender by his father, Mr. Gordon Roberts waited upon Mr. Ewart, as he himself says, by appointment. According to his letter to the Commissioners, dated June 23rd, 1904, the Slate Com- pany had furnished, some weeks before, quotations for various materials to Mr. Ewart and a number of other intending contractors, and his interview of March 30th, 1901, had reference to these quotations. From Mr. Ewart's statement to Messrs. Dodds and Forshaw, the object of the visit of Mr. Gordon Roberts to Mr. Ewart (who did not then know the tenders had been opened, nor that his tender was the lowest) was to induce Mr. Ewart to substitute the materials of the Slate Company for those of the firms whose names and goods Mr.. Ewart had included in his tender as required by the terms of the Corporation specification under which he had tendered. It appears to your Commissioners that unless Mr. Gordon Roberts knew that Mr. Ewart had not included the name of the Slate Company nor their goods in his tender, he could not possibly have any other object in waiting upon Mr. Ewart than that stated by the latter. Your Commissioners need not _point out the gravity of these facts. That the Mayor, whose son, Mr. Gordon Roberts, has admitted that the Warrington Slate Com- pany were among the sub-tenderers, should have opened these tenders, and that he should have opened them when no other member of the Council or of the res- ponsible Committee was present, was highly improper. That while as Mayor of the town, and Chairman of the Committee, he was in honour bound to rigidly protect, each of the tendering contractors and their sub-tender- ers as against each of the remaining contractors and sub-contractors, his company should the very next day after that on which he had opened the tenders, be writ- ing to the sub-tenderers of several of the tenderers, with a view to his own and his firm's advantage, was most reprehensible. That in addition to all this, his son. Mr. Gordon Roberts, should, two days later, by his own admission, be interviewing Mr. Ewart (who did not then knew the tenders had been opened or that his tender was the lowest) with the object of securing for the Slate Company the subtitution of their materials for those of other firms namesi by Mr. Ewart in his tender, is so serious, in our opinion, as to be absolutely indefensible. The whole of the proceedings in connec- tion with this Ewart contract are such as nothing can excuse.

===== 0002601_19041022_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
Councillor W. J. Forshaw.

This gentleman affords a striking and regrettable in- stance of the difficulties we have met with from the beginning in obtaining evidence. Mr. Forshaw was present when a very important statement was made by Mr. J. A. Ewart, contractor. Mr. Dodds was also present and duly committed to writing his recollections of the whole conversation. This document was read by Mr. Dodds to Mr. Forshaw in the presence of Mr. Forshaw and Mr. Norbury Williams. Mr. Forshaw stated that the document was an entirely correct report of Mr. Ewart's statement, and that he would certainly attend and give evidence in corroboration if called on to do so. The Commissioners four times invited his attendance. Four dates were consecutively fixed, June 16th end July 4th, 7th, and 14th. On each occasion he failed to appear, and the Commission- ers having no power to compel any person to attend, had regretfully to relinquish the attempt to secure his evidence. It must be remembered that Mr. Ewart also had previously refused to come and give evidence. In ordinary Fircumstances your three Commissioners would have given up the case as hopeless, but by an unexpected and surprising turn of events we have been able, from the evidence of un- expected persons, to verify all the salient points in Mr. Ewart's statements. What we have to say as to Coun- cillor Forshaw's part in this case is that he has (after admitting the correctness of the report of Mr. Ewart's statement, and promising to give evidence upon it) shown a deplorable lack of public spirit, and has, by his persistent failure to attend, done what he could to prevent your Commissioners from arriving at the truth in this very important branch of the case. in spite of his failure they have, however, been able to obtain sufficient evidence to satisfy them that the Ewart tatement was perfectly correct.

===== 0002601_19041022_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
"Ignorance " of Officials. •

The continuation of the report from this point under the above heading consists very largely of quotations in extenso from the evidence given by various officials before different sittings of the Commissioners. The names of the officials so interrogated and quoted are Messrs. W. S. Haddock, gas engineer, F. V. L. Matthias electrical engineer, James Dens, water engineer, Thomas Longdin, borough surveyor, T. Fletcher, clerk of works and J. Lyon Whittle, town clerk. Readers of the " Examiner " who desire further in- formation on this matter can refer to the printed

minutes of proceedings which are now to be obtained front the Corporation.

Payments to Mayors' Auditors.

We have ascertained that during the seven years' period Alderman Arthur Bennett held this office for five years and Mr. A. J. Jolley (ex-Councilor) for two years, and that both these gentlemen received for their services a sum of about .230 per year irom the- Corporation of which they were members. Here follows the evidence of Alderman Bennett and the Town Clerk and the Report then proceeds : A printed document was furnished to us by the Deputy Town Clerk containing the names of sixty-four Corporations from whom information had been obtained as to their procedure of this matter of paying Mayors' auditors, and we find that in ten of these cases they were paid. But we also find that no Court has yet declared' the practice legal, and we are strongly of opinion that it should cease in Warrington. No mem- ber of a Council should, on any pretext, make a prat out of his membership of the oouncil. The Municipal Corporations' Act has clearly enacted this, and no counsel's opinion can override the law. It is a thoroughly bad practice, and ought, in our opinion, never again to be permitted.

===== 0002601_19041022_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
Pearson & Knowles, Ltd

We consider it to be due to the Ex-Mayor (Mr. Charlton Parr), and to Mr. James Dodds, as well as to the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company, Ltd., that we should record our disapproval of the inclus ion, without an explanation of the name of that firm in the scheduled list of companies which traded with the Cor- poration during the seven years covered by the Inquiry as having supplied goods during that period, to the extent of £34.660 9s. 7d. We also disapprove of the starring, by our three first colleagues, of the names of Mr. Parr and Mr. Dodds, in connection with such trad- ing, without it having been at the same time stated— FIRST, IN REGARD TO Mn. PARR: (a) That he was never an elected member of the Town Council. ' (b.) That he was petitioneji to become Mayor largely as a result of the Non-trading Movement, and tor displace a well-known Trader. (c) That he was a member of the Council for only 10 months of the Inquiry period of seven years. 'cl) That of the Pearson and Knowles capital of £1,000,000 (one million) he held only £lOO. AND SECOND, IN REGARD TO Mn.DODDS : _ _ _ _ _ _

(a) That he was not a shareholder in Pearson and Knowles or in any other trading company, whilst a member of the Council. (b) That, though an official of Pearson and Knowles, ' he never "as a past or present member of the Council or official of the Corporation traded with the Corporation, either direct or indirect, through limited companies or officials of such companies, or as a contractor or sub-contractor," and that there were never any dealings or transactions of any kind between him as a member of the Town Council or official of the Corporation and the Corporation. (c) That he was a member of the Council during only five months of the Inquiry period of seven years. Clearly Mr. Dodds is entirely outside any connection whatever 'with any trading as defined by the resolutions governing the Inquiry, and his namn I.erefore been wrongly and, as we think, want only included in the starred list accompanying the report of Messrs. Birchall Greening and Jolley. Further, in view of the special circumstances under which Mr. Parr entered the Coun- cil, we consider that not only the trading of the Pearson and Knowles Company daring the short re- spective parts of the seven years that Mr. Parr and Mr. Dodds were members of the Council, but the whole of the trading of tht, seven years (in other words, every penny of the scheduled £34,9.60 9s. 7d.) represented fair and honourable business.

===== 0002601_19041022_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
Conclusion.

Our task. however imperfectly performed,is now com- pleted. The cost of the Inquiry,up to this day. is about £470. The people of Warrington will he called upon to pay this money. Some of them may consider it a large outlay. But we believe that the best of the bur- gesses, those who love the town most, and are most jealous for its good name, will consider this expendi- ture a right and wise one,. The inquiry will, we trust,. result in removing from Warrington the shadow which has rested upon it. For the evidence has proved that the trading which was alleged three years ago, was actually carried on; it has been demonstrated that the formation of limited companies, which at that time were springing up like mushrooms, has been checked; and the experience of those who have been sent to the Council in order to- stop the illicit trading is, that it has ceased to exist. These facts are ample compensation tons for the labour bestowed on this Inquiry, which has refuted the state- ments persistently made by a small section of the. burgesses that the allegations of the Non-Traders were "absolutely untrue and without foundation." The per; sistent " ignorance" of the officials, the boycotting the Inquiry by men who should have known better and. who ought to have rendered us assistance, and the lukewarmness of our Council colleagues, have all cora- bined to make this Inquiry a very difficult matter. We' therefore congratulate the burgesses that we have beell able to bring out as ninny facts as are to be found in the 6,600 paragraphs of the evidence, and we concludet with the expression of our earnest hope that for the future, the work of the borough may be carried on by its municipal representatives with a sir gle eye for the good of the town, and that self-seeking may not again show itself as it has done in the past, with such serious results to the good name of Warrington. We are, Gentlemen, Your faithful servants, ADAM HORROCKS. JOHN PEMBERTON. S. NORBURY WILLIAMS. • Warrington, 20th October. 1904.

The Unemployed.—Mr. Crosfield and Some Mis-statements,

Sir.—There are some notes in last Saturday's issue of one of your contemporaries where my name is mentioned in connection with certain passages (quoted from a Tory journal) as if I was responsible for them. Lest this should cause any misunderstanding,will you kindlY allow me to say in your columns that I read these passages with the deepest regret, and that I entirely disagree with them. Sad and weary faces there are, too many of them, just now, while slack trade swelJs. the ranks of the unemployed. But it is as well to remember that this lack of employment is largely due - to the reckless and senseless foreign policy of the last nine or ten years, a policy which, squandering the .resources of the nation, piling up debt, disorganising' finance and heavily increasing taxation, has seriously damaged trade. One can but deeply pity the unfortunate. men, women and children who are the sufferers,and take care we do not make matters worse by raising (a. 4 I unfortunately too many influential people in Warring-- ton are seeking to raise) the cost to them of the bare. necessaries of life.—Yours

' A. H. CROSFIELD. Warrington, October 21st, 1904.

An Offer to " Republican."

Sir,—l was disappointed on Saturday last when I gcr my "Examiner" to find that "Republican" had decided to retire from the list of anonymous writers after se, short a life. lam very sorry he has done so for I had hoped that we might have continued a most interesting and friendly controversy had the Editor allowed OP After reading" Republican's" first letter I came to the conclusion that at last a writer had come forward to denounce the Education Act who would act straight and deal with the Act as a controversialist should, but I must say that I was sorry to sce that he had become; so personal in Saturday's letter as to refer to myseh and other writers as being "lamentably ignorant." I won't at present reply to "Republican's " letter seeing that he does not intend to reply, as it would net be fair on my part, but T will make an offer to hisl (with your permission, Mr. Editor) to continue the- controversy, the conditions being :- 1. That we will not attack each other personally,but will keep to the subject of the controversy. 2. That our letters be published in the same issue of the "Examiner," the following week we reply t'Y each other's letters, and annex any questions which care to ask, and the answers to those questions wiw close the controversy. 3. That we will ignore any other letters that might' be written to the press touching on our controversy'. 4. That we try to conduct the controversyas an ideal one, so that at the end we shall come out good friends,' although our views may be different. If "Republican" would care to add any more condit- ions, I shall be pleased to consider them. Trusting, Mr. Editor,vou will see your way clear to- favour this controversy (If "Republican" is willing) believe rue to be faithfully yours,

" CHURCHINIAN."

MOTOR NOTES. A contemporary declares that, with the introduction of fast traffic upon country roads, it should now be made compulsory on the part of local authorities to remove I all trees and hedges where they obstruct a clear vie* of the cross-roads. This would indeed be a wise precau- tion, for, where both roads are main thoroughfares, a driver using either is apt to consider he has, if not a precedence, at least an equal right to cross without warning, and too frequently demonstrates this supposed right by .taking his vehicle across without perceptibly slackening his pace at all. --- A prudent person always makes a rule to slow down upon approaching cross roads, for the very reason that approaching cross traffic cannot always be discerned, owing to the presence of trees or tall hedges. at the angle. If this were invariably done by all users of the roads—motorists as well as horse-drivers—there would be fewer accidents and narrow escapes to be recorded from what is now fruitful cause. At the same time, the compulsory removal of any growth obstructing a dear view at such points would be an excellent step and we should much like to see it enforced. --- A very important consideration in the purchase of a new car is the matter of its petrol consumption. To in- dicate how much this may vary, we may mention that two cars of different and very well-known makes, but each sold as a 10 h.p. and both being fitted with a twin- cylinder motor, vary as much as ten to twelve miles on a gallon, the one running eighteen miles on this quan- tity, and the other averaging a steady twenty-eight to thirty on the same. These figures apply in each in- stance to the vehicles when carrying a full load. Such a difference, is, of course, a tremendous one, and would make a very striking contrast in the petrol bills for each car for a year, assuming that they were run over approximately: the same distances and the same kind of roads. A good driver will use far less spirit than one who has never troubled to study the engine's needs from an intelligent standpoint; but the best driving in the world could not get a car to run an additional ten, or even five miles on a gallon to the distance she will average under ordinarily fair driving conditions. -- Strong protest is being made against the growing practice of substituting a motor horn for a bell on a pedal-bicycle. Some writers assert that this is only I done to scare pedestrians who move much more quickly for the" raucous tootling" of the former than they will for the persuasive tinkling of the latter ; whilst others argue that, in congested traffic and noisy thoroughfares, such horns are really necessary, as the sound of a bell—at any rate, of a clear one—does not " carry " sufficiently to be of any material use. -- There is certainly considerable force in the latter argument, but, at the same time, one prefers to have some definite instrument reserved to indicate the approach of what is the fastest traffic on the road, and now that the great majority of motor cars are so much quieter than used to be the case, the "audible warning" by bell or otherwise, required by law is really necessary in order to give due notice of the approach of a motor car. Perhaps the solution of tLe difficulty will be found to lie with the "Tally-ho" musical horn patented by Mr. Moffat Ford and designed to be used solely upon motor cars Indeed this can hardly be adapted to a bicycle, for it would be a puzzle to decide upon the right spot for the accommodation of the air-container. Therefore, the best way will be to leave the " raucous" horn to the motor cyclist, and let the motor-carists delight themselves with Mr. Ford's tuneful "Tally-Ho !"—which is certainly a wonderZul improvement upon the gener- ally accepted "otherwise" demanded by law.
